3.1 Installing Terraform — macOS, Linux, Windows

Terraform is distributed as a single binary written in Go. No dependencies, no package manager required (though using one is recommended for version management).

🧠 Exam: terraform init Is ALWAYS First

The exam will ask: "You just cloned a Terraform repo. What's the first command you run?" Answer: terraform init. It downloads providers, initializes the backend, and creates .terraform.lock.hcl. Without init, no other command works. terraform plan or apply before init = ERROR.

macOS — Install via Homebrew
# Install Terraform
brew tap hashicorp/tap
brew install hashicorp/tap/terraform

# Verify installation
terraform version
# Terraform v1.7.0 on darwin_amd64
Linux (Ubuntu/Debian) — Manual Install
# Download and install
wget https://releases.hashicorp.com/terraform/1.7.0/terraform_1.7.0_linux_amd64.zip
unzip terraform_1.7.0_linux_amd64.zip
sudo mv terraform /usr/local/bin/

# Verify
terraform version
Windows — Chocolatey or Manual
# Using Chocolatey (recommended)
choco install terraform

# OR: Download from releases.hashicorp.com/terraform
# Extract zip, add terraform.exe to PATH

# Verify in PowerShell
terraform version
💡 Use tfenv for Version Management

Just like nvm for Node.js or pyenv for Python, tfenv lets you switch between Terraform versions per project. Critical when working across multiple projects with different version requirements.

Why this matters: Your production project might be pinned to Terraform 1.5.x, while a new project uses 1.7.x. Without tfenv, you'd need to manually download and swap binaries. With tfenv: tfenv install 1.7.0 && tfenv use 1.7.0 — done in seconds.

Install tfenv: git clone https://github.com/tfutils/tfenv.git ~/.tfenv, add to PATH, then tfenv install latest.

⚠️ Version Mismatch = Error

If your terraform {} block specifies required_version = ">= 1.5.0" and you have Terraform 1.3.0 installed, you'll get: "Error: Unsupported Terraform Core version." This is a SAFETY feature — it prevents you from using a version that might not understand your configuration syntax. Always match your installed version to your project's requirements.

3.2 Tab Completion & Shell Setup

Enable tab completion to speed up your Terraform workflow dramatically. Terraform supports bash and zsh autocompletion out of the box.

Shell Setup — Tab Completion
# Bash (~/.bashrc)
terraform -install-autocomplete

# Zsh (~/.zshrc)
terraform -install-autocomplete

# Now try: terraform <TAB>
# Shows: apply console destroy fmt get graph import init
#         login logout output plan providers refresh show
#         state taint test untaint validate version workspace

3.3 terraform --help — Your Best Friend

The Terraform CLI has excellent built-in help. Before searching Google, try terraform --help or terraform <command> --help.

📖 Help Hierarchy

terraform --help → lists ALL top-level commands

terraform plan --help → shows every flag and option for plan

terraform state --help → shows all state subcommands (list, mv, rm, show, etc.)

Exam tip: On the exam, if you forget a flag, remember the pattern: terraform <command> --help.

3.4 The Core Workflow — Write → Init → Plan → Apply → Destroy

This is THE most important pattern in Terraform. Every Terraform user runs this loop hundreds of times. Memorize it now — it's the foundation of everything that follows.

THE TERRAFORM CORE WORKFLOW — Repeat Forever
  ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐
  │  WRITE   │─────▶│   INIT   │─────▶│   PLAN   │
  │          │      │          │      │          │
  │ Create   │      │ Downloads│      │ Shows    │
  │ .tf      │      │ providers│      │ changes  │
  │ files    │      │ & modules│      │ (+/-/~)  │
  │          │      │          │      │          │
  └──────────┘      └──────────┘      └────┬─────┘
                                           │
                        ┌──────────────────┘
                        │  Review the plan
                        │  "Do these changes look right?"
                        ▼
                  ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐
                  │  APPLY   │─────▶│ VERIFY   │
                  │          │      │          │
                  │ Execute  │      │ Check    │
                  │ changes  │      │ resources│
                  │ (API     │      │ in cloud │
                  │  calls)  │      │ console  │
                  └──────────┘      └──────────┘

  🔁 Then: Edit .tf files → plan again → apply again → repeat

Let's walk through what happens at each step in detail:

📝 1. WRITE — Define Desired State

You create .tf files describing what infrastructure you want: VPCs, EC2 instances, databases, load balancers. These are declarative — you say "I want a VPC with CIDR 10.0.0.0/16" — NOT "Step 1: click Create VPC, Step 2: enter 10.0.0.0/16..."

Key files: main.tf (resources), variables.tf (inputs), outputs.tf (exposed values), versions.tf (provider pins).

⬇️ 2. INIT — Download Dependencies

terraform init reads your configuration and downloads everything needed: provider binaries (Go programs that translate HCL to API calls), referenced modules, and backend configuration. Creates .terraform/ directory and .terraform.lock.hcl lock file.

When to re-run: After adding a new provider, changing provider version, or adding a new module reference. NOT needed for regular resource changes.

🔍 3. PLAN — Preview Changes

terraform plan compares your .tf files against the state file AND the real infrastructure. It outputs exactly what will be created (+), updated (~), replaced (-/+), or destroyed (-). No changes are made during plan. It's a read-only preview.

Critical habit: ALWAYS review plan output before applying. The plan is your safety net — it catches mistakes before they become production incidents.

🚀 4. APPLY — Execute Changes

terraform apply executes the plan by making API calls to your cloud provider. It shows the plan one more time and asks for confirmation (unless -auto-approve is used — dangerous in production!). After apply, the state file is updated with new resource IDs.

After apply: Verify resources in the cloud console. Run terraform output to see exposed values like IPs and endpoints.

3.5 Your First Terraform File — main.tf for anihpj

Let's write a real Terraform configuration file for the anihpj project. This example uses the Docker provider — it's free, runs locally, and perfectly demonstrates Terraform concepts without needing a cloud account.

main.tf — Your First Terraform File (anihpj Local Dev)
terraform {
  required_providers {
    docker = {
      source  = "kreuzwerker/docker"
      version = "~> 3.0"
    }
  }
}

provider "docker" {}

# Pull the PostgreSQL Docker image
resource "docker_image" "postgres" {
  name         = "postgres:16-alpine"
  keep_locally = true
}

# Pull the Redis Docker image
resource "docker_image" "redis" {
  name         = "redis:7-alpine"
  keep_locally = true
}

# Create a Docker network for anihpj
resource "docker_network" "anihpj_net" {
  name = "anihpj-network"
}

# Start PostgreSQL container
resource "docker_container" "postgres" {
  image = docker_image.postgres.image_id
  name  = "anihpj-db"
  networks_advanced { name = docker_network.anihpj_net.name }
  env = [
    "POSTGRES_DB=anihpj",
    "POSTGRES_USER=anihpj_admin",
    "POSTGRES_PASSWORD=secret123"
  ]
  ports { internal = 5432; external = 5432 }
}

# Start Redis container
resource "docker_container" "redis" {
  image = docker_image.redis.image_id
  name  = "anihpj-redis"
  networks_advanced { name = docker_network.anihpj_net.name }
  ports { internal = 6379; external = 6379 }
}
💡 Run It Yourself!

Prerequisites: Docker Desktop installed and running.

Steps: (1) Save the code above as main.tf, (2) Run terraform init, (3) Run terraform plan, (4) Run terraform apply. You'll have PostgreSQL and Redis running in seconds — all managed by Terraform!

🧩 What Each Block Does — Line by Line Walkthrough

terraform {} block: Tells Terraform "I need the Docker provider, version 3.x." This is the setup — it declares what plugins are needed before any resources can be created.

provider "docker" {} block: Configures the Docker provider. Since we're running Docker locally, no authentication is needed — Terraform connects to the local Docker daemon via the Unix socket.

docker_image resources: Pulls the PostgreSQL and Redis Docker images from Docker Hub. The keep_locally = true argument prevents Terraform from deleting the images when you run terraform destroy.

docker_network resource: Creates an isolated Docker network called "anihpj-network." All containers attached to this network can communicate with each other using their container names as DNS hostnames.

docker_container resources: Starts the actual containers. Each one references the image it needs, connects to the network, sets environment variables, and maps ports. The depends_on isn't needed — Terraform figures out the dependency from the references (e.g., docker_image.postgres.image_id creates an implicit dependency).

3.6 Anatomy of a Terraform Block — Visual Breakdown

ANATOMY OF A TERRAFORM BLOCK — Every Label Explained
  resource "aws_instance" "anihpj_web" {
  ─────── ────────────── ───────────
     ▲          ▲              ▲
     │          │              └── RESOURCE NAME (your label)
     │          │                  • Must be unique within this type
     │          │                  • Used in references:
     │          │                    aws_instance.anihpj_web.id
     │          │
     │          └── RESOURCE TYPE (<provider>_<service>)
     │              • aws_instance, aws_vpc, aws_s3_bucket
     │              • google_compute_instance
     │              • azurerm_virtual_network
     │
     └── BLOCK TYPE KEYWORD
         • resource = create/manage infrastructure
         • data     = read-only query
         • variable = declare input parameter
         • output   = expose value after apply
         • provider = configure cloud API access
         • module   = call reusable code
         • locals   = named expressions (DRY)

     ami           = "ami-0c55b159cbfafe1f0"   ← STRING argument
     instance_type = "t3.micro"                 ← STRING argument
     tags = {                                   ← MAP argument
       Name = "anihpj-web-${var.env}"           ← INTERPOLATION
     }
  }

3.7 Directory Structure — Best Practices for Organizing .tf Files

Terraform loads ALL .tf files in a directory and merges them into one configuration. This means file names don't matter to Terraform — they matter to HUMANS. A consistent naming convention makes your codebase maintainable.

📖 Why Split Into Multiple Files?

You COULD put everything in one main.tf. But when that file hits 500+ lines, finding anything becomes painful. Splitting by purpose — not by resource type — makes code navigable. Every experienced Terraform developer can open a new project and immediately know where to look: variables in variables.tf, outputs in outputs.tf, main resources in main.tf.

File / DirectoryPurposeCommit to Git?Why?
main.tfMain configuration entry point✅ YesYour infrastructure code
variables.tfInput variable declarations✅ YesDefines the API of your config
outputs.tfOutput value declarations✅ YesDefines what you expose
terraform.tfvarsVariable values (no secrets!)⚙️ MaybeOK if no secrets; use .gitignore if unsure
versions.tfProvider & Terraform version pins✅ YesPrevents version drift across team
.terraform.lock.hclDependency lock file✅ YES!HashiCorp: "You should include this file in your VCS"
.terraform/Downloaded providers & modules❌ .gitignoreLarge binaries; regenerated by init
terraform.tfstateState file (secrets inside!)❌ NEVER!Contains all resource attributes in plain text
*.auto.tfvarsAuto-loaded variable files⚙️ MaybeConvenient but can hide values from team

3.8 terraform fmt — Automatic Code Formatting

terraform fmt automatically formats your HCL code to the canonical style. It's like gofmt for Go or prettier for JavaScript — no more arguing about indentation in code reviews.

terraform fmt in Action
# Check what would be reformatted
$ terraform fmt -check

# Format all .tf files in directory recursively  
$ terraform fmt -recursive

# Format with diff output (show what changed)
$ terraform fmt -diff
⚠️ Exam Gotcha

terraform fmt only formats code style (indentation, spacing). It does NOT validate HCL syntax. For syntax validation, use terraform validate. The exam WILL try to trick you on this distinction.

3.9 terraform validate — Catch Errors Before Plan

terraform validate checks your HCL syntax and resource references WITHOUT making any API calls. Run it before every terraform plan.

Using terraform validate
$ terraform validate
Success! The configuration is valid.

# Example of a validation error:
$ terraform validate
Error: Reference to undeclared resource
  on main.tf line 15:
  resource "aws_instance" uses data.aws_vpc.nonexistent

3.10 .terraform Directory — What Gets Downloaded at Init

WHAT terraform init DOWNLOADS
  $ terraform init
  │
  ▼
  .terraform/
  ├── providers/
  │   └── registry.terraform.io/
  │       └── hashicorp/
  │           └── aws/
  │               └── 5.0.0/
  │                   └── linux_amd64/
  │                       └── terraform-provider-aws_v5.0.0  ◀── Go binary!
  ├── modules/
  │   └── modules.json          ◀── Module manifest (versions, sources)
  └── terraform.tfstate         ◀── Local state (or lock file)

  KEY INSIGHT: Providers are Go binaries (~100-500MB each).
  terraform init downloads the CORRECT version for your OS/arch.

Chapter 4: HCL Deep Dive — The Language of Infrastructure 🟢 Beginner

Chapter 4: HCL Deep Dive — Mastering the HashiCorp Configuration Language

HCL is Terraform's native language. You'll spend 90% of your Terraform time writing HCL. This chapter dives deep into every block type, syntax rule, and language feature you need for the exam and real-world use.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📝 HCL 🎯 Core

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ All 8 Terraform block types and when to use each one
  • ✅ Resource blocks deep dive — every part of the syntax explained
  • ✅ Data sources — querying existing infrastructure without creating it
  • ✅ Locals — DRY up your code with named expressions
  • ✅ Terraform settings block — version constraints & backend config
  • ✅ Provider configuration patterns — auth, regions, aliases

4.1 The 8 Terraform Block Types — Complete Reference

Every .tf file is composed of blocks. A block is a container for configuration — it has a type (what kind of thing), labels (identifying names), and a body (arguments inside { }). Understanding all 8 block types is essential — the exam will test your knowledge of which block type to use for what purpose.

Block TypePurposeRequired?When to Use
terraform {}Global settings (version, backend, required_providers)RecommendedALWAYS — pin versions to prevent drift. Configure remote state backend.
provider {}Configure cloud API access (region, auth)✅ RequiredAt least once per cloud provider. Use aliases for multi-region.
resource {}Declare WHAT to create/manage✅ RequiredEvery infrastructure component (VPC, EC2, RDS, S3...).
data {}READ existing infra (don't create)OptionalWhen you need to reference something already existing.
variable {}Declare input parametersBest PracticeAnything that changes between environments (size, count, region).
output {}Expose values after applyBest PracticeValues other teams/systems need (IPs, endpoints, ARNs).
locals {}Named expressions (DRY)Best PracticeValues repeated 3+ times — name prefixes, common tags.
module {}Call reusable codeAt ScaleWhen you need the same pattern in multiple places.
💡 How to Think About Block Types

terraform + provider = the "setup" blocks — configure the tool and its connections. resource + data = the "infrastructure" blocks — what actually exists in the cloud. variable + output + locals = the "interface" blocks — inputs, outputs, and internal helpers. module = the "composition" block — combining everything into reusable packages. This mental model helps you quickly know which block to use in any situation.

4.2 Resource Blocks — The Heart of Terraform

The resource block is Terraform's most important construct. Every infrastructure component you manage goes through a resource block.

Resource Block — Every Part Explained
resource "aws_instance" "anihpj_web" {
  #       ────────────── ───────────
  #            ▲              ▲
  #            │              └── RESOURCE NAME (your label, unique per type)
  #            │                  Referenced as: aws_instance.anihpj_web.id
  #            │
  #            └── RESOURCE TYPE: <provider>_<service>
  #                aws_instance, aws_vpc, azurerm_virtual_network

  ami           = "ami-0c55b159cbfafe1f0"   # ← string
  instance_type = "t3.micro"                 # ← string  
  count         = 3                          # ← number (meta-argument)
  
  tags = {                                   # ← map
    Name        = "anihpj-${var.env}"
    Environment = var.environment
    ManagedBy   = "Terraform"
  }
}

# Referencing resources in other blocks:
# aws_instance.anihpj_web.id          → resource attribute
# aws_instance.anihpj_web[0].public_ip → indexed (when count > 1)
# module.vpc.aws_vpc.main.id           → resource inside a module

4.3 Data Sources — Querying Existing Infrastructure

Data sources let you READ information about existing resources — whether Terraform-managed or not. They make API calls during terraform plan.

DATA SOURCES — 4 Key Use Cases (From the .txt)
  DATA SOURCE = Read-Only Query at Plan Time (no API write!)

  USE CASE 1: LOOK UP EXISTING RESOURCES (not managed by Terraform):
  data "aws_ami" "amazon_linux" {
    most_recent = true
    owners      = ["amazon"]
    filter { name="name"; values=["amzn2-ami-hvm-*-x86_64"] }
  }
  → Use data.aws_ami.amazon_linux.id in EC2 resource

  USE CASE 2: CROSS-STACK REFERENCES:
  data "aws_vpc" "shared" {
    filter { name="tag:Name"; values=["shared-vpc"] }
  }
  → Get VPC ID without importing or hardcoding

  USE CASE 3: DYNAMIC CONFIGURATION:
  data "aws_availability_zones" "available" {
    state = "available"
  }
  → Subnet across all AZs: count = length(data.aws_...names)

  USE CASE 4: SECRETS & PARAMETERS:
  data "aws_secretsmanager_secret_version" "db_pass" {
    secret_id = "anihpj/db/password"
  }
  → NEVER hardcode passwords; fetch at plan time

  💡 Data sources run during terraform plan & refresh.
  💡 Failed data source = plan FAILS (can't resolve).
  💡 KEY DIFFERENCE: resource = CREATE/MANAGE; data = READ-ONLY QUERY
Data Source Examples
# Look up the latest Amazon Linux AMI
data "aws_ami" "amazon_linux" {
  most_recent = true
  owners      = ["amazon"]
  filter { name = "name"; values = ["amzn2-ami-hvm-*-x86_64"] }
}

# Query available AZs in the region
data "aws_availability_zones" "available" {
  state = "available"
}

# Use them in a resource
resource "aws_instance" "web" {
  ami           = data.aws_ami.amazon_linux.id      # ← Uses data source
  instance_type = "t3.micro"
  subnet_id     = data.aws_subnet.private[0].id      # ← Cross-stack ref
}

4.6 Locals — DRY Up Your Code

Locals are named expressions that prevent repetition. Define once, use everywhere.

LOCALS — WET (Write Everything Twice) vs DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself)
  PROBLEM: You repeat the same expression 10 times.
           When it changes, you need to update 10 places.

  ❌ WITHOUT locals (WET):
  resource "aws_instance" "web" {
    tags = { Name = "anihpj-${var.env}-${var.region}-web" }
  }
  resource "aws_db_instance" "db" {
    tags = { Name = "anihpj-${var.env}-${var.region}-db" }
  }
  resource "aws_s3_bucket" "assets" {
    tags = { Name = "anihpj-${var.env}-${var.region}-assets" }
  }
  ... (7 more resources with same pattern)
  ❌ Change prefix? Update 10 places

  ✅ WITH locals (DRY):
  locals {
    name_prefix = "anihpj-${var.env}-${var.region}"
    common_tags = {
      Environment = var.env
      ManagedBy   = "Terraform"
      Project     = "anihpj"
    }
  }
  resource "aws_instance" "web" {
    tags = merge(local.common_tags, {
      Name = "${local.name_prefix}-web"
    })
  }
  ✅ Change prefix? ONE place (locals block)
  ✅ Reference: local.name_prefix (NO quotes around "local"!)
Locals — DRY Principle
locals {
  name_prefix = "anihpj-${var.env}-${var.region}"
  common_tags = {
    Environment = var.env
    ManagedBy   = "Terraform"
    Project     = "anihpj"
    Team        = "platform-engineering"
  }
}

resource "aws_instance" "web" {
  tags = merge(local.common_tags, {
    Name = "${local.name_prefix}-web"  # ← Reuses prefix
  })
}

# ✅ Change prefix? Update ONE line in locals block
# ✅ Add a tag to ALL resources? Add to common_tags ONCE

4.7 Terraform Settings Block — Version Pinning

terraform {} Block — Complete Reference
terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.5.0, < 2.0.0"    # ← Terraform CLI version
  
  required_providers {
    aws = {
      source  = "hashicorp/aws"
      version = "~> 5.0"                    # ← Provider version (>= 5.0, < 6.0)
    }
    random = {
      source  = "hashicorp/random"
      version = "~> 3.5"
    }
  }
  
  backend "s3" {                             # ← Remote state backend
    bucket         = "anihpj-tfstate"
    key            = "prod/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "us-east-1"
    dynamodb_table = "terraform-locks"
    encrypt        = true
  }
}
⚠️ CRITICAL: required_version ≠ required_providers.version

required_version constrains the Terraform CLI version. required_providers block's version constrains the provider version. These are DIFFERENT things — the exam WILL test this distinction. ~> 5.0 means ">= 5.0, < 6.0" (pessimistic constraint).

4.9 Comments & Documentation in HCL

StyleSyntaxUse Case
Single-line# comment or // commentQuick notes, both valid
Multi-line/* comment */Large block comments

4.4 Variables — Making Configs Reusable

Variables let you parameterize your Terraform configurations. Instead of hardcoding values, you declare variables and assign them later — making the same code work for dev, staging, and prod.

Variable Declaration & Usage
# variables.tf — Declare what can vary
variable "environment" {
  type    = string
  default = "dev"
}

variable "instance_type" {
  type    = string
  default = "t3.micro"
}

# main.tf — Use the variable
resource "aws_instance" "web" {
  instance_type = var.instance_type         # ← var. prefix
  tags = { Environment = var.environment }
}

4.5 Outputs — Exposing Values After Apply

Outputs expose information about your infrastructure after terraform apply — like instance IPs, database endpoints, and load balancer DNS names.

Output Examples
output "instance_public_ip" {
  value       = aws_instance.web.public_ip
  description = "The public IP of the anihpj web server"
}

output "db_endpoint" {
  value       = aws_db_instance.postgres.address
  description = "RDS PostgreSQL endpoint"
  sensitive   = false
}

4.8 Provider Configuration — Authentication & Region Setup

Providers use the credentials from your environment — AWS CLI config, Azure CLI login, or GCP service account. Never hardcode credentials in .tf files.

Provider Configuration Patterns
# AWS — uses ~/.aws/credentials or env vars
provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-1"
}

# Multi-region with aliases
provider "aws" {
  alias  = "us-west"
  region = "us-west-2"
}

# Use aliased provider in a resource
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "dr" {
  provider = aws.us-west      # ← Use the aliased provider
  bucket   = "anihpj-dr-backup"
}

4.10 File Extension Conventions — .tf, .tfvars, .tfstate

ExtensionPurposeAuto-loaded?
.tfHCL configuration files✅ Yes (all in directory)
.tf.jsonJSON format configuration✅ Yes
.tfvarsVariable values fileOnly terraform.tfvars
.auto.tfvarsAuto-loaded variables✅ Yes (automatic)
.tfstateState file (JSON)N/A — NEVER commit

📝 Chapter 4 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
What does terraform plan do and is it read-only?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes, terraform plan is READ-ONLY. It compares desired state (.tf) against actual state (real infra) and shows a diff. It makes ZERO changes to infrastructure.

Explanation

This is the #1 safety feature of Terraform. You can run terraform plan 1000 times and nothing changes. It's also your drift detector — if someone manually changed a resource, plan will show it. Always review the plan output before applying to production.

Q2
How do you save a plan output and apply it later?
Show Answer
Answer

terraform plan -out=tfplan saves a binary plan file. terraform apply tfplan applies that exact plan — no changes, no prompts.

# CI/CD pipeline pattern:
terraform plan -out=anihpj.tfplan
# ... review plan ...
terraform apply anihpj.tfplan   # Applies EXACTLY what was planned
Explanation

Saved plans guarantee that what you reviewed is what gets applied. Without a saved plan, terraform apply generates a fresh plan — which might differ if someone else changed infrastructure between your review and apply. The exam tests this: saved plans = guaranteed consistency.

Q3
What does terraform apply -auto-approve do?
Show Answer
Answer

It skips the interactive approval prompt. Terraform applies changes immediately without asking "Do you want to perform these actions?"

Explanation

Use -auto-approve in CI/CD pipelines where human interaction isn't possible. For production, avoid it — always review the plan first. The exam may ask when -auto-approve is appropriate: answer is "automated pipelines where a plan has already been reviewed."

Q4
What's the difference between terraform apply and terraform destroy?
Show Answer
Answer

apply creates/updates resources to match desired state. destroy deletes ALL resources managed by the current configuration. Destroy is effectively "apply the inverse" — it removes everything.

Explanation
# terraform apply  = CREATE/UPDATE → convergence to desired state
# terraform destroy = DELETE → inverse of apply

# Selective destroy (safe for debugging):
terraform destroy -target=aws_instance.dev   # Only destroys dev instance
terraform destroy -target=module.staging      # Only destroys staging module

terraform destroy is the mirror of terraform apply. Apply reads .tf files and creates/updates infrastructure to match. Destroy reads the state file and deletes everything listed.

Q5
What does terraform refresh do?
Show Answer
Answer

It syncs the state file with real infrastructure — updating state to match what actually exists. It makes NO changes to infrastructure, only to state. Automatically happens during terraform plan.

Explanation

terraform refresh reads every resource from the cloud provider and updates the state file to reflect reality. It's automatically part of terraform plan — you rarely need to run it manually. Use case: someone made manual changes in the AWS console and you want state to reflect that before planning.

Q6
What is the -target flag and when should you use it?
Show Answer
Answer

-target=RESOURCE limits plan/apply to a specific resource and its dependencies. Use only for debugging/troubleshooting — not for routine operations.

Explanation
# CORRECT: Debug a single failing resource
terraform apply -target=aws_db_instance.anihpj

# WRONG: Routine production deployment with -target
terraform apply -target=module.app  # ❌ Bypasses dependency graph!

Targeting bypasses Terraform's dependency graph. The exam will ask: "When should you use -target?" Answer: "Only for debugging or emergency fixes, never as a standard practice."

Q7
What does terraform console do?
Show Answer
Answer

It opens an interactive REPL that evaluates Terraform expressions against the current state. Use it to test expressions, inspect resource attributes, and experiment with functions.

Explanation

terraform console is like a calculator for Terraform. Type aws_instance.web.public_ip and it returns the actual value from state. Test functions: cidrsubnet("10.0.0.0/16", 8, 1) returns "10.0.1.0/24". It reads from your current state, so you must have run terraform init first.

Q8
What is the -parallelism flag used for?
Show Answer
Answer

It controls how many resources Terraform creates/updates simultaneously. Default is 10. Increase for large deployments with independent resources; decrease for API-rate-limited providers.

Explanation

Terraform parallelizes resource creation. With 50 EC2 instances and -parallelism=20, Terraform creates 20 at a time. Default of 10 is fine for most cases. AWS API rate limits may require lowering. Increase when resources are truly independent (no shared dependencies).

Q9
What happens if you interrupt terraform apply mid-execution?
Show Answer
Answer

Partial infrastructure may be created. Terraform updates state for completed resources. Running terraform apply again will complete the remaining changes from where it left off.

Explanation

Terraform is resilient to interruptions. Each resource creation is tracked independently — if 5 of 10 resources succeed before Ctrl+C, those 5 are in state. Re-running picks up the remaining 5. No double-creation, no orphaned resources. This is why state tracking matters.

Q10
How do you pass variables via CLI during plan/apply?
Show Answer
Answer

terraform plan -var="env=prod" -var="count=5" or terraform apply -var-file="prod.tfvars". CLI -var flags have the HIGHEST precedence — they override .tfvars files.

Explanation
# Precedence (LOWEST → HIGHEST):
# 1. variable "name" { default = "val" }    ← Lowest
# 2. export TF_VAR_name="val"
# 3. terraform.tfvars                         ← Auto-loaded
# 4. *.auto.tfvars                             ← Auto-loaded (alphabetical)
# 5. terraform plan -var="name=val"           ← HIGHEST, always wins
# 5. terraform plan -var-file=prod.tfvars     ← Same level as -var

CLI flags ALWAYS override everything. Multiple -var-file flags: the LAST one wins for overlapping keys. This is the #1 tested variable concept on the exam.

Chapter 5: The Terraform State — Source of Truth 🟢 Beginner

Chapter 5: The Terraform State — The Brain of Your Infrastructure

The state file is the most important concept in Terraform. It's what makes Terraform different from every other IaC tool. Without state, Terraform is just a fancy script runner. With state, it becomes an intelligent infrastructure manager that remembers everything it's done. This chapter demystifies state entirely.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 🗄️ State 🎯 Critical
🎓 TF Associate Relevance
Domain 2: State Mgmt ~10% 🔴 HIGH priority

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What state is — the JSON file that maps your HCL code to real cloud resources
  • ✅ How state enables idempotency, dependency tracking, and drift detection
  • ✅ State locking — why DynamoDB prevents infrastructure corruption
  • ✅ Remote state backends — S3, GCS, Azure Blob, Terraform Cloud
  • ✅ How to import existing resources into Terraform management
  • ✅ State security — secrets in state files and how to protect them

5.1 What Is State? — The JSON Map of Your Infrastructure

The state file (terraform.tfstate) is a JSON document that maps your HCL resource definitions to real-world resource IDs. It's Terraform's "memory" — without it, Terraform doesn't know what it created.

HOW STATE WORKS — Terraform vs Ansible
  WITHOUT State (Ansible):
  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐
  │ Ansible  │────▶│ AWS API  │────▶│ "Create  │  Every run = new API call
  │ Playbook │     │  Call    │     │  EC2"    │  No memory of what exists
  └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘

  WITH State (Terraform):
  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────┐
  │ .tf      │────▶│ STATE FILE   │────▶│ AWS API  │
  │ Config   │     │ (Memory)     │     │  Call    │
  └──────────┘     └──────┬───────┘     └──────────┘
                          │
             ┌────────────┼────────────┐
             │            │            │
             ▼            ▼            ▼
       "I have EC2    "New EC2     "Old EC2
        i-abc123"     needed?      i-xyz789
        ✅            Create it"   not in config?
        (no action)   ✅            ❌ Destroy it!"
🏠 Real-World Analogy: The Home Inventory

Imagine you own 50 rental properties. You have a spreadsheet listing every property's address, number of bedrooms, and tenant name. When you want to know what you own, you check the spreadsheet. When you buy a new property, you add it. When you sell one, you remove it.

The spreadsheet IS the state file. HCL (.tf files) is your "shopping list" — what you WANT to own. The state file is what you ACTUALLY own. Terraform compares the two and makes API calls to reconcile them. Without the spreadsheet, you'd have to drive to every property to check what you own — that's what Ansible does (stateless, checks every time).

State = Terraform's Superpower

Three things state enables that no other IaC tool matches: (1) Idempotency — "I already created this, don't create it again." (2) Dependency resolution — "Subnet needs VPC vpc-xyz — wait, I already have that!" (3) Drift detection — "Someone changed the security group in the console — I'll show that in the plan." Without state, Terraform is just a fancy bash script.

5.2 terraform.tfstate — Internal Structure Deep Dive

terraform.tfstate — Simplified JSON Structure
{
  "version": 4,                    ← State format version
  "terraform_version": "1.5.0",    ← Terraform that wrote it
  "serial": 42,                    ← Monotonically increasing
  "lineage": "abc-123-def",        ← Unique state ID
  "outputs": {                     ← Output values
    "vpc_id": {"value": "vpc-abc123"}
  },
  "resources": [                   ← THE IMPORTANT PART
    {
      "mode": "managed",           ← managed vs data
      "type": "aws_instance",      ← Resource type
      "name": "anihpj_web",        ← Your resource name
      "provider": "provider[\"registry.../aws\"]",
      "instances": [{
        "attributes": {
          "id": "i-0abcd1234",     ← Real AWS resource ID
          "private_ip": "10.0.1.5", ← ⚠️ SENSITIVE!
          "arn": "arn:aws:ec2:...",
          ...
        }
      }]
    }
  ]
}
🔴 CRITICAL: State Contains Secrets

State files contain ALL resource attributes — including database passwords, private IPs, and API keys. NEVER commit state files to Git. Always use remote state backends with encryption (S3 SSE-KMS, Azure Storage encryption). Use .gitignore to exclude *.tfstate and *.tfstate.*.

5.3 State Locking — Preventing Concurrent Modifications

STATE LOCKING — Preventing Disaster with DynamoDB
  ❌ WITHOUT LOCKING (Disaster):        ✅ WITH LOCKING (DynamoDB):
  ┌────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Alice: terraform apply │          │ Alice: terraform apply     │
  │ Bob:   terraform apply │          │  │ Gets LOCK from DynamoDB │
  │                        │          │  ▼ (LockID: "alice-123")   │
  │ Both read state file   │          │ Alice reads & applies ✅    │
  │ Both apply changes     │          │  │ Releases LOCK            │
  │ RESULT:                │          │  ▼                         │
  │ • Duplicate resources  │          │ Bob: terraform apply       │
  │ • Corrupted state      │          │  │ Tries to get LOCK       │
  │ • $$$ wasted           │          │  ▼                         │
  └────────────────────────┘          │ ⛔ "Error: state locked    │
                                       │  by alice-123. Wait..."   │
                                       └────────────────────────────┘
S3 Backend with DynamoDB Locking — Production Setup
backend "s3" {
  bucket         = "anihpj-terraform-state"   ← S3 stores state file
  key            = "prod/terraform.tfstate"   ← Path within bucket
  region         = "us-east-1"
  dynamodb_table = "terraform-locks"         ← DynamoDB for locking
  encrypt        = true                      ← Server-side encryption
}
⚠️ Exam Critical: S3 alone does NOT provide locking

S3 stores the state. DynamoDB provides the LOCK. You MUST configure both for production use. The exam WILL ask: "Which AWS service provides state locking?" The answer is DynamoDB — NOT S3.

🔧 Troubleshooting: Stuck Locks — terraform force-unlock

If a terraform apply crashes or is killed (Ctrl+C during apply), the lock may not be released. The next person sees: "Error: Error acquiring the state lock" with a Lock ID.

Fix: First, verify the lock holder is truly dead (check if they're still running). Then: terraform force-unlock LOCK_ID. ⚠️ Only use force-unlock if you're CERTAIN no one else is applying. Forcing a lock while someone else is mid-apply will corrupt your infrastructure.

Best practice: Set a short Duration on the DynamoDB lock (e.g., 10 minutes instead of the default infinite). If the lock expires naturally, no force-unlock is needed.

5.4 Remote State — Sharing State Across Teams

BackendLockingEncryptionVersioningBest For
Local❌ None❌ None❌ NoneSolo dev only
S3 + DynamoDB✅ Table✅ SSE-S3✅ YesAWS shops
Terraform Cloud✅ Built-in✅ At-rest✅ Built-inTeams, CI/CD
GCS❌ Extra✅ Default✅ YesGCP shops
Azure Blob✅ Lease✅ Default✅ YesAzure shops

5.5 State Management Commands — Quick Reference with Usage Scenarios

CommandWhat It DoesWhen to Use
terraform state listList all resources in state"What resources does this project actually manage?"
terraform state show <addr>Show details of one resource"What's the actual private IP of that EC2 instance?"
terraform state mv <src> <dst>Rename/move resource in stateYou refactored: renamed aws_instance.old → aws_instance.new
terraform state rm <addr>Remove from state (⚠️ NOT destroy)Splitting state files; resource stays in cloud but leaves Terraform management
terraform state pullDownload & print remote state"Show me the raw JSON state file without SSH-ing to the backend"
terraform state pushUpload local state to remoteRare — mainly for disaster recovery after state corruption
terraform refreshSync state with real infrastructure"Someone changed a security group in the console — update state to match"
terraform import <addr> <id>Import existing resource into stateAdopting manually-created resources into Terraform management
terraform taint <addr>Mark resource for recreation"This EC2 instance is acting weird — destroy and recreate it on next apply"
terraform untaint <addr>Remove taint mark"Actually, the EC2 is fine — cancel the forced recreation"
⚠️ state rm vs terraform destroy — Critical Difference

terraform state rm removes a resource from the STATE FILE only. The real resource (EC2, RDS, S3 bucket) continues to exist and run — but Terraform no longer knows about it. This is useful for splitting state files, but dangerous if done accidentally.

terraform destroy (or removing the resource block and running terraform apply) actually DELETES the real cloud resource. If you want to stop managing something without deleting it, use state rm. If you want to tear it down, use destroy.

5.6 terraform import — Bringing Existing Resources Under Management

The most common scenario for terraform import: Your team has been running infrastructure manually for months. Now you're adopting Terraform. You don't want to destroy and recreate everything — you want to adopt the existing resources into Terraform management. That's what import does.

TERRAFORM IMPORT — Complete Step-by-Step Workflow
  SCENARIO: anihpj has an RDS database created manually 6 months ago.
           How do we bring it under Terraform management?

  ═══ STEP 1: Write the config ═══
  Create a resource block in main.tf that MATCHES the existing resource:
  resource "aws_db_instance" "anihpj_db" {
    identifier     = "anihpj-postgres"    ← Must match the real DB
    engine         = "postgres"
    instance_class = "db.t3.micro"
    allocated_storage = 20
    username       = "anihpj_admin"
    password       = var.db_password      ← Will be set after import
  }

  ═══ STEP 2: Import ═══
  $ terraform import aws_db_instance.anihpj_db anihpj-postgres
                          ▲                        ▲
                     resource address        Real AWS resource ID

  Output:
  aws_db_instance.anihpj_db: Importing from ID "anihpj-postgres"...
  aws_db_instance.anihpj_db: Import complete!

  ═══ STEP 3: Verify ═══
  $ terraform plan
  # Shows any DIFFERENCES between your .tf and the real resource
  # Fix your .tf until plan shows "No changes"

  ═══ STEP 4: Fill in missing attributes ═══
  $ terraform state show aws_db_instance.anihpj_db
  # Shows ALL attributes of the imported resource — copy any
  # missing ones into your .tf file until plan is clean

  ✅ DONE! The database is now fully managed by Terraform.
⚠️ import adds to STATE only — NOT to your .tf files

terraform import updates the state file but does NOT generate HCL code. You must manually write the matching resource block BEFORE importing. After import, run terraform plan to verify the config matches the real resource. If you see unexpected changes in the plan, your .tf code doesn't match reality — adjust it until the plan is clean.

🔧 import Pitfalls to Avoid
  • ❌ Importing without writing .tf first: The import will succeed but terraform plan will want to DESTROY the resource (because there's no resource block matching it). Always write the resource block first.
  • ❌ Partial config: If your .tf block is missing required attributes, terraform plan will show changes. Use terraform state show to see all attributes and add the missing ones.
  • ❌ Importing resources that reference each other: Import resources in dependency order — VPC first, then subnets, then EC2. Or use terraform plan -target to import one at a time.

5.7 State Refresh — Syncing with Reality

terraform refresh updates the state file to match the real-world infrastructure. It reads every resource's current attributes from the cloud provider and writes them to state — without making any changes.

terraform refresh
# Refresh state to match reality
$ terraform refresh
# Reads current state of ALL resources from cloud APIs
# Updates terraform.tfstate with any changes found
# Does NOT modify infrastructure — read-only operation

# Note: terraform plan also does a refresh automatically!

5.8 State Backup & Recovery

Every time state is modified, Terraform creates a backup file: terraform.tfstate.backup. This is your safety net for state corruption.

⚠️ State Recovery Steps

1. Stop all Terraform operations immediately

2. Copy terraform.tfstate.backup → terraform.tfstate

3. Run terraform plan to verify consistency

4. With remote state (S3): enable versioning on the bucket to restore any previous version

5.10 Partial Backend Configuration — Init-Time Config

You can pass backend configuration at terraform init time instead of hardcoding it. This is essential for CI/CD where backend config varies per environment.

Partial Backend Configuration
# In backend.tf — omit the values
backend "s3" {}   # ← Empty! Values provided at init

# At init time:
$ terraform init \
    -backend-config="bucket=anihpj-tfstate-dev" \
    -backend-config="key=dev/terraform.tfstate" \
    -backend-config="region=us-east-1" \
    -backend-config="dynamodb_table=terraform-locks"

5.11 Remote State Data Source — Reading Outputs from Another State

The terraform_remote_state data source lets one Terraform configuration read outputs from ANOTHER configuration's state file. This is how you share data between teams.

Remote State Data Source
# App team reads VPC ID from Networking team's state
data "terraform_remote_state" "network" {
  backend = "s3"
  config = {
    bucket = "networking-team-tfstate"
    key    = "prod/terraform.tfstate"
    region = "us-east-1"
  }
}

resource "aws_instance" "app" {
  subnet_id = data.terraform_remote_state.network.outputs.subnet_ids[0]
  # ← Reads subnet_id from the networking team's state file!
}

5.9 Sensitive Data in State — What's Stored and How to Protect It

🔴 State = Plain Text Secrets

Marking a variable as sensitive = true only hides it from console output. The value is still stored in plain text in the state file. Protection stack:

  • ✅ Remote state backend with encryption (S3 SSE-KMS, Azure enc)
  • ✅ Restrict access to state bucket (IAM policies)
  • ✅ Never commit state files to Git
  • ✅ Use Vault or AWS Secrets Manager for truly sensitive values

🔑 Ch5 Key Facts — Must-Know for the Exam

📁State = JSONMaps .tf → real resources. Never commit to Git.
🔒S3 + DynamoDBS3 stores state. DynamoDB locks. Both required for prod.
🔄WorkspacesIsolated state per env. NOT for strong separation — use TFC.
🔐Encrypt StateSensitive=true hides output, NOT state. Encrypt at rest.
🛡️Backed Up.tfstate.backup = previous state. Recovery safety net.
⬆️Migrate Stateinit -migrate-state: local→remote. One-time copy.

📝 Chapter 5 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
Where is the Terraform state stored by default?
Show Answer
Answer

Locally, in terraform.tfstate in the root module directory. This is called local backend. For teams, you configure a remote backend (S3, TFC, GCS, etc.).

Explanation

Default = local backend (file on disk). This works for solo dev but fails for teams — two people can't share a local file. Remote backends (S3, TFC, GCS) solve this: state is in a shared location with locking. The exam will ask: "What is the default backend?" → local.

Q2
What is state locking and why is it essential?
Show Answer
Answer

State locking prevents concurrent modifications to the same state file. Without locking, two simultaneous terraform apply runs could corrupt the state file.

Explanation

Local state uses .terraform.tfstate.lock.info. S3 uses DynamoDB table. TFC has built-in locking. The exam tests: "Which AWS service provides state locking for S3 backend?" → DynamoDB.

Q3
What command shows all resources currently in state?
Show Answer
Answer

terraform state list — lists every resource address in the state file. Use terraform state show ADDR for details on one resource.

Explanation

terraform state list is your go-to for exploring state. Output: aws_instance.web[0], aws_db_instance.anihpj, etc. Then terraform state show aws_db_instance.anihpj dumps all attributes. Safe — read-only operations.

Q4
What does terraform state mv do?
Show Answer
Answer

It renames/moves a resource in the state file. Used when refactoring — changing a resource name without destroying and recreating it.

# Rename without destroy:
terraform state mv aws_instance.old_name aws_instance.new_name
Explanation
# Original (creates "anihpj-new" as a NEW resource):
resource "aws_instance" "anihpj-old" { ... }  # ← Terraform wants to DESTROY this!
resource "aws_instance" "anihpj-new" { ... }  # ← Terraform creates NEW resource

# Fix: rename WITHOUT destroy
terraform state mv aws_instance.anihpj-old aws_instance.anihpj-new
# Now both blocks point to the same real resource. Zero downtime!

Without state mv, changing a resource name = destroy old + create new. With state mv, you just update the state file's internal reference — no API calls, zero downtime.

Q5
What's the difference between terraform state rm and terraform destroy?
Show Answer
Answer

state rm removes from state only (resource still exists in cloud). destroy deletes the real resource from the cloud provider.

Explanation
# state rm = "stop managing, leave it running"
terraform state rm aws_db_instance.old_legacy
# DB still exists! Just no longer in Terraform state.

# destroy = "DELETE from AWS"
terraform destroy -target=aws_db_instance.old_legacy
# DB is GONE. Data is GONE. Recovery from snapshots only.

This is a heavily tested distinction. state rm = "Terraform, stop managing this." destroy = "Terraform, DELETE this."

Q6
How do you migrate state from local to remote (S3)?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Add backend "s3" { ... } to terraform {} block. 2. Run terraform init -migrate-state. 3. Confirm "yes" when prompted to copy state.

Explanation

-migrate-state automatically copies your local state to the new remote backend. Without it, Terraform would start with an empty remote state — seeing all resources as "to be created." The migration is a one-time operation per backend change.

Q7
What is the terraform state pull command?
Show Answer
Answer

Downloads the remote state as JSON to stdout. Useful for debugging or scripting. Reverse: terraform state push uploads state (⚠️ dangerous).

Explanation

terraform state pull > backup.json creates a local backup of remote state. NEVER use state push casually — it overwrites the remote state file. The exam will ask which command is safe (pull) vs dangerous (push).

Q8
What backends support native state locking?
Show Answer
Answer

S3 (via DynamoDB), Terraform Cloud (built-in), Azure Blob (lease), GCS (no native), Consul (sessions), HTTP (none). The exam focuses on S3 + DynamoDB as the standard AWS pattern.

Explanation

Locking prevents state corruption. S3 alone = NO locking — you MUST add DynamoDB. TFC has it built-in. If a backend doesn't support locking, Terraform will warn you and YOU must ensure no concurrent applies happen. The exam WILL test this.

Q9
What happens if you delete the state file?
Show Answer
Answer

Terraform "forgets" about all managed resources. Running terraform plan shows everything as "to be created" — it would try to recreate resources that already exist, causing API errors or duplicates.

Explanation

This is why state backups matter. terraform.tfstate.backup saves you — copy it over the corrupted state file. For remote state, use versioned S3 buckets so you can restore previous versions. The exam: "How do you recover from corrupted state?" → restore from backup.

Q10
How do you encrypt Terraform state at rest in S3?
Show Answer
Answer

encrypt = true enables SSE-S3 (AES-256). Add kms_key_id = "alias/tfstate-key" for SSE-KMS with customer-managed keys and CloudTrail audit.

Explanation

State contains ALL resource attributes in plain text — including secrets from provider responses. SSE-S3 is basic encryption. SSE-KMS adds key management + audit trail (who accessed state). For HIPAA/PCI-DSS compliance, KMS is required. Always encrypt state at rest.

📝 Chapter 3 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
What is the correct ORDER of the Terraform core workflow?
Show Answer
Answer

Write → Init → Plan → Apply (→ Destroy, if needed)

# The Terraform Core Workflow:
1. Write .tf files       (define desired infrastructure)
2. terraform init        (download providers & modules)
3. terraform plan        (preview changes before applying)
4. terraform apply       (execute changes via API calls)
5. terraform destroy     (optional — remove everything)
Explanation

This order is fixed and logical: you must WRITE code before you can INIT, must INIT before you can PLAN (providers need to be downloaded), and must PLAN before APPLY (to review changes). The exam will test this directly — questions like "What's the first command you run after creating main.tf?" Answer: terraform init.

Q2
What does terraform init do? (Select the MOST complete answer)
Show Answer
Answer

Downloads required providers and modules, initializes the backend, and creates the .terraform directory.

Explanation

terraform init is the first command after writing .tf files. It: (1) downloads provider binaries from the Terraform Registry, (2) downloads referenced modules, (3) initializes the configured backend (local or remote), and (4) creates .terraform.lock.hcl. It does NOT validate syntax (terraform validate does that) and does NOT create resources (terraform apply does that).

Q3
What is the difference between terraform fmt and terraform validate?
Show Answer
Answer

fmt formats code style (indentation, spacing). validate checks HCL syntax and resource references.

Explanation

This is a classic exam trick question. terraform fmt is cosmetic — it fixes indentation, alignment, and spacing to the canonical HCL style. It does NOT check if your code is valid. terraform validate actually checks syntax, resource references, and attribute types but makes NO API calls. Always run BOTH before commit: terraform fmt -recursive && terraform validate.

Q4
What is the Terraform state file and why is it critical?
Show Answer
Answer

The state file (terraform.tfstate) maps HCL resource definitions to real-world resource IDs. It enables idempotency, dependency tracking, and drift detection.

Explanation

Without state, Terraform can't know which cloud resources it created. State is what makes Terraform different from Ansible. It enables: (1) idempotency — "I already created i-abc123, so I won't create it again", (2) dependency resolution — "Subnet needs VPC vpc-xyz, which already exists", (3) drift detection — "The security group was changed outside Terraform — I need to fix it."

Q5
Which AWS services are used together for Terraform remote state storage with locking?
Show Answer
Answer

S3 stores the state file. DynamoDB provides state locking.

backend "s3" {
  bucket         = "my-tfstate-bucket"       ← S3 stores the state
  key            = "prod/terraform.tfstate"
  region         = "us-east-1"
  dynamodb_table = "terraform-locks"         ← DynamoDB for locks
  encrypt        = true
}
Explanation

S3 alone does NOT lock. Two people could read the same state, apply changes, and corrupt infrastructure. DynamoDB provides a distributed lock: when Alice runs terraform apply, a lock item is created in the DynamoDB table. Bob's terraform apply sees the lock and waits. The exam WILL ask "which service provides locking?" — answer: DynamoDB.

Q6
What command would you use to bring an existing RDS database (created manually) under Terraform management?
Show Answer
Answer

terraform import aws_db_instance.my_db my-db-identifier

You must first write a matching resource block in your .tf files, then run terraform import to link it to the state file.

Explanation

terraform import adds a resource TO THE STATE FILE only. It does NOT generate HCL code. The workflow: (1) Write the resource block in main.tf, (2) Run terraform import <address> <real-ID>, (3) Run terraform plan to verify config matches reality. Common exam trap: thinking import generates code — it doesn't.

Q7
Which files/directories should be in .gitignore for a Terraform project?
Show Answer
Answer
# .gitignore for Terraform:
.terraform/              # Downloaded providers & modules
*.tfstate                # State files (contain secrets!)
*.tfstate.*              # State backups
*.tfvars                 # Variable files (may contain secrets)
override.tf              # Local override files
override.tf.json
.terraform.lock.hcl      # ⚠️ DEBATABLE — HashiCorp says COMMIT this!
Explanation

The golden rules: NEVER commit .terraform/ (downloaded binaries) or *.tfstate (contains secrets). DO commit .terraform.lock.hcl — HashiCorp explicitly recommends this to ensure consistent provider versions across your team. .tfvars files MAY contain secrets, so be cautious. The exam expects you to know: state files = NO, lock file = YES.

Q8
What is a Terraform data source and when would you use one?
Show Answer
Answer

A data source is a READ-ONLY query that fetches information about existing infrastructure. Use it when you need to reference resources NOT managed by the current Terraform configuration.

# Example: Look up an existing VPC by tag
data "aws_vpc" "existing" {
  filter { name = "tag:Name"; values = ["shared-vpc"] }
}

# Use it without creating a new VPC
resource "aws_subnet" "new" {
  vpc_id = data.aws_vpc.existing.id  # ← References existing VPC
}
Explanation

Data sources run during terraform plan and terraform refresh. They make READ-ONLY API calls. Use them for: (1) cross-stack references (networking team's VPC → app team's subnet), (2) looking up AMI IDs that change over time, (3) fetching secrets from AWS Secrets Manager. Critical distinction: resource = CREATE/MANAGE; data = READ-ONLY QUERY.

Q9
What does required_version = ">= 1.5.0, < 2.0.0" constrain?
Show Answer
Answer

It constrains the TERRAFORM CLI version, NOT the provider version.

Explanation

This is one of the most common exam traps. required_version in the terraform {} block controls the Terraform binary version. Provider versions are controlled by required_providers { aws = { version = "~> 5.0" } } — a completely different block. If a team member tries to terraform plan with version 2.0.0, they'll get an error: "Unsupported Terraform Core version."

Q10
What is the purpose of terraform state mv and how does it differ from terraform state rm?
Show Answer
Answer

state mv renames/moves a resource within the state file. state rm removes a resource from state WITHOUT destroying the real infrastructure.

# Rename: aws_instance.old_name → aws_instance.new_name
$ terraform state mv aws_instance.old_name aws_instance.new_name

# Remove from state (resource still exists in AWS!)
$ terraform state rm aws_instance.old_server
# ⚠️ The EC2 instance is NOT destroyed — just "forgotten" by Terraform
Explanation

terraform state mv is used when you refactor your code and rename resources — it preserves the resource's state under the new name. terraform state rm is dangerous: it removes the resource from state but leaves the real infrastructure running (and now orphaned). Use rm only when intentionally splitting state files or removing resources from Terraform management permanently.

Part 3: Providers & Resources — Bridging HCL to Real Cloud Infrastructure

Providers are the magic that makes Terraform multi-cloud — each one is a Go plugin translating HCL into cloud API calls. Resources are the building blocks — every VPC, EC2 instance, RDS database, and S3 bucket you'll ever create. In Part 3, you'll provision the complete anihpj/jobpost infrastructure on AWS with Docker and Kubernetes, master resource lifecycle rules, and learn the critical distinction between in-place updates and destroy/recreate — a heavily tested exam topic (~20% of your score).

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~6 hours 📖 Ch 6-8 ☁️ AWS + K8s 🎯 Domain 3

Chapter 6: Providers — The Bridge to Infrastructure 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 6: Providers — The Universal Adapters That Connect Terraform to Everything

Providers are the magic that makes Terraform multi-cloud. Each provider is a Go plugin that translates HCL resource declarations into the specific API calls of a platform — AWS, Azure, GCP, Kubernetes, Datadog, GitHub, and 3000+ more. Understanding providers deeply is essential because every resource you create depends on them.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔌 Providers 🌐 Multi-Cloud

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ How providers work internally — the adapter pattern that translates HCL to API calls
  • ✅ Provider configuration — authentication, regions, aliases for multi-region
  • ✅ Version constraints — how ~> 5.0 protects you from breaking changes
  • ✅ The major providers — AWS (4B+ downloads), Azure, GCP, Kubernetes, Docker
  • ✅ Multiple provider configurations for multi-region and multi-account setups
  • ✅ How terraform init downloads and caches provider binaries

6.1 Provider Architecture — How Terraform Talks to Clouds

A provider is a Go binary (~100-500MB) that acts as a translator between Terraform Core and a cloud platform's REST API. Terraform Core speaks a generic resource language. The provider translates that into specific API calls.

🧠 Exam Focus: Provider Version Constraints

The exam tests provider versioning extensively. Key points to remember:

  • version = "~> 5.0" — allows 5.x but NOT 6.0 (rightmost component only)
  • version = ">= 4.0, < 5.0" — explicit range (pessimistic constraint is clearer)
  • Provider versions are pinned in .terraform.lock.hcl — COMMIT this file to Git
  • terraform init -upgrade — upgrades providers to latest within constraints
  • Providers are downloaded from the Terraform Registry at registry.terraform.io
PROVIDER ARCHITECTURE — The Adapter Pattern
  TERRAFORM PROVIDER = Translator between HCL and Cloud APIs

  ┌─────────────┐         ┌──────────────────┐         ┌──────────┐
  │  main.tf    │────────▶│  AWS PROVIDER    │────────▶│ AWS API  │
  │  (HCL)      │         │  (Go Binary)     │         │ (REST)   │
  │             │         │                  │         │          │
  │ resource    │         │ Translates HCL   │         │ Creates  │
  │ "aws_vpc"   │         │ → AWS API calls  │         │ VPC in   │
  │ "main" {    │         │                  │         │ your     │
  │   cidr =    │         │ Handles:          │         │ account  │
  │   "10.0..." │         │ • Auth (IAM/STS) │         │          │
  │ }           │         │ • Retries         │         │          │
  └─────────────┘         │ • Error handling  │         └──────────┘
                          │ • State mapping   │
  Same HCL,               └──────────────────┘
  different provider:
  ┌─────────────┐         ┌──────────────────┐         ┌──────────┐
  │  main.tf    │────────▶│  AZURE PROVIDER  │────────▶│ Azure    │
  │  (HCL)      │         │  (Go Binary)     │         │ REST API │
  │             │         │                  │         │          │
  │ resource    │         │ SAME HCL pattern  │         │ Creates  │
  │ "azurerm_   │         │ → Different API  │         │ VNet in  │
  │ virtual_    │         │ → Same workflow!  │         │ Azure    │
  │ network"... │         │                  │         │          │
  └─────────────┘         └──────────────────┘         └──────────┘

  💡 KEY INSIGHT: You learn HCL ONCE, deploy to ANY cloud.

6.2 The Provider Block — Configuration & Authentication

Provider Block — Basic & Advanced Configurations
# Basic: Single region
provider "aws" {
  region = "us-east-1"
}

# Advanced: Multi-region with aliases
provider "aws" {
  alias  = "us_west"
  region = "us-west-2"
}

provider "aws" {
  alias  = "eu_west"
  region = "eu-west-1"
}

# Use specific provider in a resource
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "dr_backup" {
  provider = aws.us_west       # ← Uses the aliased provider
  bucket   = "anihpj-dr-backup"
}

6.3 Provider Versioning & Constraints — ~> 4.0 Explained

PROVIDER VERSION CONSTRAINTS — What ~> Really Means
  required_providers {
    aws = {
      source  = "hashicorp/aws"
      version = "~> 5.0"    ◀── What does this mean?
    }
  }

  "~> 5.0" = ">= 5.0, < 6.0"   (Pessimistic Constraint)

    5.0.0 ✅    5.1.0 ✅    5.99.0 ✅    6.0.0 ❌
    ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ">= 5.0, < 5.1"   = "~> 5.0.0" (patch only)
    ">= 5.0, < 6.0"   = "~> 5.0"   (minor updates)
    ">= 5.0"          = ANY 5.x.x+ (dangerous — no upper bound!)
    "= 5.42.0"        = EXACT version (most locked down)

6.4 Multiple Provider Configurations — Aliases & Multi-Region

In production, you often need to deploy resources across multiple AWS regions or even multiple cloud providers from a single Terraform configuration. Aliases make this possible.

Multi-Region + Multi-Cloud Example
# Primary (default) AWS provider
provider "aws" { region = "us-east-1" }

# Disaster recovery region
provider "aws" { alias = "dr"; region = "us-west-2" }

# Also deploy to Azure
provider "azurerm" { features {} }

# Use them in resources
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "primary" {
  bucket = "anihpj-assets"       # Uses default provider
}
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "dr" {
  provider = aws.dr              # Uses DR provider
  bucket   = "anihpj-assets-dr"
}

6.7 Provider Authentication Methods

HOW ANIHPJ AUTHENTICATES TO MULTIPLE PROVIDERS
  AWS:         AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID + AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY
               (env vars) OR ~/.aws/credentials OR IAM Role

  Docker:      Unix socket (/var/run/docker.sock)
               OR DOCKER_HOST=tcp://...

  Kubernetes:  KUBECONFIG file (~/.kube/config)
               OR in-cluster service account

  Helm:        Same as Kubernetes (uses kubeconfig)

  ⚠️ NEVER hardcode credentials in .tf files!
  ✅ Use env vars, Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, or TFC variables

6.6 Provider Registry — Finding, Trusting & Pinning Providers

The Terraform Registry (registry.terraform.io) is the central catalog of providers and modules. Every provider has a page showing documentation, version history, and usage examples.

📖 How Provider Resolution Works

source = "hashicorp/aws" resolves to registry.terraform.io/hashicorp/aws. The format is hostname/namespace/provider. For the public registry, the hostname defaults to registry.terraform.io. For private registries (Terraform Cloud), use the full hostname. Always check a provider's download count and version history before adopting it — this indicates community trust and active maintenance.

6.8 Building Your Own Provider — When and Why

Terraform providers can be built for any API. If your organization has an internal platform with a REST API, you can build a Terraform provider for it — enabling your team to manage internal services using the same HCL workflow.

💡 When to Build a Custom Provider

✅ Your company has an internal platform with a REST API. ✅ You want to manage SaaS tools not yet covered by existing providers. ✅ You need tighter integration than the HTTP provider offers. The Terraform Plugin Framework (Go SDK) provides scaffolding. Start with: terraform-provider-scaffold repository.

7.1 AWS Provider Setup — VPC, EC2, RDS, S3, IAM for anihpj

🧠 Exam Focus: Provisioners Are a Last Resort

The Terraform Associate exam WILL test provisioner best practices:

  • Provisioners are NOT idempotent — they run scripts, don't manage state
  • local-exec runs on YOUR machine; remote-exec needs SSH to the resource
  • Failed provisioner → resource is TAINTED → destroyed & recreated next apply
  • Prefer user_data, cloud-init, or Packer golden images over provisioners
  • null_resource + triggers = the standard pattern for running scripts

7.2 Docker Provider — Local Development with Terraform

You can use Terraform with the Docker provider to spin up your ENTIRE local development environment with one command. No more "it works on my machine" — your infrastructure IS code.

Local Dev Environment — One terraform apply
resource "docker_network" "anihpj_net" { name = "anihpj-network" }
resource "docker_image" "postgres" { name = "postgres:16-alpine" }
resource "docker_image" "redis" { name = "redis:7-alpine" }

resource "docker_container" "postgres" {
  image = docker_image.postgres.image_id; name = "anihpj-db"
  networks_advanced { name = docker_network.anihpj_net.name }
  env = ["POSTGRES_DB=anihpj", "POSTGRES_PASSWORD=secret"]
}

resource "docker_container" "redis" {
  image = docker_image.redis.image_id; name = "anihpj-redis"
  networks_advanced { name = docker_network.anihpj_net.name }
}

7.3 Kubernetes Provider — Deploying anihpj to K8s

anihpj Deployment on Kubernetes
resource "kubernetes_deployment" "anihpj" {
  metadata { name = "anihpj-django" }
  spec {
    replicas = 3
    selector { match_labels = { app = "anihpj" } }
    template {
      metadata { labels = { app = "anihpj" } }
      spec {
        container {
          image = "anihpj/jobpost:${var.image_tag}"
          name  = "django"; port { container_port = 8000 }
          resources {
            limits   = { cpu = "500m"; memory = "512Mi" }
            requests = { cpu = "250m"; memory = "256Mi" }
          }
        }
      }
    }
  }
}

resource "kubernetes_service" "anihpj" {
  metadata { name = "anihpj-svc" }
  spec {
    selector = { app = "anihpj" }
    port { port = 80; target_port = 8000 }
    type = "LoadBalancer"
  }
}

7.4 Helm Provider — Deploying anihpj with Helm Charts

For even simpler Kubernetes deployments, use the Helm provider. Helm charts package all Kubernetes resources (Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, etc.) into a single versioned artifact.

Helm Release for anihpj
resource "helm_release" "anihpj" {
  name      = "anihpj"
  chart     = "./helm/anihpj"
  namespace = "anihpj-prod"

  set { name = "replicaCount"; value = "3" }
  set { name = "image.tag";   value = var.image_tag }
  set { name = "db.host";     value = module.rds.endpoint }
}

7.5 Random Provider — Generating Secure Passwords & Unique Names

Random Provider Examples
# Secure database password
resource "random_password" "db_pass" {
  length  = 32
  special = false     # Avoid chars that break connection strings
}

# Unique S3 bucket name (must be globally unique)
resource "random_id" "bucket_suffix" {
  byte_length = 4    # 8 hex characters
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket" "assets" {
  bucket = "anihpj-assets-${random_id.bucket_suffix.hex}"
}

7.10 Dockerfile for anihpj — Multi-Stage Production Build

Dockerfile — anihpj/jobpost Production-Ready Image
# ═══ STAGE 1: BUILD ═══
FROM python:3.11-slim AS builder
WORKDIR /app
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
    gcc libpq-dev && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --user --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# ═══ STAGE 2: RUNTIME ═══
FROM python:3.11-slim
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
    libpq-dev curl && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
RUN useradd --create-home --shell /bin/bash anihpj
COPY --from=builder /root/.local /home/anihpj/.local
COPY --chown=anihpj:anihpj . /app
WORKDIR /app; USER anihpj
ENV PATH="/home/anihpj/.local/bin:$PATH"
RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
EXPOSE 8000
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s --retries=3 \
  CMD curl -f http://localhost:8000/health/ || exit 1
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000",
     "--workers", "3", "--access-logfile", "-"]

7.6 Template/Cloud-Init Provider — Dynamic Configuration

templatefile() Function
# user_data.sh.tpl — Template with variables & conditionals
#!/bin/bash
echo "Deploying ${app_name} in ${environment}"
%{ if enable_ssl }
certbot --nginx -d ${domain}
%{ endif }
%{ for mount in efs_mounts }
mount -t efs ${mount.id}:/ ${mount.path}
%{ endfor }

7.7 Null Provider — Triggering Arbitrary Scripts

The null provider is a special provider that does nothing — it's used as a trigger mechanism for provisioners and to create resources that don't correspond to any real infrastructure.

Null Provider Example — Run DB Migrations
resource "null_resource" "run_migrations" {
  triggers = { db_endpoint = aws_db_instance.pg.address }
  provisioner "local-exec" {
    command = "python manage.py migrate"
  }
}

7.8 Time Provider — Rotation & Time-Based Resources

Time Provider — Rotate Passwords Every 30 Days
resource "time_rotating" "db_password" {
  rotation_days = 30
}
resource "random_password" "db_pass" {
  keepers = { rotation = time_rotating.db_password.id }
  length  = 32
}

7.9 TLS Provider — Certificates for anihpj HTTPS

TLS Provider — Self-Signed Dev Certificate
resource "tls_private_key" "anihpj" {
  algorithm = "RSA"; rsa_bits = 4096
}
resource "tls_self_signed_cert" "anihpj" {
  private_key_pem = tls_private_key.anihpj.private_key_pem
  subject { common_name = "anihpj.local" }
  validity_period_hours = 8760  # 1 year
  allowed_uses = ["key_encipherment", "digital_signature"]
}
