📝 Chapter 7 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
What is the Docker provider used for in Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

It manages Docker containers, images, networks, and volumes through Terraform. Useful for local development or managing container infrastructure on Docker hosts.

resource "docker_container" "web" {
  name  = "anihpj-web"
  image = docker_image.nginx.latest
  ports { internal = 80; external = 8080 }
}
Explanation

The Docker provider is great for local dev — define your PostgreSQL, Redis, and Django containers as code. But for production, use AWS ECS/EKS providers instead. The Docker provider works with local Docker daemon or remote Docker hosts via TCP/TLS.

Q2
How does the Kubernetes provider authenticate?
Show Answer
Answer

Through kubeconfig files, in-cluster service accounts, or explicit credentials. The provider reads ~/.kube/config by default, or you can specify config_path, host, client_certificate, etc.

Explanation

The K8s provider is often used WITH the AWS provider: Terraform creates the EKS cluster (via AWS provider), then the K8s provider deploys pods/services into that cluster. Use depends_on to ensure the cluster exists before deploying to it.

Q3
What are provisioners and when should you use them?
Show Answer
Answer

Provisioners run scripts on resource creation/destruction. Use as a last resort — prefer user_data, cloud-init, or config management tools. Common types: local-exec, remote-exec, file.

Explanation

The exam emphasizes: provisioners are a LAST RESORT. Terraform can't track what a provisioner script does — if it partially succeeds, state is unaware. Always prefer user_data (EC2), cloud-init, Packer golden images, or config management tools (Ansible, Chef).

Q4
What's the difference between local-exec and remote-exec provisioners?
Show Answer
Answer

local-exec: runs on the machine running Terraform. remote-exec: runs on the provisioned resource (requires SSH/WinRM connection).

Explanation
# Ch7 Q4 — local-exec vs remote-exec

# local-exec: runs WHERE Terraform runs (laptop, CI, TFC agent)
provisioner "local-exec" {
  command = "python manage.py migrate"
  working_dir = "../anihpj"
  environment = { DB_URL = aws_db_instance.pg.endpoint }
}

# remote-exec: runs ON the created resource (needs SSH)
provisioner "remote-exec" {
  inline = ["sudo apt update", "sudo apt install -y nginx"]
  connection { host = self.public_ip; type = "ssh"; user = "ubuntu" }
}

local-exec doesn't need SSH. remote-exec requires SSH connectivity. The exam will test this distinction.

Q5
What happens if a provisioner fails?
Show Answer
Answer

The resource is marked as tainted — it will be destroyed and recreated on the next terraform apply. Use on_failure = continue to allow the resource to persist despite provisioner failure.

Explanation

By default, a failed provisioner TAINTS the resource → destroyed and recreated on next apply. This can cause data loss. Use on_failure = continue when the provisioner is optional (e.g., sending a notification). The exam: "What happens when a provisioner fails?" → resource is tainted.

Q6
How do you pass values from a resource to a provisioner script?
Show Answer
Answer

Use self to reference the resource's attributes: self.public_ip. For local-exec, pass via environment map or interpolate directly in the command string.

Explanation

self is the magic variable inside provisioners — it refers to the parent resource. self.public_ip, self.private_key_pem, etc. For local-exec use the environment map: environment = { DB_HOST = self.address }. The script reads $DB_HOST.

Q7
What is the null_resource and when is it useful?
Show Answer
Answer

null_resource is a do-nothing resource that runs provisioners. Use it when you need to run a script but don't need a real cloud resource. Its triggers map controls when it re-runs.

Explanation

null_resource is the standard pattern for "run this script when X changes." Example: run database migrations whenever the DB endpoint or app version changes. The triggers act like a hash — if any trigger value changes, the null_resource is recreated (re-running its provisioners).

Q8
How do you provision an EC2 instance with a startup script WITHOUT using provisioners?
Show Answer
Answer

Use the user_data argument with templatefile(). This is the preferred approach — it runs at boot time without SSH, is idempotent, and doesn't require Terraform to maintain a connection.

Explanation

user_data is the AWS-native way to bootstrap instances. It runs once at launch, doesn't need SSH, and works at scale. Combined with templatefile(), you can inject dynamic values. This is the PREFERRED approach — provisioners are for when user_data can't do what you need.

Q9
What does the triggers argument in null_resource do?
Show Answer
Answer

It defines when to re-run the null_resource's provisioners. When any trigger value changes, the resource is recreated (re-running the provisioner). Example: triggers = { app_version = var.version }

Explanation

Triggers act as a fingerprint — when any trigger value changes, the null_resource is marked for recreation. This is how you make provisioners idempotent: tie triggers to the values your script depends on. If nothing changed, the script doesn't re-run.

Q10
Can provisioners be used with data sources?
Show Answer
Answer

No. Provisioners only work with resource blocks, not data sources. Data sources are read-only and don't support creation/destruction lifecycle hooks.

Explanation

Data sources are pure reads — they have no create/update/delete lifecycle, so provisioners (which hook into lifecycle events) have nothing to hook into. If you need to run a script based on a data source query, use null_resource with a trigger that depends on the data source.

Chapter 8: Resources Deep Dive — Mastering Terraform's Core Construct

Resources are the heart of Terraform. Every cloud component you manage — VPCs, instances, databases, load balancers — is defined as a resource block. This chapter goes deep into resource syntax, dependencies, lifecycle rules, meta-arguments, and the critical decisions about when Terraform updates in-place vs destroys and recreates.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🧱 Resources 🎯 Critical

8.1 Resource Syntax — Everything You Can Configure

Every resource block follows the same pattern: resource "type" "name" { arguments }. Understanding this syntax deeply means you can work with ANY resource from ANY provider.

Resource Syntax — Complete Reference
resource "aws_instance" "anihpj_web" {   # ← type.name = unique address
  # Required arguments (provider-specific)
  ami           = "ami-0c55b159cbfafe1f0"
  instance_type = "t3.micro"
  
  # Optional arguments
  subnet_id     = aws_subnet.public[0].id
  key_name      = var.ssh_key_name
  
  # Meta-arguments (available on ALL resources)
  count         = 3              # Create N copies
  depends_on    = [aws_s3_bucket.assets]  # Explicit dependency
  provider      = aws.us_west    # Non-default provider
  
  # Nested blocks
  root_block_device { volume_size = 20 }
  
  # Lifecycle
  lifecycle {
    create_before_destroy = true
    ignore_changes = [ami]       # Don't replace on AMI change
  }
}

8.2 Resource Dependencies — Implicit vs Explicit

IMPLICIT vs EXPLICIT DEPENDENCIES
  IMPLICIT (Terraform figures it out from references):
  resource "aws_vpc" "main" { cidr_block = "10.0.0.0/16" }
  resource "aws_subnet" "public" {
    vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id  ◀── Terraform sees this & KNOWS subnet
  }                                depends on VPC. Creates VPC FIRST.

  EXPLICIT (You tell Terraform — use only when no reference exists):
  resource "null_resource" "upload_files" {
    depends_on = [aws_s3_bucket.assets]  ◀── No reference to bucket
    provisioner "local-exec" {                in body, so TF wouldn't
      command = "aws s3 sync ./static s3://anihpj-assets/"  know.
    }                               depends_on fixes this.
  }

8.3 Resource Meta-Arguments — count, for_each, lifecycle, provider

Meta-arguments are special arguments available on EVERY resource block. They control how many instances are created, dependencies, and lifecycle behavior.

Meta-Arguments in Action
# count — Create N identical resources
resource "aws_instance" "web" {
  count         = 3                    # Creates web[0], web[1], web[2]
  instance_type = "t3.micro"
  tags = { Name = "web-${count.index}" }  # count.index = 0, 1, 2
}

# for_each — Create from a map (keys survive additions/removals)
resource "aws_instance" "app" {
  for_each      = { web="t3.medium", worker="t3.small" }
  instance_type = each.value           # "t3.medium", "t3.small"
  tags = { Name = each.key }           # "web", "worker"
}

# lifecycle — Control create/destroy behavior
resource "aws_db_instance" "prod_db" {
  # ... config ...
  lifecycle {
    prevent_destroy = true             # Block accidental deletion
    ignore_changes  = [engine_version] # Don't reset minor version upgrades
  }
}

8.4 Lifecycle Rules — create_before_destroy, prevent_destroy, ignore_changes

THE 4 LIFECYCLE META-ARGUMENTS
  create_before_destroy = true    ← Create NEW resource FIRST, then destroy old
                                    ✅ Zero-downtime updates!
  prevent_destroy       = true    ← BLOCK accidental deletion
                                    ❌ terraform destroy REFUSES
  ignore_changes        = [...]   ← Ignore specific attribute changes
                                    e.g., ignore autoscaling adjustments
  replace_triggered_by  = [...]   ← Force recreate when attribute changes
                                    e.g., recreate when AMI changes

  create_before_destroy (avoid downtime):
  WITHOUT: Destroy old EC2 → (downtime!) → Create new EC2
  WITH:    Create new EC2 → (both running) → Destroy old EC2
           ✅ Zero downtime!  ⚠️ Need enough quota for 2x resources

8.5 Tainted Resources — terraform taint

Tainting marks a resource for destruction and recreation on the next terraform apply. Use it when a resource is in a bad state and you want a fresh replacement.

Using taint to Force Recreation
$ terraform taint aws_instance.anihpj_web[0]
Resource aws_instance.anihpj_web[0] is marked as tainted.

# Next terraform apply:
# 1. DESTROY the tainted resource
# 2. CREATE a brand new replacement

$ terraform untaint aws_instance.anihpj_web[0]  # Remove taint if mistake
$ terraform apply  # Now nothing happens to web[0]

8.8 Provisioners — The "Last Resort" Pattern

Provisioners let you run scripts during resource creation or destruction. But HashiCorp recommends using them only as a last resort — they break Terraform's declarative model and can't track what they've done.

PROVISIONER HIERARCHY — Best to Worst
  ✅ BEST: Provider-native features (user_data, cloud-init)
  ✅ GOOD: Pre-baked AMI / Docker image with everything needed
  ⚠️ OK:   Configuration management (Ansible AFTER Terraform)
  🔴 LAST: Terraform provisioners (local-exec, remote-exec)

  local-exec (runs on YOUR machine):
  provisioner "local-exec" {
    command = "echo ${self.public_ip} >> inventory.ini"
  }
  ⚠️ Runs on Terraform host, NOT the resource
  ❌ Not captured in state — if fails, Terraform may not know

  remote-exec (runs ON the resource via SSH):
  provisioner "remote-exec" {
    inline = ["sudo apt-get update", "sudo apt-get install -y nginx"]
  }
  ⚠️ Requires SSH access; can't track provisioner state

8.6 Resource Targeting — Apply to Specific Resources Only

The -target flag limits plan, apply, or destroy to specific resources. Use only for debugging — never in production workflows.

Resource Targeting
$ terraform plan -target=aws_instance.anihpj_web[0]
# Only shows web[0] — everything else hidden
# ⚠️ Breaks the dependency graph! Use only for recovery.

8.7 Timeouts — Custom Operation Time Limits

Resource Timeouts
resource "aws_db_instance" "anihpj_db" {
  engine = "postgres"; instance_class = "db.t3.medium"
  timeouts {
    create = "40m"   # Default 20min; RDS can take longer
    update = "80m"   # Multi-AZ conversion can be SLOW
    delete = "60m"   # Final snapshot takes time
  }
}

8.9 Connection Block — SSH/WinRM for Remote Provisioners

Connection blocks configure how Terraform connects to a resource for remote-exec and file provisioners.

Connection Block
connection {
  type        = "ssh"
  user        = "ec2-user"
  private_key = file("~/.ssh/anihpj.pem")
  host        = self.public_ip
}

8.10 Resource Replacement vs Update — When Terraform Destroys

Change MadeActionPlan Symbol
Change tagsUpdate in-place~
Change instance_typeRecreate 🔄-/+
Change AMIRecreate 🔄-/+
Change security groupUpdate in-place~
Change subnetRecreate 🔄-/+
Change volume size (up)Update in-place~
Change volume size (down)Recreate 🔄-/+
REPLACE vs UPDATE — The Decision Algorithm (Exam Critical!)
  YOU CHANGE AN ATTRIBUTE IN .tf
           │
           ▼
  ┌─────────────────────┐
  │ Can provider update │
  │ this in-place?      │
  └─────────┬───────────┘
            │
    ┌───────┴───────┐
    │               │
    ▼ YES           ▼ NO
 ┌────────┐    ┌──────────────┐
 │ UPDATE │    │ DESTROY +    │
 │ IN-PLACE│   │ RECREATE     │
 │ ~ tags │    │ -/+ AMI      │
 │ ~ sg   │    │ -/+ subnet   │
 └────────┘    │ -/+ vol down │
               └──────┬───────┘
                      │
                      ▼
               ┌──────────────────┐
               │ lifecycle {       │
               │   create_before_  │
               │   destroy = true  │
               │ }                 │
               │ TRUE: New first → │
               │   then delete old │
               │ FALSE: Delete old │
               │   first → new     │
               └──────────────────┘
⚠️ Exam Trap: AMI change = DESTROY/RECREATE

EC2 instances do NOT support in-place AMI updates. Changing the AMI ID in your resource block causes Terraform to destroy the old instance and create a new one. This means DOWNTIME unless you use create_before_destroy. The exam WILL test this.

📝 Chapter 8 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
What is the resource address format in Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

resource_type.resource_name — e.g., aws_instance.web. For modules: module.name.resource_type.resource_name. This address is used in terraform state commands, -target, and depends_on.

Q2
What does depends_on do and when is it necessary?
Show Answer
Answer

It creates an explicit dependency between resources. Only needed when Terraform can't infer the dependency from references — typically for IAM policies, null_resources, or when dependencies are hidden inside provisioners.

depends_on = [aws_iam_role_policy.ecs_policy]  # Explicit ordering
Explanation

Terraform builds an implicit dependency graph from references. depends_on is ONLY needed when Terraform can't infer the dependency — typically IAM policies (referenced by ARN string, not resource reference), null_resources, or provisioner-internal dependencies. Overusing it is an anti-pattern.

Q3
What are the three lifecycle meta-arguments?
Show Answer
Answer

create_before_destroy — new resource created first, then old deleted. prevent_destroy — blocks accidental deletion (terraform destroy fails). ignore_changes — ignores external changes to specified attributes.

Explanation

These three lifecycle rules control resource replacement behavior. create_before_destroy is critical for zero-downtime. prevent_destroy protects stateful resources (databases!). ignore_changes handles external modifications. The exam will test all three.

Q4
When would you use create_before_destroy?
Show Answer
Answer

For zero-downtime deployments — when you need the new resource running before the old one is destroyed. Critical for stateful resources like databases, or when an EC2 instance's AMI changes.

Explanation

Without create_before_destroy, Terraform deletes the old resource FIRST, then creates the new one — causing downtime. With it, the new resource is created while the old one still runs. Once the new one is ready, the old is destroyed. Requires resources to have unique names (append random suffix).

Q5
What does ignore_changes do? Give a real example.
Show Answer
Answer

It tells Terraform to ignore external modifications to specific attributes. Example: ignore_changes = [tags, tags_all] — if someone adds a tag in the AWS console, Terraform won't remove it on the next apply.

Explanation

Common use: ignore_changes = [ami, user_data] on an ASG launch template — if someone updates the AMI outside Terraform, don't revert it. Another: ignore_changes = [tags, tags_all] when cloud services auto-add tags. The exam: "How do you prevent Terraform from overwriting manual changes?" → ignore_changes.

Q6
What is taint and what replaced it?
Show Answer
Answer

terraform taint ADDR marks a resource for recreation on next apply. Deprecated in TF 1.5+ — use terraform apply -replace=ADDR instead. The -replace flag is safer because it doesn't modify state until apply succeeds.

Explanation

terraform taint modifies state immediately (marks resource as "tainted"). If the subsequent apply fails, state is already changed. -replace doesn't touch state until the replacement succeeds — much safer. The exam may ask about the deprecated taint command.

Q7
How does Terraform determine the order to create/destroy resources?
Show Answer
Answer

It builds a dependency graph automatically from resource references. Resources with dependencies wait. Destruction happens in reverse order. depends_on adds explicit edges to the graph.

Explanation

Terraform builds a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG). Each resource reference (like aws_vpc.main.id) creates an edge. Creation = topological order (dependencies first). Destruction = reverse topological order. Resources at the same level are created in parallel (up to -parallelism limit).

Q8
What is a resource's id attribute?
Show Answer
Answer

Every resource has an id — the provider's unique identifier for that resource. For AWS: i-0c55b159cbfafe1f0 (EC2), vpc-12345 (VPC). It's set by the provider after creation and stored in state.

Explanation

The id is the bridge between Terraform and the cloud provider. Terraform uses the address (aws_instance.web) internally, but the provider uses the id (i-0c55b159) for API calls. The state file maps one to the other. All resources have an id even if you don't explicitly set it.

Q9
What happens when you change a resource's name in .tf?
Show Answer
Answer

Terraform sees it as delete old + create new (destroy & recreate), NOT a rename. Use terraform state mv old_name new_name to rename without destroying the resource.

Explanation

Resource names are part of the resource ADDRESS, not the resource itself. Changing the name = new address = Terraform thinks old resource should be deleted and a new one created. state mv just updates the address mapping in state — no API calls, no downtime. Common exam trap.

Q10
What is the difference between count and for_each applied to resources?
Show Answer
Answer

count = 3 creates resources name[0], name[1], name[2] — if you remove [1], [2] shifts down and gets destroyed. for_each uses stable keys from a map/set — removing one key only destroys that one resource.

Explanation

This is the single most tested resource meta-argument distinction. With count, index values determine resource identity — changing the list causes cascading destroys. With for_each, the map key IS the identity — add/remove freely without affecting other resources. The exam will ask: "Which should you use?" → for_each for most cases.

📝 Chapter 6 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1
What is a Terraform provider and how does it work?
Show Answer
Answer

A provider is a Go binary plugin that translates HCL resource declarations into cloud platform API calls. It is downloaded during terraform init.

Explanation

Providers are the adapter layer between Terraform Core and cloud APIs. Each provider (~100-500MB Go binary) knows the specific API of its target platform. When you write resource "aws_vpc" "main" {}, the AWS provider translates that into a CreateVpc API call. Providers handle authentication, retries, error handling, and state mapping. They're stored in .terraform/providers/ after init.

Q2
What does the version constraint ~> 5.0 mean for a Terraform provider?
Show Answer
Answer

It means ">= 5.0, < 6.0" — any version 5.x.x is allowed, but version 6.0.0 and above are blocked.

Explanation

This is called a "pessimistic constraint." It allows patch and minor updates (5.0.1, 5.1.0, 5.99.0) but blocks major version bumps (6.0.0) that could contain breaking changes. Compare: ~> 5.0 = any 5.x, ~> 5.0.0 = any 5.0.x only, >= 5.0 = any version above 5.0 (dangerous — no upper bound).

Q3
How do you use multiple AWS provider configurations (e.g., for multi-region)?
Show Answer
Answer

Use provider aliases. Define one provider without alias (default) and additional providers with unique aliases. Reference them in resources with provider = aws.alias_name.

provider "aws" { region = "us-east-1" }              # default
provider "aws" { alias = "west"; region = "us-west-2" }
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "dr" { provider = aws.west }
Q4
What is the difference between implicit and explicit resource dependencies?
Show Answer
Answer

Implicit: Terraform detects dependency from references (e.g., vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id). Explicit: You declare it with depends_on when no reference exists.

Explanation

Implicit dependencies are preferred — they're automatic and always correct. Use depends_on only when there's no actual attribute reference between resources (e.g., a null_resource provisioner that needs an S3 bucket to exist). Overusing depends_on reduces parallelism and slows down applies.

Q5
What does create_before_destroy = true do in a lifecycle block?
Show Answer
Answer

It tells Terraform to create the NEW resource FIRST, verify it's working, and THEN destroy the old resource. This enables zero-downtime updates.

Q6
When should you avoid using Terraform provisioners?
Show Answer
Answer

Almost always. Use provider-native features (user_data, cloud-init) or configuration management tools (Ansible) instead. Provisioners are a last resort.

Explanation

Provisioners break Terraform's declarative model: Terraform can't track what the provisioner did, can't revert it, and may not know if it failed. They run only on CREATE (not update). The HashiCorp-recommended pattern is: (1) pre-bake AMIs/Docker images, (2) use user_data/cloud-init for bootstrap, (3) use Ansible/Chef for complex configuration — all invoked OUTSIDE of Terraform provisioners.

Q7
What happens when you change the AMI ID of an aws_instance resource?
Show Answer
Answer

The instance is DESTROYED and RECREATED (shown as -/+ in plan). EC2 does NOT support in-place AMI updates.

Q8
How does prevent_destroy = true protect your infrastructure?
Show Answer
Answer

It blocks any attempt to destroy the resource. terraform destroy will ERROR on that resource. You must remove the lifecycle rule before destroying.

Q9
What does terraform taint do?
Show Answer
Answer

It marks a resource for destruction and recreation on the next terraform apply. Use when a resource is in a bad state.

$ terraform taint aws_instance.web[0]      # Mark for recreation
$ terraform untaint aws_instance.web[0]    # Remove the mark
$ terraform apply                           # Destroys & recreates web[0]
Q10
What are the 4 lifecycle meta-arguments available on every resource?
Show Answer
Answer

1. create_before_destroy — Create new before destroying old. 2. prevent_destroy — Block accidental deletion. 3. ignore_changes — Ignore specific attribute changes. 4. replace_triggered_by — Force recreate when specified attributes change.

You now understand providers (the bridge to AWS cloud APIs) and resources (the building blocks of AWS infrastructure). Here's how to apply this knowledge in production with anihpj/jobpost on AWS.

🛠️ How to Implement — AWS Provider & Resource Strategy

AWS Provider & Resource Implementation — 5-Step Production Pattern
  STEP 1: PIN PROVIDERS               STEP 2: CONFIGURE AUTH
  ────────────────────                 ────────────────────
  terraform { required_providers {     provider "aws" {
    aws = { source="hashicorp/aws";       region = "us-east-1"
            version = "~> 5.0" }          # Uses AWS CLI credentials
  } } }                                  # OR env: AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID

  STEP 3: WRITE RESOURCES             STEP 4: MANAGE LIFECYCLE
  ────────────────────                 ────────────────────
  resource "aws_vpc" "main" {          lifecycle {
    cidr_block = "10.0.0.0/16"           create_before_destroy = true
  }                                       prevent_destroy = true (for DBs)
                                          ignore_changes = [desired_count]
  resource "aws_instance" "web" {
    count = 3; ami = data.aws_ami...   STEP 5: TEST & APPLY
    vpc_id = aws_vpc.main.id            ────────────────────
  }                                     terraform fmt → validate → plan
                                        Review plan → terraform apply

⚡ Part 3 Speed Tips — Provider & Resource Mastery
  • Always pin provider versions with ~> X.Y — unpinned providers drift and break your config.
  • Implicit dependencies > explicit. Let Terraform figure out the order from references. Only use depends_on as a last resort.
  • Provisioners = last resort. Use cloud-init, pre-baked AMIs, or config management tools instead.
  • AMI change = destroy/recreate. Always use create_before_destroy if you need zero downtime.
  • count vs for_each: count for identical copies (0,1,2). for_each for named resources with stable keys.
  • prevent_destroy on databases. This one lifecycle rule saves you from the #1 production disaster.

Part 4: Variables, Outputs & Expressions — From Static to Smart Configs

Hard-coded values are the enemy of reusable infrastructure. In Part 4, you'll learn to make your Terraform configurations dynamic, flexible, and production-ready. Input variables let you parameterize every aspect of your infrastructure — instance sizes, region names, environment labels. Expressions and functions transform data on the fly. Outputs expose critical information to other teams and CI/CD pipelines. Together, these three pillars turn a brittle single-environment config into a multi-environment, multi-region, team-ready infrastructure codebase for anihpj/jobpost.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~4 hours 📖 Ch 9-11 🎯 Domain 4

Chapter 9: Input Variables — Making Configs Flexible & Reusable 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 9: Input Variables — The Foundation of Reusable Infrastructure

Imagine you've written 500 lines of Terraform for anihpj/jobpost in the dev environment. Now you need the same infrastructure in staging and prod. Do you copy-paste and change 37 values? No — you replace those 37 hard-coded values with variables. Input variables are the difference between a one-off script and production-grade infrastructure as code. This chapter covers every aspect: declaration syntax, type system, assignment methods, validation rules, sensitivity, precedence hierarchy, and complex patterns like maps of objects.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 8 Sections 🎯 Domain 4
🎓 TF Associate Relevance
Domain 4: Variables & Outputs ~15% ⚠️ High-Yield

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Declare variables with type, default, description, validation, and sensitive flags
  • ✅ Master all 8 variable types — from simple strings to complex maps of objects
  • ✅ Assign values through 6 different methods — CLI flags, .tfvars files, env vars, defaults
  • ✅ Enforce business rules with custom validation blocks
  • ✅ Protect secrets with sensitive variables and understand their limitations
  • ✅ Memorize the variable precedence hierarchy — the #1 exam topic in Domain 4
  • ✅ Structure complex configs using maps of objects and workspace-based selection
Variable Precedence is THE Most-Tested Topic in Domain 4

If you memorize only one thing from this chapter, make it the variable precedence order. The exam WILL ask you "which value wins?" scenarios. The answer is always: CLI -var > *.auto.tfvars > terraform.tfvars > TF_VAR_* env vars > default. Every level overrides all levels below it. CLI flags are the nuclear option — they override everything, always.

9.1 Variable Declaration — The Anatomy of a Variable Block

Every input variable in Terraform is declared with a variable block. Think of it as a function parameter for your infrastructure — it says "I need this piece of information, here's what it should look like, and here's what happens if you don't give it to me."

ANATOMY OF A VARIABLE BLOCK — Every Field Explained
  variable "environment" {            ◀── ① NAME: The variable identifier
                                         Used as var.environment in HCL
    type        = string              ◀── ② TYPE: What kind of value?
                                         string, number, bool, list, map,
                                         set, object, tuple, any
    default     = "dev"               ◀── ③ DEFAULT: Fallback value
                                         If omitted, variable is REQUIRED
    description = "Deployment env:    ◀── ④ DESCRIPTION: Human-readable
                   dev, staging,      docs. Shows in terraform plan,
                   or prod"           console, and auto-generated docs
    sensitive   = true                ◀── ⑤ SENSITIVE: Hides from
                                         terraform plan/apply output
                                         ⚠️ Still stored PLAINTEXT in state!
    nullable    = true                ◀── ⑥ NULLABLE: Can value be null?
                                         Default: true (allows null)
    validation {                      ◀── ⑦ VALIDATION: Custom rules
      condition     = contains(       Prevents bad values BEFORE any
        ["dev","staging","prod"],     API call is made
        var.environment)
      error_message = "Must be dev, staging, or prod."
    }
  }
📋 Variable Rules You MUST Know

1. Names must be unique within a module. Two variable "name" blocks = error.

2. No default = required. Terraform will prompt interactively or fail in non-interactive mode.

3. Description shows everywhere. It appears in terraform plan, terraform console, and auto-generated documentation.

4. sensitive only hides output. The value is STILL stored in plaintext in the state file. Use remote state encryption.

5. Variable files are .tfvars or .tfvars.json. Any other extension is ignored unless passed with -var-file.

✅ Best Practices for Variable Declaration

Always add a description. Your future self (and teammates) will thank you. Descriptions are documentation.

Always specify a type. Type constraints catch errors early. type = string is better than no type.

Use sensible defaults. Set defaults for the most common case. Make staging/prod override, not the other way around.

Add validation for enumerated values. If only "dev"/"staging"/"prod" are valid, enforce it with a validation block.

9.2 Variable Types — From Simple Strings to Complex Objects

Terraform's type system is one of its most powerful features. Choosing the right type prevents entire categories of bugs at plan time — before any API call is made. Let's explore every type with real anihpj/jobpost examples.

THE 8 TERRAFORM VARIABLE TYPES — Complete Reference
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║                      PRIMITIVE TYPES                                 ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ║
  ║  │ 1. string                                                      │ ║
  ║  │    variable "env" { type = string; default = "prod" }          │ ║
  ║  │    Most common type. UTF-8 text.                               │ ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ║
  ║  │ 2. number                                                      │ ║
  ║  │    variable "port" { type = number; default = 8000 }           │ ║
  ║  │    Integer or float. No quotes! 8000 ≠ "8000"                  │ ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ║
  ║  │ 3. bool                                                        │ ║
  ║  │    variable "enable_monitoring" { type = bool; default = true }│ ║
  ║  │    true or false (lowercase, no quotes!)                       │ ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║                      COLLECTION TYPES                                ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ║
  ║  │ 4. list()          — ORDERED, duplicates allowed         │ ║
  ║  │    variable "azs" {                                            │ ║
  ║  │      type    = list(string)                                    │ ║
  ║  │      default = ["us-east-1a", "us-east-1b", "us-east-1c"]     │ ║
  ║  │    }                                                           │ ║
  ║  │    Access: var.azs[0] → "us-east-1a"                           │ ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ║
  ║  │ 5. set()           — UNORDERED, unique values only       │ ║
  ║  │    variable "allowed_ports" {                                  │ ║
  ║  │      type    = set(number)                                     │ ║
  ║  │      default = [80, 443, 8000]                                 │ ║
  ║  │    }                                                           │ ║
  ║  │    Duplicates auto-removed: [80, 443, 443, 8000] → {80,443,8000}│ ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ║
  ║  │ 6. map()           — KEY-VALUE lookup table              │ ║
  ║  │    variable "instance_types" {                                 │ ║
  ║  │      type = map(string)                                        │ ║
  ║  │      default = {                                               │ ║
  ║  │        dev     = "t3.micro"                                    │ ║
  ║  │        staging = "t3.small"                                    │ ║
  ║  │        prod    = "t3.medium"                                   │ ║
  ║  │      }                                                         │ ║
  ║  │    }                                                           │ ║
  ║  │    Access: var.instance_types["prod"] → "t3.medium"            │ ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║                      COMPLEX TYPES                                   ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ║
  ║  │ 7. object({...})         — STRUCTURED data with named fields   │ ║
  ║  │    variable "db_config" {                                      │ ║
  ║  │      type = object({                                           │ ║
  ║  │        engine         = string                                 │ ║
  ║  │        version        = string                                 │ ║
  ║  │        instance_class = string                                 │ ║
  ║  │        storage_gb     = number                                 │ ║
  ║  │        multi_az       = bool                                   │ ║
  ║  │      })                                                        │ ║
  ║  │    }                                                           │ ║
  ║  │    Access: var.db_config.engine → "postgres"                   │ ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ║
  ║  │ 8. any                   — FLEXIBLE: accepts any type          │ ║
  ║  │    variable "misc" { type = any }                              │ ║
  ║  │    ⚠️  Avoid in production — defeats type safety               │ ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
🧠 Type Conversion: What Terraform Accepts

Terraform automatically converts types when possible: "8000" can become 8000 if the target is number. A single value becomes a list automatically: "us-east-1a" → ["us-east-1a"]. But "true" does NOT become true — booleans are strict. Exam tip: if you see "true" assigned to a bool variable, that's a type error.

anihpj/jobpost: Complex Variable for Docker + K8s Config
variable "jobpost_config" {
  description = "Complete configuration for jobpost Django application"
  type = object({
    docker = object({
      image_tag    = string
      registry_url = string
    })
    kubernetes = object({
      replicas        = number
      cpu_request     = string
      memory_request  = string
      cpu_limit       = string
      memory_limit    = string
    })
    database = object({
      engine         = string
      version        = string
      instance_class = string
      storage_gb     = number
      backup_retention_days = number
    })
  })
  default = {
    docker = {
      image_tag    = "latest"
      registry_url = "123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com"
    }
    kubernetes = {
      replicas       = 3
      cpu_request    = "250m"
      memory_request = "256Mi"
      cpu_limit      = "500m"
      memory_limit   = "512Mi"
    }
    database = {
      engine         = "postgres"
      version        = "16.1"
      instance_class = "db.t3.medium"
      storage_gb     = 20
      backup_retention_days = 7
    }
  }
}

# Usage in resources:
resource "kubernetes_deployment" "jobpost" {
  spec {
    replicas = var.jobpost_config.kubernetes.replicas
    template {
      spec {
        container {
          image = "${var.jobpost_config.docker.registry_url}:${var.jobpost_config.docker.image_tag}"
          resources {
            requests = {
              cpu    = var.jobpost_config.kubernetes.cpu_request
              memory = var.jobpost_config.kubernetes.memory_request
            }
          }
        }
      }
    }
  }
}

9.3 Variable Assignment Methods — 6 Ways to Feed Values In

Terraform gives you six different ways to assign values to variables. Each has a specific priority level and use case. Understanding when to use each method is critical for both the exam and real-world CI/CD pipelines.

THE 6 VARIABLE ASSIGNMENT METHODS — Priority from Highest to Lowest
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              VARIABLE ASSIGNMENT PRECEDENCE HIERARCHY               │
  │              (🥇 = highest priority, overrides everything below)    │
  │                                                                     │
  │  🥇 METHOD 1: CLI -var FLAG                                        │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ $ terraform apply -var="environment=prod"                     │ │
  │  │ $ terraform apply -var='ports=[80,443]'                       │ │
  │  │ ⚡ Nuclear option — overrides EVERYTHING. Use for emergencies. │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                      │
  │  🥇 METHOD 2: CLI -var-file FLAG                                   │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ $ terraform apply -var-file="prod.tfvars"                     │ │
  │  │ Same priority as -var. If both set, last one wins.            │ │
  │  │ Use for: CI/CD pipelines, one-off manual deployments.         │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                      │
  │  🥈 METHOD 3: *.auto.tfvars / *.auto.tfvars.json                   │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ $ ls *.auto.tfvars                                            │ │
  │  │ production.auto.tfvars   staging.auto.tfvars                  │ │
  │  │ AUTO-LOADED — no flag needed. Any file matching *.auto.tfvars │ │
  │  │ ⚠️  EXAM GOTCHA: *.auto.tfvars BEATS terraform.tfvars!       │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                      │
  │  🥉 METHOD 4: terraform.tfvars / terraform.tfvars.json             │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ $ cat terraform.tfvars                                        │ │
  │  │ environment = "staging"                                       │ │
  │  │ AUTO-LOADED if present. The "default" variable file.          │ │
  │  │ Use for: Shared team defaults, checked into Git.              │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                      │
  │  METHOD 5: TF_VAR_* ENVIRONMENT VARIABLES                           │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ $ export TF_VAR_environment=prod                              │ │
  │  │ $ export TF_VAR_db_password=correcthorsebatterystaple          │ │
  │  │ Terraform reads ANY env var prefixed with TF_VAR_.            │ │
  │  │ Use for: CI/CD secret injection, Docker containers.           │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                      │
  │  METHOD 6: VARIABLE DEFAULT VALUE                                   │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ variable "environment" { default = "dev" }                    │ │
  │  │ 📌 Last resort — only used if nothing else provides a value.  │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
MethodPriorityAuto-Loaded?Best ForExam Note
-var🥇 HighestNoEmergencies, one-offsOverrides everything
-var-file🥇 HighestNoCI/CD, manual runsSame level as -var
*.auto.tfvars🥈 High✅ YesEnvironment configsBEATS terraform.tfvars!
terraform.tfvars🥉 Medium✅ YesShared defaultsStandard auto-load file
TF_VAR_*LowN/ACI/CD secretsPrefix is case-sensitive
Default value📌 LowestN/ADevelopment defaultsFallback only

9.4 Variable Validation — Gatekeeping Values Before They Break Things

Custom validation rules are Terraform's built-in guardrails. They check variable values before any API call is made, catching configuration errors at plan time instead of 20 minutes into an apply. Every validation block has a condition (must evaluate to true) and an error_message (shown when it fails).

CUSTOM VALIDATION — 5 Production-Ready Examples for anihpj/jobpost
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  1. ENUMERATED VALUES — Only allow specific strings                 ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  variable "environment" {                                           ║
  ║    type = string                                                    ║
  ║    validation {                                                     ║
  ║      condition     = contains(["dev","staging","prod"],             ║
  ║                               var.environment)                      ║
  ║      error_message = "Environment must be dev, staging, or prod."   ║
  ║    }                                                                ║
  ║  }                                                                  ║
  ║  $ terraform plan -var="environment=production"                     ║
  ║  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗    ║
  ║  ║ Error: Invalid value for variable                          ║    ║
  ║  ║ Environment must be dev, staging, or prod.                 ║    ║
  ║  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝    ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  2. LENGTH CONSTRAINTS — String/number min/max                      ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  variable "db_name" {                                               ║
  ║    type = string                                                    ║
  ║    validation {                                                     ║
  ║      condition     = length(var.db_name) >= 3 &&                   ║
  ║                      length(var.db_name) <= 63                     ║
  ║      error_message = "DB name must be 3-63 characters."            ║
  ║    }                                                                ║
  ║  }                                                                  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  3. REGEX PATTERNS — Validate format                                ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  variable "domain_name" {                                           ║
  ║    type = string                                                    ║
  ║    validation {                                                     ║
  ║      condition     = can(regex("^[a-z0-9.-]+\\.[a-z]{2,}$",        ║
  ║                                 var.domain_name))                   ║
  ║      error_message = "Domain must be a valid FQDN like anihpj.com." ║
  ║    }                                                                ║
  ║  }                                                                  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  4. NUMERIC RANGES — Min/max values                                 ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  variable "k8s_replicas" {                                          ║
  ║    type = number                                                    ║
  ║    validation {                                                     ║
  ║      condition     = var.k8s_replicas >= 1 &&                      ║
  ║                      var.k8s_replicas <= 10                        ║
  ║      error_message = "K8s replicas must be between 1 and 10."      ║
  ║    }                                                                ║
  ║  }                                                                  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  5. CROSS-FIELD VALIDATION — One field depends on another           ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  variable "storage_config" {                                        ║
  ║    type = object({                                                  ║
  ║      storage_gb       = number                                     ║
  ║      backup_retention = number                                     ║
  ║    })                                                               ║
  ║    validation {                                                     ║
  ║      condition     = var.storage_config.backup_retention > 0 ?     ║
  ║                      var.storage_config.storage_gb >= 20 : true    ║
  ║      error_message = "Backups require at least 20GB storage."      ║
  ║    }                                                                ║
  ║  }                                                                  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
🧠 Validation Rules — What the Exam Tests

1. condition must evaluate to bool. If it returns a string or number, it's a type error.

2. Multiple validations are allowed. One variable can have many validation{} blocks — each is checked independently.

3. Validation runs at plan time. This means NO API calls, NO cost, NO risk. Pure syntax/semantic check.

4. error_message is required. If you forget it, Terraform won't even parse the file.

5. can() is your friend. Wrap regex or other functions in can() to gracefully handle errors instead of crashing.

9.5 Sensitive Variables — Protecting Secrets in Terraform

Setting sensitive = true on a variable hides its value from console output during terraform plan and terraform apply. But here's the critical exam fact: sensitive does NOT encrypt the value in state. The value is still stored as plaintext in terraform.tfstate. You need defense in depth.

THE SENSITIVE VARIABLE LIFECYCLE — Where Secrets Travel
  variable "db_password" {
    type      = string
    sensitive = true
    description = "RDS master password"
  }

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    🔐 SECRET FLOW THROUGH TERRAFORM              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🔐 SOURCE                    📍 INTERMEDIATE                     │
  │  ┌──────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────┐            │
  │  │ Vault / AWS  │───fetch───▶│ TF_VAR_db_password   │            │
  │  │ Secrets Mgr  │            │ (env var in CI/CD)    │            │
  │  │ / SSM Param  │            └──────────┬───────────┘            │
  │  └──────────────┘                       │                        │
  │                                         ▼                        │
  │  🔒 CONSOLE OUTPUT                ⚠️  STATE FILE                 │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────┐       │
  │  │ terraform plan   │            │ terraform.tfstate    │       │
  │  │   + db_password  │            │ "db_password":       │       │
  │  │     = (sensitive │◀───────────│  "correcthorsebat..." │       │
  │  │       value)     │            │ ⚠️  PLAIN TEXT!       │       │
  │  │ ✅ REDACTED      │            └──────────────────────┘       │
  │  └──────────────────┘                                           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  🛡️ PROTECTION STACK (defense in depth):                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. sensitive = true       → Hides from console output      │ │
  │  │ 2. Remote state encryption → S3 SSE-KMS / Azure Storage    │ │
  │  │ 3. TF_VAR_* in CI/CD      → GitHub Secrets / GitLab CI var │ │
  │  │ 4. Vault dynamic secrets  → Auto-rotate, short-lived TTL  │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
⚠️ What sensitive = true does NOT do

❌ Does NOT encrypt the state file

❌ Does NOT prevent the value from being stored in state

❌ Does NOT encrypt values in transit

❌ Does NOT prevent the value from appearing in error messages (sometimes)

❌ Does NOT encrypt .tfvars files

✅ What sensitive = true DOES do

✅ Hides value from terraform plan output

✅ Hides value from terraform apply output

✅ Hides value from terraform output (if output is also sensitive)

✅ Prevents the value from appearing in logs

✅ Marks the variable as sensitive in auto-generated docs

⚠️ EXAM HOTSPOT: sensitive does NOT encrypt state

This is one of the most common trick questions on the exam. They'll say "setting sensitive=true encrypts the variable in the state file" — this is FALSE. The state file stores ALL values in plaintext regardless of sensitivity. You must use remote state with encryption (S3 SSE-KMS, Azure Storage encryption) to protect state at rest. sensitive=true only controls what's displayed to humans, not what's stored.

9.6 Variable Definition Precedence — Who Wins When Values Conflict?

When the same variable is set in multiple places, Terraform uses a strict priority system to resolve the conflict. This is the single most-tested topic in Domain 4. Memorize this hierarchy cold.

VARIABLE PRECEDENCE HIERARCHY — The Definitive Diagram (Memorize This!)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │     🥇 LEVEL 1: CLI -var / -var-file                                 │
  │     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │     │ $ terraform apply -var="env=prod"                          │  │
  │     │ ☢️  NUCLEAR OPTION — Overrides EVERYTHING below            │  │
  │     │ If both -var and -var-file set, the LAST one wins          │  │
  │     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                              │                                       │
  │                              ▼ OVERRIDES                             │
  │     🥈 LEVEL 2: *.auto.tfvars & *.auto.tfvars.json                  │
  │     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │     │ $ cat staging.auto.tfvars                                  │  │
  │     │ environment = "staging"                                    │  │
  │     │ ✅ Auto-loaded — no flag needed                            │  │
  │     │ ✅ Multiple *.auto.tfvars files are all loaded (alphabet.)  │  │
  │     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                              │                                       │
  │                              ▼ OVERRIDES                             │
  │     🥉 LEVEL 3: terraform.tfvars & terraform.tfvars.json            │
  │     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │     │ $ cat terraform.tfvars                                     │  │
  │     │ environment = "development"                                │  │
  │     │ ✅ Auto-loaded if file exists                              │  │
  │     │ ⚠️  GETS OVERRIDDEN by *.auto.tfvars!                     │  │
  │     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                              │                                       │
  │                              ▼ OVERRIDES                             │
  │     LEVEL 4: TF_VAR_* Environment Variables                         │
  │     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │     │ $ export TF_VAR_environment=staging                        │  │
  │     │ $ export TF_VAR_db_password=secret123                      │  │
  │     │ ⚠️  NOT auto-loaded files — read from shell env            │  │
  │     │ ⚠️  Prefix TF_VAR_ is CASE-SENSITIVE                      │  │
  │     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                              │                                       │
  │                              ▼ OVERRIDES                             │
  │     LEVEL 5: Variable Default Value                                  │
  │     ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │     │ variable "environment" { default = "dev" }                 │  │
  │     │ 📌 Last resort — only used if NOTHING else provides value  │  │
  │     └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🧠 EXAM MNEMONIC: "Cats Always Try Fish"

CLI -var → Auto.tfvars → Terraform.tfvars → F_VAR_* env → Default. Or remember: "Command line Always Takes First place" — the CLI is always the highest priority. Every lower level only applies if no higher level has set the variable.

9.7 Complex Variable Patterns — Maps of Objects & Lists of Maps

As your infrastructure grows, simple variables aren't enough. You need structured data — maps of objects that define entire environment configurations in one variable. This is the pattern used by every production Terraform codebase.

COMPLEX VARIABLE PATTERN — Maps of Objects for anihpj/jobpost
  variable "environments" {
    description = "Per-environment configuration for anihpj/jobpost"
    type = map(object({
      instance_type   = string
      min_size        = number
      max_size        = number
      enable_monitoring = bool
      k8s_namespace   = string
      db_instance     = string
      db_storage      = number
    }))
    default = {
      dev = {
        instance_type     = "t3.micro"
        min_size          = 1
        max_size          = 2
        enable_monitoring = false
        k8s_namespace     = "anihpj-dev"
        db_instance       = "db.t3.micro"
        db_storage        = 20
      }
      staging = {
        instance_type     = "t3.small"
        min_size          = 2
        max_size          = 4
        enable_monitoring = true
        k8s_namespace     = "anihpj-staging"
        db_instance       = "db.t3.small"
        db_storage        = 50
      }
      prod = {
        instance_type     = "t3.medium"
        min_size          = 3
        max_size          = 10
        enable_monitoring = true
        k8s_namespace     = "anihpj-prod"
        db_instance       = "db.t3.medium"
        db_storage        = 100
      }
    }
  }

  # USAGE: ONE resource block, ALL environments — iterate with for_each
  resource "kubernetes_namespace" "anihpj" {
    for_each = var.environments
    metadata { name = each.value.k8s_namespace }
  }

  # Access specific env: var.environments["prod"].db_storage → 100
🗺️ Map of Objects

Best for: Environment-specific configs. Each key (dev/staging/prod) maps to a complete object.

Access: var.config["dev"].size

Iterate with: for_each

📋 List of Objects

Best for: Ordered collections. Each item is a complete object at a numeric index.

Access: var.config[0].size

Iterate with: count

🧩 List of Maps

Best for: Simpler ordered collections where objects aren't needed.

Access: var.config[0]["key"]

Iterate with: count or for

9.8 Environment-Specific Variables — Workspace-Based Selection

Terraform workspaces let you use the same code to deploy to different environments. The terraform.workspace variable tells you which workspace is active, and you can use it with lookup() or try() to select environment-specific values.

Workspace-Based Environment Selection for anihpj/jobpost
variable "instance_config" {
  type = map(object({
    size  = string
    count = number
  }))
  default = {
    dev     = { size = "t3.micro",  count = 1 }
    staging = { size = "t3.small",  count = 2 }
    prod    = { size = "t3.medium", count = 3 }
  }
}

locals {
  config = lookup(var.instance_config, terraform.workspace,
                   var.instance_config["dev"])  # dev is fallback
}

resource "aws_instance" "anihpj" {
  count         = local.config.count
  instance_type = local.config.size
  tags = {
    Name        = "anihpj-${terraform.workspace}"
    Environment = terraform.workspace
  }
}

# $ terraform workspace select dev    → 1× t3.micro
# $ terraform workspace select prod   → 3× t3.medium
# SAME code → DIFFERENT infrastructure!
🧠 terraform.workspace — Key Facts

Default workspace = "default". Always exists, can't be deleted.

Available everywhere in HCL. Use it in locals, resources, outputs — anywhere.

Not suitable for strong isolation. Workspaces share the same backend and credentials. For hard isolation, use separate backends (different S3 buckets) or separate directories.

Works with Terraform Cloud workspaces too. TFC workspaces map to CLI workspaces seamlessly.

📝 Chapter 9 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions

Q1
What is the HIGHEST priority method for setting a variable value in Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

CLI -var and -var-file flags (tied for highest priority).

Explanation

The CLI flags -var and -var-file override ALL other variable assignment methods. The full precedence order (highest to lowest) is:

CLI -var/-var-file → *.auto.tfvars → terraform.tfvars → TF_VAR_* env vars → default

Between -var and -var-file, if both are used for the same variable, the one specified LAST wins.

Q2
You set sensitive = true on a variable. Which of the following is TRUE?
Show Answer
Answer

The value is hidden from terraform plan and terraform apply console output, but is still stored in plaintext in the state file.

Explanation

sensitive = true only controls what's displayed — it does NOT encrypt the state file. To protect secrets at rest:

1. Use remote state with encryption (S3 + SSE-KMS)
2. Store secrets in Vault / AWS Secrets Manager
3. Use TF_VAR_* env vars from CI/CD secret stores
4. NEVER commit .tfvars with secrets to Git
Q3
You have terraform.tfvars AND production.auto.tfvars in the same directory. Both set environment. Which value wins?
Show Answer
Answer

production.auto.tfvars wins because *.auto.tfvars has HIGHER priority than terraform.tfvars.

Explanation

This is one of the most commonly tested precedence scenarios:

*.auto.tfvars  (LEVEL 2) — AUTO-LOADED, HIGHER PRIORITY
      │
      ▼ OVERRIDES
terraform.tfvars (LEVEL 3) — AUTO-LOADED, LOWER PRIORITY

Both files are auto-loaded, but *.auto.tfvars always beats
terraform.tfvars when there's a conflict.
Q4
Which variable type would you use to ensure each value is unique and order doesn't matter?
Show Answer
Answer

set(<type>) — an unordered collection of unique values.

Explanation
TypeOrdered?Unique?Access
list✅❌index [0]
set❌✅for_each only
map❌✅ (keys)key ["name"]

Use set(string) for allowed ports or availability zones where duplicates are meaningless.

Q5
A variable has NO default value and NO value is provided through any method. What happens when you run terraform plan?
Show Answer
Answer

Terraform prompts interactively for the value. In non-interactive mode (CI/CD), it fails with an error.

Explanation

Variables without defaults are required inputs:

INTERACTIVE TERMINAL:  Prompts user: "var.name: Enter a value"
NON-INTERACTIVE (CI):  Error: No value for required variable

SOLUTION: Always provide a default OR pass via -var,
-var-file, *.auto.tfvars, terraform.tfvars, or TF_VAR_* env var.
Q6
You need a variable that accepts either a string, a number, or a list. Which type should you use?
Show Answer
Answer

any — accepts any type. Use sparingly as it defeats type safety.

Explanation

type = any is flexible but dangerous. The exam may ask: "any" is a valid type → TRUE. But best practice is to use specific types.

Q7
What is the purpose of the validation block inside a variable block?
Show Answer
Answer

To enforce custom rules on variable values BEFORE any infrastructure API call is made. Validation runs at plan time.

Explanation
variable "environment" {
  validation {
    condition     = contains(["dev","staging","prod"], var.environment)
    error_message = "Environment must be dev, staging, or prod."
  }
}

$ terraform plan -var="environment=production"
╔═══════════════════════════════════════════════╗
║ Error: Invalid value for variable            ║
║ Environment must be dev, staging, or prod.   ║
╚═══════════════════════════════════════════════╝
✅ Caught BEFORE any AWS API call!
Q8
How do you reference an environment variable named TF_VAR_region inside your HCL code?
Show Answer
Answer

As var.region. Terraform strips the TF_VAR_ prefix and maps env vars to variables automatically.

Explanation
# Shell: export TF_VAR_region=us-east-1
# HCL:  variable "region" { type = string }
# Usage: var.region  → "us-east-1" (NOT "TF_VAR_region")
Q9
Which variable file names will be auto-loaded by Terraform? (Select TWO)
Show Answer
Answer

terraform.tfvars and staging.auto.tfvars are auto-loaded. custom.tfvars and vars.tf are NOT.

Explanation
✅ terraform.tfvars         ✅ *.auto.tfvars
✅ terraform.tfvars.json    ✅ *.auto.tfvars.json
❌ custom.tfvars (needs -var-file flag)
❌ vars.tf (config file, not variable file)
Q10
You have a variable of type object({name: string, ports: list(number)}). How do you access the second port?
Show Answer
Answer

var.config.ports[1] — dot notation for object fields, bracket notation for list index (0-based).

Explanation
var.config.name      → "anihpj"     (dot notation)
var.config.ports[0]  → 80           (bracket, 0-based)
var.config.ports[1]  → 443          (second element)
