Chapter 10: Expressions, Functions & Conditionals 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 10: Expressions, Functions & Conditionals — HCL's Power Tools

Variables store data. Expressions and functions transform it. This is where HCL stops being a configuration format and starts being a programming language. You'll learn to use conditional logic (? :), list/map transformations (for expressions), wildcard operations ([*] splat), 100+ built-in functions, dynamic blocks, and error handling — all to make your infrastructure code smarter, shorter, and more maintainable for anihpj/jobpost.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 10 Sections 🎯 Domain 4
🎓 TF Associate Relevance
Domain 4: Variables & Outputs ~15%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Master string interpolation — "${var.name}" and template directives %{if}
  • ✅ Use arithmetic (+ - * / %) and logical (&& || !) operators
  • ✅ Write conditional expressions — condition ? true_val : false_val
  • ✅ Transform lists and maps with for expressions — filtering, mapping, grouping
  • ✅ Apply splat expressions [*] to extract attributes from resource lists

10.1 String Interpolation — "${var.name}" and Template Syntax

String interpolation embeds variable values directly into strings. The syntax "${...}" evaluates any expression and converts the result to a string. For multi-line templates with conditionals and loops, use the template directive syntax %{...}.

STRING INTERPOLATION — All 4 Patterns with Examples
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  PATTERN 1: Simple interpolation                                    ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  "Hello, ${var.username}"               → "Hello, admin"            ║
  ║  "ami-${var.ami_id}"                    → "ami-0a1b2c3d"            ║
  ║  "${var.region}-${var.environment}"      → "us-east-1-prod"          ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  PATTERN 2: Template directives (%{...}) — Multi-line with logic    ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  user_data = <<-EOT                                                   ║
  ║  #!/bin/bash                                                         ║
  ║  echo "Deploying anihpj ${var.version}"                              ║
  ║  %{ if var.environment == "prod" }                                   ║
  ║  echo "WARNING: Production environment!"                             ║
  ║  certbot --nginx -d ${var.domain}                                    ║
  ║  %{ else }                                                           ║
  ║  echo "Non-production deployment"                                    ║
  ║  %{ endif }                                                          ║
  ║  EOT                                                                 ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  PATTERN 3: Template for loop (%{ for ... in ... })                 ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  config = <<-EOT                                                      ║
  ║  %{ for mount in var.efs_mounts }                                    ║
  ║  mount -t efs ${mount.id}:/ ${mount.path}                            ║
  ║  %{ endfor }                                                         ║
  ║  EOT                                                                 ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  PATTERN 4: STRIP MARKER (~) — Remove whitespace before/after       ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  %{~ if condition ~}    ← Strip whitespace around the directive     ║
  ║  content                ← Only this line appears in output           ║
  ║  %{~ endif ~}           ← No extra blank lines in output             ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

10.2 Arithmetic & Logical Operators — Math and Boolean Logic

Terraform supports standard arithmetic and comparison operators. These are essential for computed values, conditional logic, and resource sizing calculations for anihpj/jobpost.

🔢 Arithmetic Operators
a + bAddition
a - bSubtraction
a * bMultiplication
a / bDivision (float result)
a % bModulus (remainder)
-aNegation
🔀 Comparison & Logical Operators
a == b / !=Equal / Not equal
a > b / <Greater / Less than
a >= b / <=Greater-or-equal / Less-or-equal
a && bLogical AND
a || bLogical OR
!aLogical NOT
anihpj/jobpost: Practical Operator Examples
# Compute K8s resource limits based on environment tier
locals {
  cpu_limit = var.environment == "prod" ? "1000m" : "500m"
  memory_multiplier = var.environment == "prod" ? 2 : 1
  total_memory = 256 * local.memory_multiplier  # 256 or 512 Mi

  # Multi-condition: backups needed for non-dev WITH flag enabled
  needs_backup = var.environment != "dev" && var.enable_backups
}

# Validation with logical operators
validation {
  condition     = var.replicas >= 1 && var.replicas <= 10
  error_message = "Replicas must be between 1 and 10 (inclusive)."
}

10.3 Conditional Expressions — Making Decisions in HCL

The conditional expression condition ? true_value : false_value is Terraform's if/else. It evaluates the condition as a boolean and returns one of two values. This is used everywhere: resource sizing, feature toggles, count decisions, and environment-specific configurations.

CONDITIONAL EXPRESSIONS — 5 Production-Ready Patterns
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  1. RESOURCE SIZING — Different sizes per environment               ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  instance_type = var.environment == "prod" ? "t3.medium" : "t3.micro"║
  ║  #              └───── condition ──────┘  └── true ──┘  └── false ──┘║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  2. CONDITIONAL RESOURCE CREATION — count = 1 or 0                  ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  resource "aws_cloudwatch_dashboard" "anihpj" {                      ║
  ║    count = var.environment == "prod" ? 1 : 0                         ║
  ║    dashboard_name = "anihpj-${terraform.workspace}"                  ║
  ║  }                                                                   ║
  ║  # count=0 means: do NOT create this resource at all                ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  3. FEATURE TOGGLE — Enable/disable features                        ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  backup_retention_period = var.enable_backups ? 30 : 0               ║
  ║  deletion_protection     = var.environment == "prod" ? true : false  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  4. DEFAULT VALUE WITH FALLBACK                                     ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  ami = var.custom_ami != "" ? var.custom_ami : data.aws_ami.latest.id║
  ║  #    └── condition ──┘    └── true ──┘   └── false/default ──┘     ║
  ║  💡 A cleaner alternative: coalesce(var.custom_ami, data.aws_ami...) ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  5. NESTED CONDITIONALS — Multi-way branching                        ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  instance_type = (                                                   ║
  ║    var.environment == "prod"    ? "t3.large"  :                      ║
  ║    var.environment == "staging" ? "t3.medium" :                      ║
  ║    "t3.micro"                     # default (dev)                    ║
  ║  )                                                                   ║
  ║  ⚠️  Better approach: use lookup() with a map for 3+ branches!      ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

10.4 For Expressions — Transforming Lists & Maps

for expressions iterate over collections and produce new collections. They're the functional programming backbone of HCL — used for filtering, mapping, grouping, and transforming data in a single expression.

FOR EXPRESSIONS — The Complete Reference Card
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  PATTERN 1: LIST → LIST (transform with :)                          ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  [for env in ["dev", "staging", "prod"] : upper(env)]               ║
  ║  → ["DEV", "STAGING", "PROD"]                                       ║
  ║  Syntax: [ for  in  :  ]                   ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  PATTERN 2: LIST → LIST (filter with if)                             ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  [for cidr in var.subnets : cidr if length(cidr) > 0]              ║
  ║  Syntax: [ for  in  :  if  ]         ║
  ║  # Only items satisfying the condition are included                  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  PATTERN 3: MAP → MAP (transform with =>)                            ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  {for env, size in var.instance_types : env => upper(size)}         ║
  ║  Input:  {dev = "t3.micro", prod = "t3.medium"}                     ║
  ║  Output: {dev = "T3.MICRO", prod = "T3.MEDIUM"}                     ║
  ║  Syntax: { for , in  :  =>  }   ║
  ║  💡 {} outputs a MAP; [] outputs a LIST                              ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
anihpj/jobpost: Real-World For Expressions
# Get all private IPs of running EC2 instances
output "private_ips" {
  value = [for instance in aws_instance.anihpj : instance.private_ip]
}

# Create namespace → pod-count map from K8s namespace labels
locals {
  ns_pod_map = {
    for ns in kubernetes_namespace.anihpj :
    ns.metadata[0].name => ns.metadata[0].labels["pod-count"]
  }
}

# Filter to only production subnets
locals {
  prod_subnets = [
    for subnet in data.aws_subnets.all.ids :
    subnet if can(regex("prod", subnet))
  ]
}

10.5 Splat Expressions [*] — Extracting Attributes from Resource Lists

The splat expression [*] is a shorthand for extracting a single attribute from every element of a list or map. It's equivalent to a for expression but more concise.

SPLAT [*] vs FOR — Two Ways to Extract Attributes
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  THE SPLAT WAY (shorter, preferred for simple extraction):           ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  aws_instance.web[*].id          → ["i-abc", "i-def", "i-ghi"]     ║
  ║  aws_instance.web[*].public_ip   → ["54.1.2.3", "54.4.5.6"]        ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  # Works on both:                                                    ║
  ║  # count-based resources: aws_instance.web[*].id                     ║
  ║  # for_each-based resources: aws_instance.web[*].id (same syntax!)  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  THE FOR WAY (longer, for complex transformations):                  ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  [for inst in aws_instance.web : inst.id]    ← Same as splat [*]   ║
  ║  [for inst in aws_instance.web : "${inst.id}:${inst.public_ip}"]   ║
  ║  # For expressions are better when you need to combine attributes   ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  EXAM NOTE: Legacy .* syntax (pre-0.12)                              ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  aws_instance.web.*.id    ← Legacy: only works on count resources   ║
  ║  aws_instance.web[*].id   ← Modern: works on count AND for_each    ║
  ║  ✅ Always use [*] in new code. The exam may still reference .*     ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

10.6 Built-in Functions — The Terraform Function Library

Terraform ships with 100+ built-in functions organized into categories. You don't import them — they're available globally in any expression. The exam tests your knowledge of the most commonly used ones.

TERRAFORM BUILT-IN FUNCTIONS — Quick Reference by Category
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  STRING FUNCTIONS                                                   ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  upper("hello")        → "HELLO"       lower("WORLD")   → "world"  ║
  ║  join(", ", ["a","b"]) → "a, b"        split(",", "a,b") → ["a","b"]║
  ║  replace("ab", "b","c")→ "ac"          trimspace(" x ")  → "x"     ║
  ║  substr("hello", 0, 2) → "he"          format("%s-%d","v",1)→"v-1" ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  NUMERIC FUNCTIONS                                                  ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  max(1,5,3) → 5               min(1,5,3) → 1                       ║
  ║  abs(-5) → 5                  ceil(3.1) → 4     floor(3.9) → 3     ║
  ║  log(16, 2) → 4              pow(2, 3) → 8                         ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  COLLECTION FUNCTIONS                                               ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  length(["a","b"]) → 2       length({a=1,b=2}) → 2                 ║
  ║  contains(["a","b"], "a") → true                                   ║
  ║  merge({a=1},{b=2}) → {a=1, b=2}                                   ║
  ║  keys({a=1,b=2}) → ["a","b"]   values({a=1,b=2}) → [1,2]           ║
  ║  flatten([[1],[2,3]]) → [1,2,3]                                     ║
  ║  distinct([1,2,1,3,2]) → [1,2,3]                                    ║
  ║  toset([1,2,1]) → [1,2]  (converts to set, removes dups)           ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  LOOKUP FUNCTIONS (Exam Favorites!)                                 ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  lookup({a=1}, "a", 99)   → 1    (key exists → return value)       ║
  ║  lookup({a=1}, "b", 99)   → 99   (key missing → return default)    ║
  ║  element(["a","b","c"], 1)→ "b"  (0-based index, wraps if negative)║
  ║  index(["a","b","c"], "b")→ 1    (find position of value)          ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  FILESYSTEM FUNCTIONS                                               ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  file("script.sh") →           (read file as string)      ║
  ║  templatefile("tpl.tftpl", {vars...})    (render template)         ║
  ║  filebase64("img.png") →        (read as base64)          ║
  ║  abspath("main.tf") → /abs/path/main.tf                            ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  IP & CIDR FUNCTIONS                                                ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  cidrsubnet("10.0.0.0/16", 8, 1) → "10.0.1.0/24"                   ║
  ║  cidrhost("10.0.1.0/24", 5) → "10.0.1.5"                           ║
  ║  cidrnetmask("10.0.0.0/16") → "255.255.0.0"                        ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
anihpj/jobpost: Functions in Action
# Generate S3 bucket name (must be globally unique)
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "assets" {
  bucket = "anihpj-${var.environment}-assets-${random_id.suffix.hex}"
  # Uses: join() implicitly, random_id resource
}

# CIDR math for subnet allocation
locals {
  vpc_cidr = "10.0.0.0/16"
  public_subnets  = [for i in range(3) : cidrsubnet(local.vpc_cidr, 8, i)]
  private_subnets = [for i in range(3) : cidrsubnet(local.vpc_cidr, 8, i + 3)]
  # → ["10.0.0.0/24", "10.0.1.0/24", "10.0.2.0/24"]  (public)
  # → ["10.0.3.0/24", "10.0.4.0/24", "10.0.5.0/24"]  (private)
}

# Template rendering for user_data
user_data = templatefile("${path.module}/user_data.sh.tftpl", {
  environment = var.environment
  db_host     = aws_db_instance.anihpj.address
  docker_tag  = var.jobpost_config.docker.image_tag
})
TEMPLATEFILE() — Dynamic Configuration Generation (Exam Favorite!)
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  THE TEMPLATE FILE: user_data.sh.tftpl                              ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  #!/bin/bash                                                       ║
  ║  echo "Deploying ${app_name} in ${environment}"                    ║
  ║  echo "Database host: ${db_host}"                                   ║
  ║  echo "Redis host: ${redis_host}"                                   ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  %{ if enable_ssl }                                                 ║
  ║  echo "Configuring SSL..."                                          ║
  ║  certbot --nginx -d ${domain}                                       ║
  ║  %{ endif }                                                         ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  %{ for mount_point in efs_mounts }                                 ║
  ║  mount -t efs ${mount_point.id}:/ ${mount_point.path}               ║
  ║  %{ endfor }                                                        ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  systemctl start app.service                                        ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  main.tf — RENDERS the template with REAL values:                   ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  user_data = templatefile("${path.module}/user_data.sh.tftpl", {    ║
  ║    app_name    = "anihpj"                                           ║
  ║    environment = var.environment                                    ║
  ║    db_host     = aws_db_instance.pg.address                         ║
  ║    redis_host  = aws_elasticache_cluster.redis.address              ║
  ║    enable_ssl  = var.environment == "prod"                          ║
  ║    domain      = "anihpj.com"                                       ║
  ║    efs_mounts  = var.efs_volumes                                    ║
  ║  })                                                                 ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  RESULT: A fully-configured user_data script with:                  ║
  ║  ✅ Real DB/Redis endpoints injected                                ║
  ║  ✅ SSL config ONLY for prod (conditional %{ if })                  ║
  ║  ✅ EFS mounts generated from list (loop %{ for })                  ║
  ║  ✅ Separation of logic (template) from values (.tf file)           ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  💡 templatefile() is THE function for dynamic configuration.
  It reads a .tftpl file, substitutes ${variables}, and processes
  %{ if }/%{ for } directives. The result is a rendered string —
  perfect for user_data, IAM policies, or any multi-line config.
CIDRSUBNET() — Never Manually Calculate Subnets Again
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ variable "vpc_cidr" { default = "10.0.0.0/16" }                     │
  │ variable "azs" {                                                    │
  │   default = ["us-east-1a", "us-east-1b", "us-east-1c"]             │
  │ }                                                                   │
  │                                                                     │
  │ # ONE LINE generates ALL subnet CIDRs dynamically:                  │
  │ resource "aws_subnet" "public" {                                    │
  │   count             = length(var.azs)                               │
  │   vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id                               │
  │   availability_zone = var.azs[count.index]                          │
  │   cidr_block        = cidrsubnet(var.vpc_cidr, 8, count.index)     │
  │   ▲                  ▲           ▲    ▲       ▲                      │
  │   │                  │           │    │       └── netnum (0,1,2)    │
  │   │                  │           │    └── newbits (8 = /24 subnets) │
  │   │                  │           └── base CIDR (/16 VPC)            │
  │   │                  └── Terraform built-in function                │
  │   └── Result: 10.0.0.0/24, 10.0.1.0/24, 10.0.2.0/24               │
  │ }                                                                   │
  │                                                                     │
  │  💡 cidrsubnet("10.0.0.0/16", 8, 0) = "10.0.0.0/24"                │
  │  💡 cidrsubnet("10.0.0.0/16", 8, 1) = "10.0.1.0/24"                │
  │  💡 cidrsubnet("10.0.0.0/16", 8, 255) = "10.0.255.0/24"            │
  │                                                                     │
  │  ADD 5 MORE AZs? Just append to var.azs list — no IP math needed!  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

10.7 Dynamic Blocks — Generating Nested Blocks Programmatically

Dynamic blocks let you generate repeated nested blocks from a collection. Instead of copy-pasting the same block 5 times, you write one dynamic block that iterates over a list or map. This is essential for security group rules, IAM policy statements, and any resource with variable numbers of nested blocks.

DYNAMIC BLOCKS — From Hardcoded to Dynamic
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  WITHOUT dynamic blocks (HARDCODED — 3 rules, 15 lines):            ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  resource "aws_security_group" "web" {                               ║
  ║    name = "anihpj-web-sg"                                           ║
  ║    ingress { from_port = 80;  to_port = 80;  protocol = "tcp"      ║
  ║              cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"] }                          ║
  ║    ingress { from_port = 443; to_port = 443; protocol = "tcp"      ║
  ║              cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"] }                          ║
  ║    ingress { from_port = 8000;to_port = 8000;protocol = "tcp"      ║
  ║              cidr_blocks = ["10.0.0.0/8"] }                         ║
  ║  }                                                                   ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  WITH dynamic blocks (DYNAMIC — 3 rules, data-driven):              ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  variable "ingress_rules" {                                         ║
  ║    type = list(object({                                             ║
  ║      port        = number                                           ║
  ║      protocol    = string                                           ║
  ║      cidr_blocks = list(string)                                     ║
  ║    }))                                                              ║
  ║    default = [                                                      ║
  ║      { port = 80,   protocol = "tcp", cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]},║
  ║      { port = 443,  protocol = "tcp", cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]},║
  ║      { port = 8000, protocol = "tcp", cidr_blocks = ["10.0.0.0/8"]}║
  ║    ]                                                                ║
  ║  }                                                                   ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  resource "aws_security_group" "web" {                               ║
  ║    name = "anihpj-web-sg"                                           ║
  ║    dynamic "ingress" {                    ◀── Generate ingress blocks║
  ║      for_each = var.ingress_rules         ◀── Iterate over variable  ║
  ║      content {                             ◀── Block template        ║
  ║        from_port   = ingress.value.port                              ║
  ║        to_port     = ingress.value.port                              ║
  ║        protocol    = ingress.value.protocol                          ║
  ║        cidr_blocks = ingress.value.cidr_blocks                       ║
  ║      }                                                               ║
  ║    }                                                                 ║
  ║  }                                                                   ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  💡 "ingress" (the block name) becomes the iterator variable name    ║
  ║  💡 ingress.value gives you the current element from for_each        ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
🧠 Dynamic Block Rules (Exam-Relevant)

1. iterator name = block label. dynamic "ingress" creates the iterator ingress. Use ingress.value to access the current item.

2. content block is the template. Everything inside content {} is repeated for each element in for_each.

3. Can't use count. Dynamic blocks use for_each only — no count support.

4. Can access iterator.key. If iterating over a map, block_name.key gives you the map key.

5. Nested dynamic blocks are possible. You can have dynamic blocks inside dynamic blocks (but be careful with complexity).

10.8 Try & Can — Graceful Error Handling

Not all expressions succeed. When you're working with optional data, uncertain inputs, or complex operations, try() and can() let you handle failures gracefully instead of crashing the entire plan.

🛡️ try() — Evaluate expressions, return first success

try(val1, val2, val3) evaluates each argument in order and returns the first one that doesn't produce an error.

try(var.prod_config, var.staging_config, var.dev_config)

If var.prod_config is null or errors, it tries var.staging_config, then var.dev_config. If all fail, try() itself errors.

✅ can() — Test if an expression succeeds

can(expression) returns true if the expression evaluates without error, false otherwise.

can(regex("^[a-z]+$", var.name))

Returns true if the regex matches. Perfect for validation blocks — can(regex(...)) is much safer than raw regex().

anihpj/jobpost: try() and can() Examples
# try() for environment-specific config with fallback
locals {
  db_config = try(
    var.environment_configs[terraform.workspace].database,  # Try specific
    var.environment_configs["dev"].database                 # Fallback
  )
}

# can() in validation — safe regex check
variable "domain_name" {
  type = string
  validation {
    condition     = can(regex("^[a-z0-9.-]+\\.[a-z]{2,}$", var.domain_name))
    error_message = "Domain must be a valid FQDN (e.g., anihpj.com)."
  }
}

# can() for conditional resource creation
resource "aws_route53_zone" "anihpj" {
  count = can(var.domain_name) ? 1 : 0
  name  = var.domain_name
}

10.9 Coalesce — First Non-Empty Value

coalesce() returns the first argument that isn't null or empty. It's like try() but simpler — it only checks for empty/null, not for evaluation errors. Use it for providing fallback values in a clean, readable way.

coalesce() vs try() vs lookup() — When to Use Which
# coalesce(): First non-null, non-empty
coalesce("", "hello", "world")  → "hello"     ("" is empty → skip)
coalesce(null, "", "final")     → "final"     (null and "" skipped)
coalesce(var.custom, "default") → var.custom or "default"

# try(): First that doesn't error
try(var.a.invalid, var.b, "fallback") → var.b  (a errors, b works)

# lookup(): Map key lookup with fallback
lookup({a=1}, "b", 99) → 99                    (key missing → default)

# ═══ COMPARISON TABLE ═══
# Function   Checks For          Use Case
# ─────────  ──────────────────  ─────────────────────────────────
# coalesce   null or ""          Simple fallback chain for strings
# try        evaluation errors   Complex objects, nested attributes
# lookup     missing map key     Map lookups with safe defaults

10.10 Lookup, Element, Index — Collection Access Functions

These three functions are the exam's favorite ways to test your understanding of collection access patterns. Know them cold — especially the differences in behavior when keys are missing or indices are out of bounds.

FunctionSignatureMissing Key BehaviorUse Case
lookup() lookup(map, key, default) Returns default if key not found.
Errors if no default and key missing.		 Map access with fallback. Used everywhere with terraform.workspace.
element() element(list, index) WRAPS if index exceeds length!
element(["a","b"],3) → "a" (wraps!)		 List access. ⚠️ Wrapping behavior is a common exam trap!
index() index(list, value) Errors if value not in list. Find position of a value in a list.
⚠️ EXAM TRAP: element() wraps around!

element(["a","b","c"], 3) → "a" — NOT an error! The index wraps modulo the list length. element(["a","b","c"], 5) → "c" (index 5 mod 3 = 2). This surprises many exam takers. Use index() carefully — it errors if the value isn't found.

📝 Chapter 10 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions

Q1
What does condition ? value_if_true : value_if_false evaluate to if condition is false?
Show Answer
Answer

value_if_false. The ternary returns the value after : when condition is false. Both branches must be compatible types.

Explanation

The conditional expression evaluates the condition as a boolean first. If true → returns first value. If false → returns second value. Terraform type-checks both branches at plan time.

Q2
What does [for s in ["a","b","c"] : upper(s)] produce?
Show Answer
Answer

["A", "B", "C"]. The for expression transforms each element using upper().

Explanation
[for s in ["a","b","c"] : upper(s)]
# └─ iterator ─┘  └─ source ─┘  └transform┘
# Result: ["A", "B", "C"]
Q3
You have resource "aws_instance" "web" { count = 3 }. How do you get a list of all instance IDs?
Show Answer
Answer

aws_instance.web[*].id (splat) or [for i in aws_instance.web : i.id] (for expression).

Explanation

The splat [*] is shorthand. Both produce ["i-abc", "i-def", "i-ghi"]. Use for expressions when you need to combine or transform attributes.

Q4
What is the difference between count and for_each in a dynamic block?
Show Answer
Answer

Dynamic blocks ONLY support for_each. count is NOT valid inside a dynamic block.

Explanation
dynamic "ingress" {
  for_each = var.rules    # ✅ ONLY for_each is valid
  # count = length(var.rules)  # ❌ NOT valid in dynamic blocks!
  content { ... }
}
Q5
What does coalesce("", null, "hello") return?
Show Answer
Answer

"hello". coalesce() skips empty strings and null, returning the first non-empty, non-null value.

Explanation
coalesce("")       → ""        (only arg, returns it)
coalesce("", null) → null      (only args are empty/null)
coalesce("", null, "x") → "x"  (skips first two)
Q6
What does element(["a","b","c"], 5) return? (Index 5 exceeds length 3)
Show Answer
Answer

"c". element() wraps around using modulo: 5 mod 3 = 2, so it returns index 2 = "c".

Explanation

This wrapping behavior is a classic exam trap. element(["a","b"], 2) → "a" (2 mod 2 = 0). It NEVER errors on out-of-bounds — always wraps.

Q7
How do you create a map from a list of objects using a for expression?
Show Answer
Answer

Use {} and =>: {for obj in var.list : obj.name => obj}.

Explanation
{for w in var.workers : w.name => w.port}
# Input: [{name="web",port=80}, {name="api",port=8000}]
# Output: { web = 80, api = 8000 }
# [] creates LIST; {} creates MAP; => separates key from value
Q8
What does can(regex("^[a-z]+$", "Hello123")) return?
Show Answer
Answer

false. regex() fails (uppercase + digits), but can() catches the error and returns false.

Explanation
can(regex("^[a-z]+$", "hello"))   → true   (matches)
can(regex("^[a-z]+$", "Hello"))   → false  (uppercase)
# Without can(), regex() would crash the plan entirely.
Q9
Which function would you use to generate 3 subnet CIDRs from "10.0.0.0/16"?
Show Answer
Answer

cidrsubnet() with for and range(): [for i in range(3) : cidrsubnet("10.0.0.0/16", 8, i)].

Explanation
# Result: ["10.0.0.0/24", "10.0.1.0/24", "10.0.2.0/24"]
# cidrsubnet(prefix, newbits, netnum)
# "10.0.0.0/16" + 8 bits = /24 subnets; netnum 0,1,2 = first three
Q10
In a dynamic "ingress" block, how do you access the current element's port?
Show Answer
Answer

ingress.value.port. The block label becomes the iterator name.

Explanation
dynamic "ingress" {
  for_each = var.rules
  content {
    from_port = ingress.value.port    # label.value.field
  }
}
# Iterator name = block label; available only inside content {}

Chapter 11: Output Values & Cross-Stack Data Flow 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 11: Output Values — Exposing Infrastructure Data to the World

Input variables bring data into your Terraform config. Output values send data out — to your terminal, to CI/CD pipelines, and crucially, to other Terraform stacks via remote state. Outputs are how one team's VPC module tells another team's application module "here's the subnet IDs you need." For anihpj/jobpost, outputs expose the RDS endpoint to developers, the EKS cluster name to CI/CD, and the load balancer DNS to DNS configuration.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1 hour 📖 7 Sections 🎯 Domain 4

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Declare output values with description, sensitive, and depends_on
  • ✅ Use outputs for CI/CD — piping infrastructure data to deployment scripts
  • ✅ Query outputs with terraform output and -json for automation
  • ✅ Protect sensitive outputs like database passwords and API keys
  • ✅ Share data across stacks using terraform_remote_state data source
  • ✅ Invert dependencies — how outputs enable loose coupling between stacks

11.1 Output Syntax — The Anatomy of an Output Block

OUTPUT BLOCK — Every Field Explained
  output "rds_endpoint" {             ◀── ① NAME: Unique output identifier
                                         Used as: module.vpc.rds_endpoint
    value       = aws_db_instance.anihpj.endpoint   ◀── ② VALUE (required)
    description = "RDS PostgreSQL endpoint for anihpj/jobpost"
    sensitive   = false               ◀── ③ SENSITIVE (optional, default: false)
                                         true → hides from console & terraform output
                                         ⚠️ Still stored in state!
    depends_on  = [                   ◀── ④ DEPENDS_ON (optional, rare)
      aws_db_instance.anihpj            Explicit dependency for outputs that
    ]                                   need a specific creation order
    precondition {                    ◀── ⑤ PRECONDITION (Terraform 1.2+)
      condition     = var.environment == "prod" ?
        aws_db_instance.anihpj.multi_az : true
      error_message = "Prod RDS must be Multi-AZ."
    }
  }
anihpj/jobpost: Key Output Examples
# Database connection info
output "database_endpoint" {
  value       = aws_db_instance.anihpj.endpoint
  description = "RDS PostgreSQL endpoint for application connection"
}

# K8s cluster access
output "eks_cluster_name" {
  value       = aws_eks_cluster.anihpj.name
  description = "EKS cluster name for kubectl configuration"
}

# Load balancer DNS
output "alb_dns_name" {
  value       = aws_lb.anihpj.dns_name
  description = "ALB DNS name — point your DNS CNAME here"
}

# Sensitive output — hidden by default
output "db_master_password" {
  value       = random_password.db_pass.result
  description = "RDS master password (handle with care!)"
  sensitive   = true    # ← Won't show in terraform output
}

11.2 Output Use Cases — Why Outputs Matter in Real Workflows

🔗 Cross-Stack References

One Terraform stack's outputs become another stack's inputs via terraform_remote_state.

data.terraform_remote_state.vpc.outputs.vpc_id

Example: Network team outputs VPC ID → App team reads it for EC2 placement.

🚀 CI/CD Integration

Pipe terraform output -json to deployment scripts, Ansible inventories, or kubectl.

terraform output -json | jq .db_host.value

Example: GitHub Actions reads EKS cluster name and updates kubeconfig.

📋 Documentation & Visibility

Outputs serve as live documentation — always showing the current state.

terraform output → instant infrastructure inventory.

Example: New team member runs terraform output to see all endpoints.

11.3 The terraform output Command — Querying Output Values

TERRAFORM OUTPUT — All Command Variants
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  $ terraform output                                                 ║
  ║  Lists ALL outputs (human-readable)                                 ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  $ terraform output rds_endpoint                                    ║
  ║  Shows ONE specific output by name                                  ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  $ terraform output -json                                           ║
  ║  ALL outputs in machine-readable JSON format                        ║
  ║  {                                                                   ║
  ║    "rds_endpoint": {                                                ║
  ║      "value": "anihpj.cdf89.xyz.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com",       ║
  ║      "type": "string",                                              ║
  ║      "sensitive": false                                             ║
  ║    }                                                                 ║
  ║  }                                                                   ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  $ terraform output -raw rds_endpoint                               ║
  ║  RAW value (no quotes) — perfect for shell scripts:                 ║
  ║  anihpj.cdf89.xyz.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com                       ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  $ terraform output -state=dev.tfstate rds_endpoint                 ║
  ║  Read from a SPECIFIC state file (not current workspace)            ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
CI/CD Pipeline: Using terraform output in Shell Scripts
# Get RDS endpoint and update Kubernetes Secret
#!/bin/bash
export DB_HOST=$(terraform output -raw rds_endpoint)
export DB_PASS=$(terraform output -raw db_master_password)

kubectl create secret generic db-credentials \
  --from-literal=host=$DB_HOST \
  --from-literal=password=$DB_PASS \
  -n anihpj-prod

# Get EKS cluster name and update kubeconfig
CLUSTER_NAME=$(terraform output -raw eks_cluster_name)
aws eks update-kubeconfig --name $CLUSTER_NAME --region us-east-1

11.4 Sensitive Outputs — Protecting Secrets in Output Values

Setting sensitive = true on an output hides its value from the console. But just like sensitive variables, the value is still stored in plaintext in the state file. Sensitive outputs also propagate sensitivity downstream — any value that references a sensitive output becomes sensitive too.

SENSITIVE OUTPUTS — Protection in Depth
  output "db_password" {
    value     = random_password.db.result
    sensitive = true   ◀── Hides from console & terraform output
  }

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    🛡️ PROTECTION STACK                           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  LAYER 1: sensitive = true                                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ $ terraform output                                         │ │
  │  │ db_password = (sensitive value)    ← REDACTED!             │ │
  │  │ $ terraform output -json                                   │ │
  │  │ {"db_password":{"sensitive":true,"type":"string"}}         │ │
  │  │ ← Value NOT included in JSON output!                       │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                   │
  │  LAYER 2: Sensitive chain (automatic propagation)                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ output "connection_string" {                                │ │
  │  │   value = "postgres://user:${random_password.db.result}@..."│ │
  │  │   # ← Becomes sensitive AUTOMATICALLY (references sensitive)│ │
  │  │ }                                                           │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                   │
  │  LAYER 3: Remote state encryption                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ backend "s3" { encrypt = true; kms_key_id = "alias/state" }│ │
  │  │ ← State file encrypted at rest (SSE-KMS)                   │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
⚠️ Sensitive Output Chain — One Sensitive Taints All

If a module output is sensitive, any root-level output that references it becomes sensitive too — even if you don't mark it. This is the "sensitivity chain": once a value is sensitive, everything downstream inherits sensitivity. This prevents accidental exposure but can be surprising when an output you didn't mark shows as (sensitive value).

11.5 Remote State Data Source — Sharing Data Across Stacks

The terraform_remote_state data source reads outputs from another Terraform state file. This is how separate Terraform stacks communicate — the network stack exposes VPC/subnet IDs, and the application stack reads them.

Cross-Stack Data Flow: VPC Stack → Application Stack
# ═══ STACK 1: NETWORK (vpc/) — Exposes networking data ═══
# vpc/outputs.tf
output "vpc_id" {
  value       = aws_vpc.anihpj.id
  description = "VPC ID for application deployment"
}
output "private_subnet_ids" {
  value       = aws_subnet.private[*].id
  description = "Private subnet IDs for EC2/EKS placement"
}

# ═══ STACK 2: APPLICATION (app/) — Reads networking data ═══
# app/main.tf
data "terraform_remote_state" "network" {
  backend = "s3"
  config = {
    bucket = "anihpj-terraform-state"
    key    = "vpc/terraform.tfstate"
    region = "us-east-1"
  }
}

# Now use the remote outputs like local data
resource "aws_instance" "anihpj" {
  subnet_id = data.terraform_remote_state.network.outputs.private_subnet_ids[0]
  #                └── data source ──┘  └── .outputs ──┘  └── output name ──┘
}
🧠 Exam Note: terraform_remote_state vs Data Sources

The exam may ask: "Should you use terraform_remote_state or a data source like aws_vpc?" The answer: Prefer direct data sources (data "aws_vpc") when possible — they're more decoupled and don't require access to another state file. Use terraform_remote_state only when the data you need isn't exposed as a cloud resource attribute (e.g., computed values like a generated random name).

OUTPUT FLOW — How Data Travels Between Terraform Stacks
  SCENARIO: Networking team builds VPC. App team needs subnet IDs.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │  NETWORKING STACK                           APP STACK            │
  │  (terraform apply)                          (terraform apply)    │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐                   ┌─────────────────┐  │
  │  │ resource "aws_vpc"  │                   │ data "terraform_ │  │
  │  │   "main" { ... }    │                   │   remote_state"  │  │
  │  │                     │                   │   "network" {    │  │
  │  │ output "vpc_id" {   │                   │   backend = "s3" │  │
  │  │   value = aws_vpc   │─── state file ──▶│   config = {     │  │
  │  │     .main.id        │   (S3 bucket)    │     bucket = ...  │  │
  │  │ }                   │                   │   }              │  │
  │  │                     │                   │ }                │  │
  │  │ output "subnet_ids"{│                   │                  │  │
  │  │   value = aws_subnet│                   │ resource "aws_   │  │
  │  │     .public[*].id   │                   │   instance" "app"{│  │
  │  └─────────────────────┘                   │   subnet_id =    │  │
  │                                            │   data.terraform_ │  │
  │                                            │   remote_state.   │  │
  │                                            │   network.outputs │  │
  │                                            │   .subnet_ids[0]  │  │
  │                                            │ }                │  │
  │                                            └─────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Networking team "publishes" vpc_id & subnet_ids as outputs  │
  │  💡 App team "consumes" them via terraform_remote_state         │
  │  💡 No hardcoded IDs! No manual coordination!                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

11.6 Dependency Inversion with Outputs — Loose Coupling

Outputs enable dependency inversion — instead of Stack A importing Stack B's resources directly, Stack B exposes outputs and Stack A consumes them. This means you can change Stack B's internals without breaking Stack A, as long as the output contract stays the same.

DEPENDENCY INVERSION — Tight Coupling vs Loose Coupling
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ❌ TIGHT COUPLING (Bad):                                            ║
  ║  App stack directly references Network stack resources by ARN        ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  App → aws_vpc.main.id  ← Direct reference to another stack's       ║
  ║                            resource. If Network renames "main"       ║
  ║                            to "anihpj", App breaks!                  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ✅ LOOSE COUPLING (Good):                                           ║
  ║  Network stack exposes outputs → App stack consumes them             ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  Network → output "vpc_id" { value = aws_vpc.anihpj.id }            ║
  ║  App     → data.terraform_remote_state.network.outputs.vpc_id       ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  Network team can rename resources, refactor internals —             ║
  ║  as long as output "vpc_id" still returns the VPC ID, App            ║
  ║  doesn't care what changed under the hood.                           ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

11.7 Output Best Practices — Production Patterns

✅ DO❌ DON'T
Add description to every output. They're documentation.Leave outputs undocumented — "what is this endpoint for?"
Mark secrets as sensitive. Passwords, keys, tokens.Output raw passwords. Use sensitive = true.
Use precondition for critical invariants. (Terraform 1.2+)Skip validation — "prod RDS should always be Multi-AZ"
Prefer data sources over terraform_remote_state.Couple stacks unnecessarily via remote state.
Use terraform output -raw in shell scripts.Parse JSON manually when -raw gives exactly what you need.
Expose stable, contractual outputs. vpc_id, subnet_ids.Expose internal implementation details that change frequently.

📝 Chapter 11 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions

Q1
What is the minimum required argument in an output block?
Show Answer
Answer

value. Every output block must have a value argument. description, sensitive, and depends_on are optional.

Explanation
output "db_endpoint" {
  value       = aws_db_instance.anihpj.endpoint  # ← ONLY required field
  description = "Database connection endpoint"     # Optional: documents the output
  sensitive   = false                              # Optional: hide from console
}

The exam may ask "What's the minimum for an output block?" — answer is value.

Q2
How do you get output values in machine-readable format for CI/CD automation?
Show Answer
Answer

terraform output -json returns all outputs as JSON. Use -raw for a single unquoted value for shell scripts.

Explanation
# JSON (for CI/CD pipelines — parse with jq):
$ terraform output -json
{"db_endpoint":{"value":"anihpj.xxx.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com","type":"string"}}

# Raw (for shell scripts — no quotes):
$ terraform output -raw db_endpoint
anihpj.xxx.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com
Q3
What happens if you reference a sensitive output in another output's value?
Show Answer
Answer

The referencing output becomes implicitly sensitive. Terraform propagates sensitivity through the dependency chain.

Explanation
output "password" { value = random_password.db.result; sensitive = true }
output "conn_str" { value = "user:${random_password.db.result}@host/db" }
# conn_str becomes sensitive AUTOMATICALLY — no manual mark needed
Q4
What data source reads outputs from another Terraform state?
Show Answer
Answer

terraform_remote_state. It reads outputs from another state file and exposes them via .outputs.

Explanation
data "terraform_remote_state" "networking" {
  backend = "s3"
  config = { bucket = "tfstate"; key = "networking/terraform.tfstate"; region = "us-east-1" }
}
# Access: data.terraform_remote_state.networking.outputs.vpc_id

Note: Data sources (like data "aws_vpc") are now preferred over remote_state for cross-stack references — more decoupled.

Q5
Can you use terraform output to read a specific output by name?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes — terraform output <name> shows just that output. -raw gives the raw value for shell scripts.

Explanation
terraform output db_endpoint          # Shows value with metadata
terraform output -raw db_endpoint     # Raw value (for shell: DB=$(tf output -raw db_endpoint))
terraform output -json db_endpoint    # JSON for a single output
Q6
What is a precondition block in an output? (Terraform 1.2+)
Show Answer
Answer

Validates conditions BEFORE the output is computed. If it fails, the plan/apply errors — an output-level validation gate.

Explanation
output "db_endpoint" {
  value = aws_db_instance.anihpj.endpoint
  precondition {
    condition     = aws_db_instance.anihpj.engine == "postgres"
    error_message = "Database must be PostgreSQL!"
  }
}

Preconditions catch misconfigurations early — before downstream dependencies break.

Q7
Your module has a sensitive output. What happens when you run terraform output?
Show Answer
Answer

It shows as (sensitive value) instead of the actual value. Redacted from console output.

Explanation

Important: sensitive = true only hides from console/UI output. The value is STILL stored in plain text in the state file. Anyone with state access can read it. For truly secret data, use Vault or cloud secret managers.

Q8
Which is preferred for cross-stack sharing — terraform_remote_state or a data source?
Show Answer
Answer

Data sources (like data "aws_vpc") are preferred — more decoupled. Use terraform_remote_state only when data isn't available via a data source.

Explanation
# PREFERRED: Data source (no state access needed)
data "aws_vpc" "shared" { filter { name = "tag:Name"; values = ["shared"] } }

# FALLBACK: Remote state (requires access to other team's state)
data "terraform_remote_state" "net" { backend = "s3"; config = {...} }

Data sources = loose coupling. Remote state = tight coupling. The exam prefers loose coupling.

Q9
What is the purpose of depends_on in an output block?
Show Answer
Answer

Ensures the output is computed only AFTER specific resources are created. Rarely needed — implicit dependencies handle 99% of cases.

Explanation

Terraform infers dependencies from references in value. depends_on in outputs is for edge cases where the dependency isn't visible (e.g., the output value uses a function result that doesn't directly reference the resource). Almost never needed in practice.

Q10
How do outputs enable dependency inversion between infrastructure stacks?
Show Answer
Answer

Outputs define a contract. Stack A exposes outputs → Stack B consumes them. Stack A can refactor internally — as long as the output contract stays the same, Stack B doesn't break.

Explanation
# Stack A (networking) — defines the contract:
output "vpc_id"    { value = aws_vpc.main.id }
output "subnet_ids" { value = aws_subnet.public[*].id }

# Stack B (compute) — consumes the contract:
data "terraform_remote_state" "net" { ... }
resource "aws_instance" "web" { subnet_id = data...net.outputs.subnet_ids[0] }
# Stack B doesn't care HOW Stack A creates subnets — only that subnet_ids exists.

📋 Chapter 11 Summary — Output Values
