Part 5: Modules & Code Reuse — The LEGO Bricks of Infrastructure

You've written hundreds of lines of Terraform. Now imagine needing that same VPC in 5 different projects. Do you copy-paste? No — you modularize. Modules are Terraform's answer to code reuse. They're like functions: defined once, called many times, with clear inputs (variables) and outputs (return values). In Part 5, you'll learn to create modules, call them from the Registry, compose them into production architectures, and build the entire anihpj/jobpost infrastructure as a collection of reusable, versioned, tested modules — each one doing ONE thing well.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~3 hours 📖 Ch 12-13 🎯 Domain 5

Chapter 12: Modules — Reusable Infrastructure Components 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 12: Modules — Functions for Infrastructure

A module is a self-contained package of Terraform configuration — variables go in, infrastructure comes out. Just like a function in programming: create_vpc(cidr="10.0.0.0/16") → vpc_id, subnet_ids. Modules are the difference between a sprawling 2000-line main.tf and a clean, composable infrastructure codebase. For the Terraform Associate exam, modules are Domain 5 (~10%, ~6 questions) — and understanding them is essential for any real-world Terraform work with anihpj/jobpost.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 10 Sections 🎯 Domain 5
🎓 TF Associate Relevance
Domain 5: Modules & Code Reuse ~10%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand what modules are — reusable packages of Terraform configuration
  • ✅ Distinguish root modules from child modules and build module trees
  • ✅ Structure modules with standard file layout (main, variables, outputs, versions)
  • ✅ Call modules from 6 different sources — local, Registry, GitHub, S3, Git, private
  • ✅ Pin module versions for deterministic, repeatable deployments
  • ✅ Compose small modules into complex production architectures
Modules = Domain 5 (~10%, ~6 Questions) — Know the Sources and Versioning

The exam tests your understanding of: (1) what a module IS and how it differs from the root module, (2) the module block syntax — especially source and version, (3) how to pass variables INTO a module and access outputs FROM a module, and (4) the 6 different module source types. The Registry (terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws) is the most commonly tested source format.

12.1 What Are Modules? — Functions for Infrastructure

A Terraform module is any directory containing .tf files. That's it. Your root directory with main.tf is a module (the "root module"). Any subdirectory you reference with module "name" { source = "./path" } is a "child module." Modules are Terraform's primary abstraction mechanism — they bundle resources, inputs, and outputs into reusable, versioned packages.

MODULES = FUNCTIONS — The Programming Analogy
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  PROGRAMMING FUNCTION               TERRAFORM MODULE                ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  function createVpc(cidr, name)   module "vpc" {                    ║
  ║    └── parameters (inputs)           source = "./modules/vpc"        ║
  ║                                      cidr_block = "10.0.0.0/16"    ║
  ║    // ... implementation ...          name       = "anihpj"          ║
  ║                                    }                                ║
  ║    return { vpc_id, subnets }                                       ║
  ║    └── return values (outputs)     module.vpc.vpc_id                 ║
  ║                                    module.vpc.subnet_ids             ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  💡 Module = function: variables = parameters, outputs = return     ║
  ║  💡 Call it 100 times with different inputs → 100 different VPCs    ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
📦 Encapsulation

Modules hide implementation details. Consumers only see variables (inputs) and outputs (results). You can refactor the internals without breaking anything.

♻️ Reusability

Write a VPC module once. Use it in dev, staging, prod, and 5 other projects. One source of truth for "how we build VPCs."

📋 Consistency

Every VPC created by the module follows the same pattern — same tags, same NAT config, same security defaults. No snowflakes.

12.2 Root Module vs Child Modules — The Module Tree

Every Terraform configuration has exactly one root module — the directory where you run terraform init/plan/apply. All module blocks in the root module create child modules. Child modules can themselves call other modules (grandchildren), forming a module tree.

THE MODULE TREE — Root → Child → Grandchild (anihpj Example)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  anihpj ROOT MODULE (where you run terraform apply)                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  module "networking" {                                          │ │
  │  │    source = "./modules/networking"                              │ │
  │  │    vpc_cidr = "10.0.0.0/16"                                     │ │
  │  │  }                                                              │ │
  │  │  └──▶ ./modules/networking/                                     │ │
  │  │       ├── main.tf      (VPC + Subnets + NAT + SG)               │ │
  │  │       ├── variables.tf                                           │ │
  │  │       └── outputs.tf    (vpc_id, subnet_ids)                    │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  module "compute" {                                             │ │
  │  │    source     = "./modules/compute"                             │ │
  │  │    vpc_id     = module.networking.vpc_id    ◀── Output from     │ │
  │  │    subnet_ids = module.networking.subnet_ids◀── networking      │ │
  │  │  }                                                              │ │
  │  │  └──▶ ./modules/compute/                    (CHILD MODULE)      │ │
  │  │       ├── main.tf    (EC2 + ASG + ALB)                          │ │
  │  │       └── outputs.tf (instance_ips)                             │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                      │
  │  CHILD CAN CALL GRANDCHILD:                                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  Inside ./modules/compute/main.tf:                              │ │
  │  │    module "security_group" {                                    │ │
  │  │      source = "../security_group"  ◀── GRANDCHILD MODULE       │ │
  │  │      vpc_id = var.vpc_id                                        │ │
  │  │    }                                                            │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
FeatureRoot ModuleChild Module
Where definedDirectory where you run terraformCalled via module "name" {} block
Can call modules?✅ Yes✅ Yes (grandchildren)
Has outputs?✅ Yes (printed after apply)✅ Yes (accessed via module.name.output)
Has backend?✅ Yes (state storage)❌ No (inherits from root)
count/for_each?N/A✅ Terraform 0.13+ supports count/for_each on modules

12.3 Module Structure — The Standard File Layout

Every well-crafted module follows a standard file structure. This isn't enforced by Terraform — it's a convention that makes modules discoverable and maintainable. When you open any module, you know exactly where to look.

MODULE FILE STRUCTURE — The Gold Standard
  modules/networking/
  ├── main.tf           ◀── RESOURCES: The actual infrastructure (VPC, subnets, etc.)
  ├── variables.tf      ◀── INPUTS: What the module NEEDS (cidr, name, tags)
  ├── outputs.tf        ◀── OUTPUTS: What the module RETURNS (vpc_id, subnet_ids)
  ├── versions.tf       ◀── VERSION PINS: Required providers & their versions
  ├── README.md         ◀── DOCS: What it does, inputs, outputs, examples
  └── LICENSE            ◀── LICENSE: Usually Apache 2.0 or MPL 2.0

  ═══ WHY THIS STRUCTURE MATTERS ═══
  • Terraform loads ALL .tf files in a directory — file names are for HUMANS
  • Consistent structure = instant orientation for anyone reading your code
  • README.md is REQUIRED for publishing to the Terraform Registry
  • Separation by PURPOSE (not resource type) makes files navigable
modules/networking/variables.tf — The Input Contract
variable "vpc_cidr" {
  description = "CIDR block for the VPC"
  type        = string
  default     = "10.0.0.0/16"
}

variable "subnet_count" {
  description = "Number of public & private subnets to create"
  type        = number
  default     = 3
  validation {
    condition     = var.subnet_count >= 1 && var.subnet_count <= 6
    error_message = "Subnet count must be 1-6."
  }
}

variable "enable_nat_gateway" {
  description = "Provision NAT Gateways for private subnet egress"
  type        = bool
  default     = true
}

variable "tags" {
  description = "Common tags to apply to all resources"
  type        = map(string)
  default     = {}
}
MODULE AS A BLACK BOX — Input/Output Contract Pattern
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    MODULE = FUNCTION (Black Box)                      │
  │                                                                      │
  │   INPUTS (variables)              OUTPUTS (outputs)                   │
  │   ┌──────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────┐               │
  │   │ vpc_cidr         │           │ vpc_id           │               │
  │   │ subnet_count     │──▶ ┌───┐──▶│ subnet_ids       │               │
  │   │ enable_nat       │   │ ? │   │ nat_ip           │               │
  │   │ environment      │──▶ └───┘──▶│ route_table_ids  │               │
  │   └──────────────────┘   MODULE   └──────────────────┘               │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 CONSUMERS only see INPUTS & OUTPUTS — never internals            │
  │  💡 Refactor internals freely as long as contract is unchanged        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MODULE CONTRACT EXAMPLE (networking module):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ # variables.tf (INPUT CONTRACT — what the module NEEDS)               │
  │ variable "vpc_cidr"       { type = string }                          │
  │ variable "subnet_count"   { type = number; default = 3 }             │
  │ variable "enable_nat"     { type = bool;   default = true }          │
  │                                                                      │
  │ # outputs.tf (OUTPUT CONTRACT — what the module RETURNS)              │
  │ output "vpc_id"           { value = aws_vpc.main.id }                │
  │ output "subnet_ids"       { value = aws_subnet.public[*].id }        │
  │ output "nat_ip"           { value = aws_eip.nat.public_ip }          │
  │                                                                      │
  │ # main.tf (IMPLEMENTATION — hidden from consumers, free to change)   │
  │ resource "aws_vpc" "main" { cidr_block = var.vpc_cidr }             │
  │ resource "aws_subnet" "public" { count = var.subnet_count }         │
  │ # ... internals can be refactored without breaking consumers         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

12.4 Calling Modules — The module Block Deep Dive

The module block is how you invoke a module. It specifies where the module lives (source), which version to use (version), and what inputs to pass (variables as arguments). The results are accessed via module.<NAME>.<OUTPUT>.

MODULE BLOCK — Every Argument Explained
  module "networking" {                 ◀── ① LOCAL NAME: How you reference
                                            this module instance
                                            Used as: module.networking.vpc_id
    source  = "./modules/networking"     ◀── ② SOURCE: Where the module lives
                                            (local path, Registry, Git, S3...)
    version = "1.2.0"                   ◀── ③ VERSION: Which version to use
                                            (only for Registry/Git sources)

    # INPUT VARIABLES — mapped from module's variables.tf:
    vpc_cidr            = "10.0.0.0/16"      ◀── Passes to var.vpc_cidr
    subnet_count        = 3                   ◀── Passes to var.subnet_count
    enable_nat_gateway  = true                ◀── Passes to var.enable_nat
    tags                = var.common_tags     ◀── Passes to var.tags

    # DEPENDENCY (rarely needed — implicit deps from references):
    depends_on = [aws_s3_bucket.assets]
  }

  # ACCESS OUTPUTS:
  module.networking.vpc_id        → "vpc-0a1b2c3d4e5f"
  module.networking.subnet_ids    → ["subnet-abc", "subnet-def", "subnet-ghi"]
  module.networking.nat_ip        → "54.123.45.67"
🧠 Module Block Rules (Exam-Relevant)

1. source is REQUIRED. Every module block must have a source. It's the only required argument.

2. version only works with Registry. You can't version a local path module with version = "1.0" — use Git tags instead.

3. Module names must be unique. Two module "networking" blocks in the same root = error.

4. count and for_each work on modules. Since Terraform 0.13, you can create multiple instances of a module with count or for_each.

5. Outputs are accessed with dot notation. module.NAME.OUTPUT — not module["NAME"].OUTPUT.

12.5 Module Sources — 6 Ways to Fetch Modules

The source argument tells Terraform where to find the module code. Terraform supports 6 different source types — from simple local paths to private registries. This is one of the most-tested topics in Domain 5.

THE 6 MODULE SOURCE TYPES — Complete Reference with Examples
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  1. LOCAL PATH — For modules in your repo (development)             ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  source = "./modules/vpc"             ← Relative to calling module  ║
  ║  source = "../shared/networking"      ← Parent directory            ║
  ║  source = "/opt/terraform/modules/vpc" ← Absolute path (avoid!)     ║
  ║  ✅ Best for: monorepo modules, internal development                ║
  ║  ❌ No version pinning support                                      ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  2. TERRAFORM REGISTRY — Public community modules (4B+ downloads)   ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  source  = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"                          ║
  ║  version = "5.1.2"                    ← Pin to specific version     ║
  ║  Format: NAMESPACE/MODULE_NAME/PROVIDER                             ║
  ║  ✅ Best for: AWS VPC, EKS, RDS, S3, Lambda (community battle-tested)║
  ║  ✅ 4000+ modules available, versioned, documented                  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  3. GITHUB — Public or private repositories                         ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  source = "github.com/anihs-canvas/terraform-aws-vpc"               ║
  ║  source = "github.com/anihs-canvas/terraform-aws-vpc?ref=v2.1.0"  ║
  ║                                          └─── Tag, branch, or commit║
  ║  ✅ Best for: private/internal modules hosted on GitHub             ║
  ║  ✅ ref= lets you pin to a tag/branch/commit                        ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  4. GENERIC GIT — Any Git repository (GitLab, Bitbucket, Azure)     ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  source = "git::https://gitlab.com/team/terraform-modules.git//vpc" ║
  ║           └── git:: prefix ──┘                     └── //subdir ──┘  ║
  ║  ✅ Best for: GitLab, Bitbucket, Azure DevOps, self-hosted Git      ║
  ║  ✅ //subdir lets you reference a subdirectory within the repo       ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  5. S3 / GCS / HTTP — Archived module packages                     ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  source = "s3::https://s3.amazonaws.com/my-bucket/vpc-module.zip"  ║
  ║  source = "gcs::https://www.googleapis.com/storage/v1/bucket/module"║
  ║  ✅ Best for: air-gapped environments, pre-packaged releases        ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  6. TERRAFORM CLOUD/ENTERPRISE PRIVATE REGISTRY                     ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  source  = "app.terraform.io/my-org/vpc/aws"                        ║
  ║  version = "~> 2.0"                   ← Private, org-scoped         ║
  ║  ✅ Best for: sharing modules within your organization privately    ║
  ║  ✅ Same workflow as public Registry, but private to your org       ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
Source TypeSyntax ExampleVersioning?Best For
Local Path"./modules/vpc"❌Monorepo dev
Registry"terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"✅ versionCommunity modules
GitHub"github.com/org/repo?ref=v1.0"✅ ref=Private repos
Generic Git"git::https://gitlab.com/...//sub"✅ ref=Any Git host
S3/HTTP"s3::https://bucket/module.zip"❌Air-gapped
Private Registry"app.terraform.io/org/module/prov"✅ versionEnterprise teams

12.6 Module Composition — Building Complex Systems from Simple Blocks

Module composition is the art of combining small, focused modules into larger systems. Think LEGO: each brick does one thing well, but combined, they build anything. A VPC module handles networking. A compute module handles EC2. They connect through outputs and inputs — loose coupling through well-defined interfaces.

MODULE COMPOSITION — Small Modules, Big Systems
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    COMPOSING LIKE LEGO BRICKS                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐    │
  │  │ VPC      │   │ Security │   │   EC2    │   │   RDS    │    │
  │  │ Module   │   │ Group    │   │  Module  │   │  Module  │    │
  │  │          │   │ Module   │   │          │   │          │    │
  │  │ Outputs: │   │ Inputs:  │   │ Inputs:  │   │ Inputs:  │    │
  │  │ • vpc_id │──▶│ • vpc_id │──▶│ • vpc_id │──▶│ • subnets│    │
  │  │ • subnets│   │          │   │ • subnets│   │          │    │
  │  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘    │
  │       │              │              │              │            │
  │       └──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘            │
  │                          │                                       │
  │                          ▼                                       │
  │                ┌─────────────────┐                               │
  │                │  ANIHPJ APP     │  = 1 VPC + 3 SGs + 2 EC2s   │
  │                │  (root module)  │    + 1 RDS + 1 S3 + 1 Redis  │
  │                └─────────────────┘                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Each module does ONE thing well.                             │
  │  💡 Compose them to build ANY system.                            │
  │  💡 If you need a new feature, add a new module — don't bloat    │
  │     existing ones. Single Responsibility Principle for infra.    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

12.7 Module Versioning — Stability Through Pinning

Without version pinning, every terraform init fetches the latest module version — which might have breaking changes. Version pinning is the difference between "it worked yesterday" and deterministic, repeatable infrastructure.

VERSION PINNING — Stability vs Chaos
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ❌ WITHOUT version pinning (DANGER):                                ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  module "vpc" {                                                     ║
  ║    source = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"  ← Latest version!     ║
  ║  }                                                                  ║
  ║  ❌ Breaking changes hit you unexpectedly                           ║
  ║  ❌ Different team members = different versions                     ║
  ║  ❌ CI/CD builds are non-deterministic                               ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ✅ WITH version pinning (SAFE):                                     ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  module "vpc" {                                                     ║
  ║    source  = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"                        ║
  ║    version = "5.1.2"     ← Exact version (locked down)              ║
  ║  }                                                                  ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  # OR use pessimistic constraint for auto-patching:                 ║
  ║  module "vpc" {                                                     ║
  ║    source  = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"                        ║
  ║    version = "~> 5.0"    ← >= 5.0, < 6.0 (auto minor/patch)       ║
  ║  }                                                                  ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ✅ Everyone gets the same version (deterministic!)                  ║
  ║  ✅ Explicit upgrade: change version → terraform init → plan → apply ║
  ║  ✅ Rollback: change version back → terraform init → plan → apply   ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
🧠 Semantic Versioning for Modules (SemVer)

v5.1.2 = MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH. MAJOR (5) = breaking changes. MINOR (1) = new features, backward-compatible. PATCH (2) = bug fixes. ~> 5.0 allows any 5.x.x but blocks 6.0.0. ~> 5.0.0 allows only 5.0.x patches. The Registry enforces SemVer — you can't publish a breaking change without bumping MAJOR.

12.8 Module Nested Calls — Modules Calling Other Modules

Child modules aren't limited to being called only by the root module. A child module can call other modules, creating grandchild relationships. This enables powerful patterns like a "database" module that internally uses a "security group" module.

Nested Module Call — Database Module Uses Security Group Module
# ═══ modules/database/main.tf ═══
# This child module calls ANOTHER module (grandchild)

module "security_group" {
  source = "../security_group"        # ← Grandchild: calls sibling module
  name   = "${var.name}-db-sg"
  vpc_id = var.vpc_id

  ingress_rules = [
    { port = 5432, protocol = "tcp", cidr = var.app_subnet_cidr }
  ]
}

resource "aws_db_instance" "main" {
  identifier     = var.name
  engine         = "postgres"
  instance_class = var.instance_class
  vpc_security_group_ids = [module.security_group.sg_id]
  # ← References grandchild's output
}

# ═══ Root module calls database ═══
module "database" {
  source          = "./modules/database"
  name            = "anihpj-db"
  vpc_id          = module.networking.vpc_id
  app_subnet_cidr = "10.0.10.0/24"
}

# 💡 Nesting depth: Root → database (child) → security_group (grandchild)
# 💡 Each level encapsulates complexity — the root doesn't need to
#    know about security groups at all!

12.9 Module Best Practices — Production-Grade Checklist

✅ Best PracticeWhy It Matters
Single Responsibility"Do ONE thing well." VPC module creates VPC + subnets ONLY. Don't add EC2, RDS, or S3 to it.
Small Interface (3-5 variables)>10 variables = module is doing too much. Use objects to group related inputs.
Output useful valuesvpc_id, subnet_ids — what other modules NEED to consume. Don't output internal implementation details.
Pin provider versionsAlways use required_providers with ~> X.Y in the module's versions.tf.
Pin module versions (callers)Always use version = "~> X.Y" when calling a module from the Registry.
README per moduleExplain: what it does, inputs, outputs, example usage. Required for Registry publication.
Tag module releasesGit tags (v1.0.0, v2.1.0) match version in module calls. Enables ?ref=v1.0.0.
Test modules independentlyEach module should be testable on its own before being composed into larger systems.
❌ Anti-PatternWhy It's Bad
Embedding secrets in modulesUse variables (sensitive=true) or data sources (Vault/AWS SM). Never hardcode credentials.
Using provisioners in modulesModules should be idempotent. Config management belongs to Ansible, not Terraform.
Giant "kitchen sink" moduleA single module that creates VPC + EC2 + RDS + S3 + IAM. Impossible to reuse or test.
Skipping validation blocksWithout validation, bad inputs cause API errors 20 minutes into an apply. Catch them at plan time.

12.10 Terraform Registry — Publishing & Discovering Modules

The Terraform Registry hosts 4000+ community modules with 4+ billion downloads. It's the npm/PyPI of infrastructure — pre-built, tested, documented modules for virtually every cloud service. The AWS VPC module alone has 1+ billion downloads.

🔍 Finding Modules

Search by provider: registry.terraform.io/browse/modules?provider=aws

Check stats: Downloads, stars, last updated, verified publisher badge

Read the docs: Every module has auto-generated Inputs/Outputs/Resources docs

Most popular: terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws (1B+), terraform-aws-modules/eks/aws, terraform-aws-modules/rds/aws

📤 Publishing Modules

1. Create a GitHub repo with standard module structure

2. Add README.md with description, inputs, outputs, examples

3. Tag releases: git tag v1.0.0 && git push --tags

4. Connect repo to registry.terraform.io

5. Registry auto-detects tags and publishes versions

📝 Chapter 12 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions

Q1
What is a Terraform module?
Show Answer
Answer

A module is any directory containing .tf files. It's a self-contained package — variables go in, infrastructure comes out. The root module is where you run terraform; child modules are called via module {} blocks.

Explanation

Every .tf directory is a module. The root module is special — it has the backend config and provider blocks. Child modules are reusable components without their own state. Think of modules as functions: inputs (variables) → processing (resources) → outputs (return values).

Q2
What is the ONLY required argument in a module block?
Show Answer
Answer

source. It tells Terraform where to find the module code. version is optional but strongly recommended for Registry modules.

Explanation
module "vpc" {
  source = "./modules/vpc"  # ← Only required argument
  version = "1.0.0"         # ← Optional (Registry/Git only)
}
Q3
How do you access an output from a child module in the root module?
Show Answer
Answer

module.<NAME>.<OUTPUT> — dot notation with the module's local name and output name. NOT module["name"].output.

Explanation
module "networking" { source = "./modules/networking" }
module.networking.vpc_id       # ✅ Correct
module["networking"].vpc_id    # ❌ Wrong syntax!
Q4
Which module source would you use for a module in the same repository?
Show Answer
Answer

Local path — source = "./modules/vpc". No version pinning support — always uses current directory state.

Q5
What does source = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws" refer to?
Show Answer
Answer

The public Terraform Registry. Format: NAMESPACE/MODULE_NAME/PROVIDER. Resolves to registry.terraform.io/terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws.

Explanation

Three-part Registry format: namespace/module/provider. The third segment (provider) determines which cloud. Private TFC: app.terraform.io/org/vpc/aws. Registry modules support version pinning.

Q6
How do you pin a GitHub module to a specific version?
Show Answer
Answer

Use the ?ref= query parameter: source = "github.com/org/repo?ref=v2.1.0". ref can be a tag, branch, or commit SHA.

Explanation

Tags (v1.0.0) = production. Branches = development (flexible but risky). Commit SHA = maximum safety. Git sources work across GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket — prefix with git:: for non-GitHub.

Q7
Can a child module call another module?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes! Child modules can call other modules (grandchildren), forming nested module trees with no hard limit on depth.

Explanation

Practical limit is 2-3 levels deep. Each nesting level adds abstraction but also complexity. Grandchildren inherit providers from root. All modules are initialized together with terraform init — no separate init per module.

Q8
What files should EVERY well-structured module include?
Show Answer
Answer

main.tf (resources), variables.tf (inputs), outputs.tf (outputs), versions.tf (provider pins), and README.md (documentation — required for Registry).

Explanation

README.md is MANDATORY for publishing to the Registry. It must include: usage example, input variables table, output values table. Standard structure = other developers can understand your module in 30 seconds.

Q9
What is the difference between root module and child module?
Show Answer
Answer

Root = where you run terraform (has backend, providers, state). Child = called via module {} (inherits from root, no own backend).

Explanation

Root = has terraform {} block with backend. Root = has provider {} blocks. Child = inherits providers. Child = receives variables via module block. Only root has state — children are stateless templates.

Q10
What is module composition and why is it important?
Show Answer
Answer

Combining small, focused modules into larger systems — like LEGO bricks. Each module does ONE thing well. Independently testable, versioned, and reusable.

Explanation
module "networking" { source = "./modules/networking" }
module "database"   { source = "./modules/database";   subnet_id = module.networking.subnet_ids[0] }
module "compute"    { source = "./modules/compute";     vpc_id    = module.networking.vpc_id }

Each module = one concern. Networking doesn't know about databases. Small, focused modules are easier to test, version, and reuse than monolithic ones.

📋 Chapter 12 Summary — Modules

6
Source Types
4000+
Registry Modules
5
Standard Files
10
Practice Qs
✅ DO❌ DON'T
Pin module versions with version = "~> X.Y"Use Registry modules without version pinning
Follow standard module structure (main, vars, outputs)Put everything in one giant main.tf
Design modules with single responsibilityCreate "kitchen sink" modules that do everything
Add README.md with inputs/outputs/examplesLeave modules undocumented

Chapter 13: anihpj/jobpost Modular Architecture — Docker + K8s + AWS 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 13: anihpj/jobpost Modular Architecture — Production-Grade Design with Docker + Kubernetes + AWS

Now you understand modules in theory. Let's apply them to a real production architecture. We'll refactor the entire anihpj/jobpost infrastructure into 10 specialized modules — each handling one layer of the stack: networking, compute (EKS), container (Docker/ECR), Kubernetes, database, storage, caching, DNS, IAM, and monitoring. Then we'll tie them together in a root module and deploy across dev/staging/prod with proper isolation. This is the architecture pattern used by real production teams deploying Django on Kubernetes with AWS.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 12 Sections 🐳☸️ K8s + AWS

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Design a modular infrastructure with 10 focused, reusable modules
  • ✅ Build networking, compute (EKS), container (Docker/ECR), and K8s modules
  • ✅ Wire modules together through outputs and inputs — the contract pattern
  • ✅ Implement environment strategy with workspaces and K8s namespaces
  • ✅ Configure IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) for least-privilege K8s pods
  • ✅ Set up monitoring with CloudWatch + Fluent Bit DaemonSet for K8s logs

13.1 Networking Module — VPC, Subnets, Route Tables, NAT, Security Groups

The networking module is the foundation of everything. Every other module depends on it. It creates the VPC, public/private subnets across 3 AZs, NAT gateways for private subnet egress, and baseline security groups.

modules/networking/main.tf — Core Resources
resource "aws_vpc" "main" {
  cidr_block           = var.vpc_cidr
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  tags = merge(var.tags, { Name = "${var.name}-vpc" })
}

resource "aws_subnet" "public" {
  count                   = var.subnet_count
  vpc_id                  = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block              = cidrsubnet(var.vpc_cidr, 8, count.index)
  availability_zone       = data.aws_availability_zones.available.names[count.index]
  map_public_ip_on_launch = true
  tags = { Name = "${var.name}-public-${count.index}" }
}

resource "aws_subnet" "private" {
  count             = var.subnet_count
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block        = cidrsubnet(var.vpc_cidr, 8, count.index + var.subnet_count)
  availability_zone = data.aws_availability_zones.available.names[count.index]
  tags = { Name = "${var.name}-private-${count.index}" }
}

resource "aws_nat_gateway" "main" {
  count         = var.enable_nat ? var.subnet_count : 0
  allocation_id = aws_eip.nat[count.index].id
  subnet_id     = aws_subnet.public[count.index].id
}
modules/networking/outputs.tf — The Output Contract
output "vpc_id"              { value = aws_vpc.main.id }
output "public_subnet_ids"   { value = aws_subnet.public[*].id }
output "private_subnet_ids"  { value = aws_subnet.private[*].id }
output "nat_ips"             { value = var.enable_nat ? aws_eip.nat[*].public_ip : [] }

13.2 Compute Module — EKS Cluster + Managed Node Groups

The compute module provisions the EKS cluster and managed node groups that run the jobpost application pods. It consumes the VPC and subnet IDs from the networking module.

modules/compute/main.tf — EKS Cluster for jobpost
module "eks" {
  source  = "terraform-aws-modules/eks/aws"
  version = "~> 19.0"

  cluster_name    = var.cluster_name
  cluster_version = "1.28"
  vpc_id          = var.vpc_id
  subnet_ids      = var.private_subnet_ids

  eks_managed_node_groups = {
    jobpost = {
      instance_types = [var.instance_type]
      min_size       = var.min_size
      max_size       = var.max_size
      desired_size   = var.desired_size
      labels = { app = "jobpost" }
    }
  }
}

# Outputs for other modules
output "cluster_name"       { value = module.eks.cluster_name }
output "cluster_endpoint"   { value = module.eks.cluster_endpoint }
output "cluster_certificate_authority_data" {
  value = module.eks.cluster_certificate_authority_data
}
output "node_group_iam_role_arn" { value = module.eks.eks_managed_node_groups["jobpost"].iam_role_arn }

13.3 Container Module — Docker Build + ECR Repository + Image Versioning

This module manages the Docker image lifecycle: creating the ECR repository, building the image, and pushing it with version tags. It bridges the gap between application code and Kubernetes deployment.

modules/container/main.tf — ECR + Docker Build
resource "aws_ecr_repository" "jobpost" {
  name                 = "anihpj/jobpost"
  image_tag_mutability = "IMMUTABLE"
  force_delete         = false

  image_scanning_configuration { scan_on_push = true }

  lifecycle { prevent_destroy = true }
}

# Lifecycle policy: keep last 10 images
resource "aws_ecr_lifecycle_policy" "jobpost" {
  repository = aws_ecr_repository.jobpost.name
  policy     = jsonencode({
    rules = [{
      rulePriority = 1
      selection     = { tagStatus = "any"; countType = "imageCountMoreThan"; countNumber = 10 }
      action        = { type = "expire" }
    }]
  })
}

output "repository_url" { value = aws_ecr_repository.jobpost.repository_url }
output "registry_id"    { value = aws_ecr_repository.jobpost.registry_id }

13.4 Kubernetes Module — K8s Deployment, Service, Ingress for jobpost

This module deploys the jobpost application as Kubernetes resources: Deployment (for pods), Service (for networking), and Ingress (for external access). It references the ECR image and the EKS cluster.

modules/kubernetes/main.tf — jobpost Deployment
resource "kubernetes_deployment" "jobpost" {
  metadata {
    name      = "jobpost-django"
    namespace = var.namespace
    labels    = { app = "jobpost" }
  }
  spec {
    replicas = var.replicas
    selector { match_labels = { app = "jobpost" } }
    template {
      metadata { labels = { app = "jobpost" } }
      spec {
        service_account_name = kubernetes_service_account.jobpost.metadata[0].name
        container {
          name  = "django"
          image = "${var.ecr_repository_url}:${var.image_tag}"
          port  { container_port = 8000 }
          env {
            name  = "DATABASE_URL"
            value = "postgres://${var.db_user}:${var.db_password}@${var.db_host}:5432/anihpj"
          }
          resources {
            requests = { cpu = "250m"; memory = "256Mi" }
            limits   = { cpu = "500m"; memory = "512Mi" }
          }
        }
      }
    }
  }
}

resource "kubernetes_service" "jobpost" {
  metadata { name = "jobpost-svc"; namespace = var.namespace }
  spec {
    selector = { app = "jobpost" }
    port { port = 80; target_port = 8000 }
    type = "ClusterIP"
  }
}

13.5 Database Module — RDS PostgreSQL with Multi-AZ + K8s ExternalName Service

modules/database/main.tf — RDS for anihpj
resource "aws_db_subnet_group" "main" {
  name       = "${var.name}-db-subnet"
  subnet_ids = var.private_subnet_ids
  tags       = { Name = "${var.name}-db-subnet-group" }
}

resource "aws_db_instance" "main" {
  identifier     = var.name
  engine         = "postgres"
  engine_version = var.engine_version
  instance_class = var.instance_class
  allocated_storage     = var.storage_gb
  storage_encrypted     = true
  multi_az              = var.multi_az
  db_subnet_group_name  = aws_db_subnet_group.main.name
  vpc_security_group_ids = [aws_security_group.rds.id]
  backup_retention_period = var.backup_retention_days
  skip_final_snapshot    = false

  lifecycle { prevent_destroy = true }
}

output "endpoint" { value = aws_db_instance.main.endpoint }
output "port"     { value = aws_db_instance.main.port }
output "address"  { value = aws_db_instance.main.address }

13.6 Storage Module — S3 Buckets + CloudFront CDN for Static Assets

The storage module creates S3 buckets for Django's static files (CSS, JS, images) and user media uploads, fronted by CloudFront for global low-latency delivery. Lifecycle policies automatically transition old objects to cheaper storage tiers.

modules/storage/main.tf — S3 + CloudFront
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "static" {
  bucket = "${var.name}-static-${var.environment}"
  tags   = var.tags
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_versioning" "static" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.static.id
  versioning_configuration { status = "Enabled" }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_lifecycle_configuration" "static" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.static.id
  rule {
    id     = "transition-to-ia"
    status = "Enabled"
    transition {
      days          = 30
      storage_class = "STANDARD_IA"
    }
    transition {
      days          = 90
      storage_class = "GLACIER"
    }
  }
}

resource "aws_cloudfront_distribution" "static" {
  enabled = true
  origin {
    domain_name = aws_s3_bucket.static.bucket_regional_domain_name
    origin_id   = "S3-static"
  }
  default_cache_behavior {
    allowed_methods  = ["GET", "HEAD"]
    cached_methods   = ["GET", "HEAD"]
    target_origin_id = "S3-static"
    viewer_protocol_policy = "redirect-to-https"
    forwarded_values { query_string = false; cookies { forward = "none" } }
  }
  restrictions { geo_restriction { restriction_type = "none" } }
  viewer_certificate { cloudfront_default_certificate = true }
}

output "static_bucket_name"    { value = aws_s3_bucket.static.id }
output "cloudfront_domain"     { value = aws_cloudfront_distribution.static.domain_name }

13.7 Caching Module — ElastiCache Redis for Django Sessions & Cache

The caching module provisions an ElastiCache Redis cluster in private subnets for Django's session storage and view caching. Redis dramatically reduces database load by caching frequently-accessed data in memory.

modules/caching/main.tf — ElastiCache Redis Cluster
resource "aws_elasticache_subnet_group" "redis" {
  name       = "${var.name}-redis-subnet"
  subnet_ids = var.private_subnet_ids
}

resource "aws_elasticache_cluster" "redis" {
  cluster_id           = "${var.name}-redis"
  engine               = "redis"
  node_type            = var.node_type
  num_cache_nodes      = var.cluster_size
  parameter_group_name = "default.redis7"
  port                 = 6379
  subnet_group_name    = aws_elasticache_subnet_group.redis.name
  security_group_ids   = [aws_security_group.redis.id]
}

# K8s ExternalName Service — maps Redis endpoint to K8s DNS
# apiVersion: v1
# kind: Service
# metadata: { name: redis-external; namespace: anihpj-prod }
# spec:
#   type: ExternalName
#   externalName: ${aws_elasticache_cluster.redis.cache_nodes[0].address}
# Pods can now connect to "redis-external" like any K8s service!

output "redis_endpoint" { value = aws_elasticache_cluster.redis.cache_nodes[0].address }
output "redis_port"     { value = aws_elasticache_cluster.redis.port }

13.8 DNS Module — Route 53 for anihpj.com, ALB Ingress, Health Checks

The DNS module creates a Route 53 hosted zone for anihpj.com, an ACM TLS certificate for HTTPS, and an A-record alias pointing to the ALB. Health checks monitor the endpoint and can failover to a static error page.

modules/dns/main.tf — Route 53 + ACM
resource "aws_route53_zone" "main" {
  name = var.domain_name  # "anihpj.com"
}

resource "aws_acm_certificate" "main" {
  domain_name       = var.domain_name
  validation_method = "DNS"
  subject_alternative_names = ["*.${var.domain_name}"]
}

resource "aws_route53_record" "cert_validation" {
  for_each = {
    for dvo in aws_acm_certificate.main.domain_validation_options :
    dvo.domain_name => { name = dvo.resource_record_name; record = dvo.resource_record_value; type = dvo.resource_record_type }
  }
  zone_id = aws_route53_zone.main.zone_id
  name    = each.value.name
  type    = each.value.type
  records = [each.value.record]
  ttl     = 60
}

resource "aws_route53_record" "www" {
  zone_id = aws_route53_zone.main.zone_id
  name    = "www.${var.domain_name}"
  type    = "A"
  alias {
    name                   = var.alb_dns_name
    zone_id                = var.alb_zone_id
    evaluate_target_health = true
  }
}

resource "aws_route53_health_check" "www" {
  fqdn              = "www.${var.domain_name}"
  port              = 443
  type              = "HTTPS"
  resource_path     = "/health/"
  failure_threshold = 3
  request_interval  = 30
}

output "name_servers" { value = aws_route53_zone.main.name_servers }
output "zone_id"      { value = aws_route53_zone.main.zone_id }

13.9 IAM Module — IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts), Least-Privilege

IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) is the modern way to grant AWS permissions to Kubernetes pods. Instead of attaching IAM policies to the node group (which every pod on that node can use), you create an IAM role and annotate a K8s Service Account to assume it. Only pods using that SA get the AWS permissions.

IRSA: IAM Role → K8s Service Account Binding
# IAM Role that jobpost pods can assume
resource "aws_iam_role" "jobpost_s3_access" {
  name = "anihpj-jobpost-s3-access"
  assume_role_policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [{
      Effect = "Allow"
      Principal = { Federated = var.eks_oidc_provider_arn }
      Action = "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity"
      Condition = {
        StringEquals = {
          "${var.eks_oidc_issuer}:sub" = "system:serviceaccount:${var.namespace}:jobpost-sa"
        }
      }
    }]
  })
}

# Grant S3 read access to jobpost pods
resource "aws_iam_role_policy" "s3_access" {
  role = aws_iam_role.jobpost_s3_access.name
  policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [{
      Effect   = "Allow"
      Action   = ["s3:GetObject", "s3:ListBucket"]
      Resource = ["${var.s3_bucket_arn}/*", var.s3_bucket_arn]
    }]
  })
}

# In K8s: annotate the Service Account to use this IAM role
# kubectl annotate sa jobpost-sa -n anihpj-prod \
#   eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn=arn:aws:iam::ACCOUNT:role/anihpj-jobpost-s3-access

13.10 Monitoring Module — CloudWatch + Fluent Bit DaemonSet for K8s Logs

The monitoring module sets up CloudWatch dashboards displaying key metrics (CPU, memory, request rates, error counts), alarms that trigger SNS notifications (CPU > 80%, 5xx rate > 5%, RDS connections > 80%), and Fluent Bit running as a DaemonSet on every K8s node to ship container logs to CloudWatch Logs.

modules/monitoring/main.tf — CloudWatch Alarms + SNS
resource "aws_sns_topic" "alerts" {
  name = "${var.name}-alerts"
}

resource "aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm" "cpu_high" {
  alarm_name          = "${var.name}-cpu-high"
  comparison_operator = "GreaterThanThreshold"
  evaluation_periods  = 2
  metric_name         = "CPUUtilization"
  namespace           = "AWS/EC2"
  period              = 300
  statistic           = "Average"
  threshold           = 80
  alarm_description   = "CPU utilization exceeded 80% for 10 minutes"
  alarm_actions       = [aws_sns_topic.alerts.arn]
}

resource "aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm" "high_5xx" {
  alarm_name          = "${var.name}-high-5xx"
  comparison_operator = "GreaterThanThreshold"
  evaluation_periods  = 2
  metric_name         = "HTTPCode_Target_5XX_Count"
  namespace           = "AWS/ApplicationELB"
  period              = 300
  statistic           = "Sum"
  threshold           = 10
  alarm_actions       = [aws_sns_topic.alerts.arn]
}

resource "aws_cloudwatch_log_group" "k8s_app" {
  name              = "/aws/eks/${var.cluster_name}/application"
  retention_in_days = 30
  tags              = var.tags
}

# Fluent Bit DaemonSet configuration (deployed via Helm or kubectl)
# fluent-bit runs on EVERY node, tails /var/log/containers/*.log
# and forwards to CloudWatch Logs group above.
# Helm values:
# config:
#   outputs: |
#     [OUTPUT]
#         Name cloudwatch_logs
#         Match *
#         region us-east-1
#         log_group_name /aws/eks/anihpj/application
#         log_stream_prefix jobpost-
CPU > 80%
Critical Alarm
5xx > 10/min
Error Alarm
RDS Conn > 80%

30 days

13.11 Root Module — Tying All anihpj/jobpost Infrastructure Together

The root module orchestrates all 10 child modules, passing outputs from one to inputs of another. This is where the full architecture comes together in one main.tf.

Root Module — main.tf (Orchestrating 10 Modules)
# ═══ NETWORKING (foundation — everything depends on this) ═══
module "networking" {
  source       = "./modules/networking"
  vpc_cidr     = "10.0.0.0/16"
  subnet_count = 3
  enable_nat   = true
  name         = "anihpj"
}

# ═══ COMPUTE (EKS — needs VPC + subnets) ═══
module "compute" {
  source             = "./modules/compute"
  cluster_name       = "anihpj-${terraform.workspace}"
  vpc_id             = module.networking.vpc_id
  private_subnet_ids = module.networking.private_subnet_ids
  instance_type      = var.environment == "prod" ? "t3.medium" : "t3.small"
  min_size           = var.environment == "prod" ? 3 : 1
  max_size           = var.environment == "prod" ? 10 : 3
}

# ═══ CONTAINER (ECR — needs nothing from other modules) ═══
module "container" {
  source = "./modules/container"
}

# ═══ DATABASE (RDS — needs private subnets) ═══
module "database" {
  source             = "./modules/database"
  name               = "anihpj-db"
  private_subnet_ids = module.networking.private_subnet_ids
  multi_az           = var.environment == "prod"
  storage_gb         = var.environment == "prod" ? 100 : 20
}

# ═══ KUBERNETES (Deployment — needs EKS + ECR + RDS) ═══
module "kubernetes" {
  source             = "./modules/kubernetes"
  namespace          = "anihpj-${terraform.workspace}"
  ecr_repository_url = module.container.repository_url
  image_tag          = var.image_tag
  db_host            = module.database.address
  db_password        = var.db_password
  replicas           = var.environment == "prod" ? 3 : 1
}

13.12 Environment Strategy — dev/staging/prod with Workspaces & K8s Namespaces

ENVIRONMENT STRATEGY — Directory Layout with Full Isolation
  anihpj-terraform/
  ├── modules/              ◀── Shared module code (DRY — write once)
  │   ├── networking/       (VPC, Subnets, Route Tables, NAT, SG)
  │   ├── compute/          (EKS Cluster, Managed Node Groups)
  │   ├── container/        (ECR Repository, Lifecycle Policies)
  │   ├── kubernetes/       (Deployment, Service, Ingress)
  │   ├── database/         (RDS PostgreSQL, Parameter Groups)
  │   ├── storage/          (S3 Buckets, CloudFront CDN)
  │   ├── caching/          (ElastiCache Redis)
  │   ├── dns/              (Route 53, ACM Certificates)
  │   ├── iam/              (IRSA Roles, Least-Privilege Policies)
  │   └── monitoring/       (CloudWatch Alarms, Fluent Bit)
  │
  ├── dev/                  ◀── Dev environment (separate AWS account!)
  │   ├── main.tf           ◀── Calls modules with dev config
  │   ├── dev.tfvars        ◀── t3.small, 1 AZ, no Multi-AZ
  │   └── backend.tf        ◀── S3: anihpj-tfstate-dev + DynamoDB
  │
  ├── staging/              ◀── Staging environment (separate account)
  │   ├── main.tf           ◀── Calls modules with staging config
  │   ├── staging.tfvars    ◀── t3.medium, 2 AZs, Multi-AZ RDS
  │   └── backend.tf        ◀── S3: anihpj-tfstate-staging
  │
  └── prod/                 ◀── Production (ISOLATED account!)
      ├── main.tf           ◀── Calls modules with prod config
      ├── prod.tfvars       ◀── t3.large, 3 AZs, Multi-AZ, backups
      └── backend.tf        ◀── S3: anihpj-tfstate-prod + DynamoDB

  ✅ Full AWS account isolation (separate state, auth, IAM)
  ✅ Different instance sizes per env (cost optimization)
  ✅ K8s namespaces mirror Terraform workspaces for consistency
  ⚠️  More files to manage, but blast radius is contained
🧠 Exam Strategy: Workspaces vs Directory Layout

Workspaces = same code, same backend, ${terraform.workspace} changes behavior. Simpler but less isolated. Directory Layout = separate directories per environment, separate state files, separate AWS accounts. More complex but production-grade isolation. The exam may ask which approach to use — the answer depends on isolation requirements. For production, directory layout with separate accounts is the best practice.

📝 Chapter 13 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions

Q1
How does the compute module get the VPC ID it needs for EKS deployment?
Show Answer
Answer

Through the networking module's output: module.networking.vpc_id. The root module passes this as an input to the compute module — cross-module data flow via outputs.

Explanation

Root module wires everything: module.networking.vpc_id → module.compute variable. This is the standard pattern — modules never reference each other directly. Only the root orchestrates data flow between modules.

Q2
What is IRSA and why is it better than attaching IAM policies to the node group?
Show Answer
Answer

IRSA = IAM Roles for Service Accounts. Grants AWS permissions to specific K8s pods via annotated Service Accounts, not to the entire node (which all pods share). Least-privilege for K8s.

Explanation

Without IRSA: all pods on a node share the node's IAM role = overly broad. With IRSA: each pod gets its own IAM role via its Service Account. Terraform creates the IAM role + OIDC provider + trust policy.

Q3
What are the two main environment strategy patterns for Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

Workspaces (same code, ${terraform.workspace} toggles behavior) and Directory Layout (separate directories per environment with separate state and AWS accounts).

Explanation

Workspaces = simpler, shared backend. Directory layout = stronger isolation, separate backends. TFC workspaces = best of both. Exam: "Which provides stronger isolation?" → Directory layout or TFC workspaces (separate state per env).

Q4
Why is the networking module always created FIRST in a modular architecture?
Show Answer
Answer

Every other module depends on it — compute needs VPC/subnet IDs, database needs private subnets, K8s needs the VPC. It's the foundation layer. Terraform orders creation automatically via the dependency graph.

Explanation

You don't need depends_on for this! When module.compute references module.networking.vpc_id, Terraform automatically infers the ordering. The dependency graph handles parallel creation of independent modules.

Q5
What is the purpose of the aws_ecr_lifecycle_policy in the container module?
Show Answer
Answer

To automatically expire old Docker images, keeping only the last N. Prevents unlimited ECR storage growth and cost.

Explanation

ECR charges per GB stored. A lifecycle policy auto-deletes old untagged images. Example: keep last 10 images, expire anything older. Without it, every CI/CD push accumulates storage costs indefinitely.

Q6
How do K8s pods securely access AWS services (like S3) without hardcoding credentials?
Show Answer
Answer

Through IRSA — the pod's Service Account is annotated with an IAM role ARN. AWS STS exchanges the K8s service account token for temporary AWS credentials via OIDC federation.

Explanation
# Terraform creates the trust relationship:
resource "aws_iam_role" "s3_access" { ... }
# K8s Service Account annotation:
apiVersion: v1; kind: ServiceAccount
metadata: { annotations: { "eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn": "arn:aws:iam::..." } }

No passwords, no access keys stored anywhere. Temporary credentials rotated automatically.

Q7
In the root module, what pattern provides different instance sizes for dev vs prod?
Show Answer
Answer

Conditional expressions: instance_type = var.environment == "prod" ? "t3.medium" : "t3.small". Use lookup() with a map for 3+ environments.

Explanation
# Ternary (2 envs):
instance_type = var.env == "prod" ? "t3.large" : "t3.micro"
# lookup() map (3+ envs):
instance_type = lookup({ dev="t3.micro"; staging="t3.small"; prod="t3.large" }, var.env, "t3.micro")
Q8
What modules would you need to deploy a simple Django app with PostgreSQL on EKS?
Show Answer
Answer

Minimum 5: networking (VPC), compute (EKS), container (ECR), kubernetes (Deployment), database (RDS). Add storage, caching, DNS, IAM, monitoring for full production readiness (10 total).

Explanation

Core 5 modules = every Django-on-EKS deployment. The remaining 5 are production necessities: S3 (static files), ElastiCache (Redis), Route53 (DNS), IAM (permissions), CloudWatch (monitoring). Start minimal, add as you grow.

Q9
Why is lifecycle { prevent_destroy = true } used on RDS and ECR modules?
Show Answer
Answer

To prevent accidental deletion of the database (data loss) and container registry (image loss). Acts as a safety lock — must be removed before destruction.

Explanation

prevent_destroy = true blocks terraform destroy — it will ERROR. To actually destroy: remove the lifecycle block first, apply, then destroy. This double-confirmation prevents catastrophic mistakes on stateful resources.

Q10
How does Fluent Bit in the monitoring module ship K8s logs to CloudWatch?
Show Answer
Answer

Fluent Bit runs as a DaemonSet (one pod per node), tails container logs from /var/log/containers, and forwards to CloudWatch Logs groups via the CloudWatch output plugin.

Part 6: Terraform Cloud & Enterprise — Collaboration at Scale

Terraform CLI is perfect for solo developers. But when you have a team of 10 engineers managing infrastructure across 3 environments, you need Terraform Cloud (TFC) — HashiCorp's SaaS platform that adds remote execution, policy-as-code (Sentinel), VCS integration, cost estimation, and team governance on top of the Terraform workflow you already know. In Part 6, you'll learn how to set up TFC workspaces for anihpj/jobpost, enforce security policies before every apply, chain workspace runs, and manage collaboration across your organization.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~3 hours 📖 Ch 14-15 🎯 Domain 6

Chapter 14: Terraform Cloud — Team-Based Infrastructure as Code 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 14: Terraform Cloud — From Solo CLI to Team Collaboration

Terraform Cloud (TFC) takes the CLI workflow you've mastered and adds team superpowers: remote plan/apply execution (no more "it works on my laptop"), VCS-driven speculative plans on pull requests, Sentinel policy-as-code to block insecure changes, cost estimation before you apply, and a private module registry for your organization. For the exam, TFC is Domain 6 (~10%, ~6 questions) — and understanding the three run modes (VCS/API/CLI) is essential for anihpj/jobpost team deployments.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 8 Sections 🎯 Domain 6
🎓 TF Associate Relevance
Domain 6: Terraform Cloud ~10%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand how TFC differs from the CLI — remote execution, managed state, team governance
  • ✅ Create and configure TFC workspaces for anihpj dev/staging/prod environments
  • ✅ Set up VCS integration for speculative plans on pull requests (GitHub/GitLab)
  • ✅ Write Sentinel policies to enforce security, compliance, and cost controls
TFC = Domain 6 (~10%, ~6 Questions) — Know the 3 Run Modes

The exam tests: (1) how TFC differs from CLI — remote execution, managed state, Sentinel, (2) the three run modes: VCS-driven (plan on PR, apply on merge), API-driven (CI/CD pipeline triggers), CLI-driven (terraform apply from your laptop triggers remote execution), and (3) Sentinel policy levels: advisory, soft-mandatory, hard-mandatory.

14.1 Terraform Cloud vs CLI — What Changes with TFC?

With the CLI, everything happens on your laptop — state, credentials, execution. With TFC, execution moves to the cloud, state is centrally managed, credentials are stored securely, and every run has an audit trail.

CLI vs TERRAFORM CLOUD — Side by Side
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  CLI WORKFLOW (local laptop):                                        ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  💻 YOUR LAPTOP                                                      ║
  ║  ┌───────────┐   ┌───────────┐   ┌───────────────┐                 ║
  ║  │ Write .tf │──▶│ terraform │──▶│ terraform     │                 ║
  ║  │ files     │   │ plan      │   │ apply         │                 ║
  ║  │           │   │ (local)   │   │ (from laptop) │                 ║
  ║  └───────────┘   └───────────┘   └───────────────┘                 ║
  ║  ⚠️ State on laptop or S3    ⚠️ Credentials on laptop              ║
  ║  ⚠️ No approval workflow      ⚠️ No audit trail (who applied?)     ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  TFC WORKFLOW (remote execution):                                    ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  💻 YOUR LAPTOP          ☁️ TERRAFORM CLOUD                         ║
  ║  ┌───────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐    ║
  ║  │ Git Push  │─────────▶│ 1. VCS webhook triggers run          │    ║
  ║  │ (PR open) │          │ 2. Speculative plan executes in TFC  │    ║
  ║  └───────────┘          │ 3. Plan appears in PR comment        │    ║
  ║                         │ 4. Team reviews plan                 │    ║
  ║  👥 TEAM REVIEW         │ 5. Merge PR triggers apply           │    ║
  ║  ┌───────────┐          │ 6. Sentinel policies check           │    ║
  ║  │ Approve PR│─────────▶│ 7. Cost estimate shown               │    ║
  ║  └───────────┘          │ 8. Apply runs in TFC (secure!)       │    ║
  ║                         └──────────────────────────────────────┘    ║
  ║  ✅ State managed in TFC    ✅ Credentials never leave TFC          ║
  ║  ✅ Full audit trail         ✅ Sentinel policy enforcement         ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
FeatureTerraform CLITerraform Cloud
ExecutionOn your laptopRemote (TFC servers)
State StorageLocal or S3 (manual setup)Managed automatically
CredentialsOn your machineEncrypted in TFC (never exposed)
Approval WorkflowNone (self-governed)PR review + policy checks
Policy Enforcement❌ None✅ Sentinel (advisory/soft/hard)
Cost Estimation❌ None✅ Before every apply
Audit Trail❌ Git only✅ Full run history + who triggered

14.2 Workspaces — Isolating Environments (anihpj-dev, staging, prod)

A TFC workspace is a container for a Terraform state and configuration. Each workspace has its own state file, variables, and execution mode. The standard pattern is one workspace per environment per project.

TFC WORKSPACE STRUCTURE — anihpj/jobpost Multi-Environment
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              TERRAFORM CLOUD ORGANIZATION (anihpj)                   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  PROJECT: "anihpj-infrastructure"                              │ │
  │  │                                                                │ │
  │  │  ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐   │ │
  │  │  │ WORKSPACE      │  │ WORKSPACE      │  │ WORKSPACE      │   │ │
  │  │  │ anihpj-dev     │  │ anihpj-staging │  │ anihpj-prod    │   │ │
  │  │  │                │  │                │  │                │   │ │
  │  │  │ Branch: dev    │  │ Branch: main   │  │ Branch: main   │   │ │
  │  │  │ Auto-apply: ON │  │ Auto-apply: ON │  │ Auto-apply: OFF│   │ │
  │  │  │ Sentinel: adv. │  │ Sentinel: soft │  │ Sentinel: hard │   │ │
  │  │  │ Plan: on PR    │  │ Plan: on PR    │  │ Plan: on PR    │   │ │
  │  │  └────────────────┘  └────────────────┘  └────────────────┘   │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 Each workspace = separate state file + separate variable set     │
  │  💡 Sentinel gets stricter as you go dev → staging → prod           │
  │  💡 Prod NEVER auto-applies — requires explicit approval             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │               VARIABLE SETS (shared across workspaces)          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐  ┌────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ AWS Credentials (sensitive)│  │ Common Tags                │ │
  │  │ • AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID        │  │ • Project = "anihpj"       │ │
  │  │ • AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY    │  │ • Team = "platform"        │ │
  │  │ • AWS_REGION = us-east-1   │  │ • ManagedBy = "Terraform"  │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘  └────────────────────────────┘ │
  │  💡 Variable Sets apply to ALL or SELECTED workspaces           │
  │  💡 Sensitive variables are WRITE-ONLY (never displayed)         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

14.3 Remote Execution — Plans & Applies Run in the Cloud

In TFC's remote execution mode, terraform plan and terraform apply don't run on your laptop — they run on TFC's secure infrastructure. Your laptop only sends the configuration; TFC handles everything else.

🔒 Security

Credentials never leave TFC. Your laptop doesn't need AWS keys — TFC injects them into the run environment. No more "oops, I committed my credentials."

⚡ Consistency

Every run uses the same Terraform version, provider versions, and execution environment. No more "it works on my machine" between team members.

📋 Audit

Every plan and apply is recorded: who triggered it, what changed, when it happened. Complete history for compliance and debugging.

14.4 VCS Integration — GitHub/GitLab-Driven Speculative Plans on PR

VCS integration connects your Git repository to TFC. When you open a pull request, TFC automatically runs a speculative plan and posts the results as a PR comment. This is the heart of GitOps for Terraform.

VCS-DRIVEN WORKFLOW — From PR to Apply
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  1. 👤 Developer opens PR (changes main.tf)                          │
  │     │                                                                │
  │     ▼                                                                │
  │  2. 🔗 GitHub sends webhook to TFC                                   │
  │     │                                                                │
  │     ▼                                                                │
  │  3. ☁️ TFC runs SPECULATIVE PLAN (no changes made!)                 │
  │     │  Output: "Plan: 3 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy"          │
  │     │                                                                │
  │     ▼                                                                │
  │  4. 💬 TFC posts plan as GitHub PR comment                            │
  │     │  Team reviews: "Do these changes look right?"                  │
  │     │                                                                │
  │     ▼                                                                │
  │  5. ✅ Team approves and merges PR                                    │
  │     │                                                                │
  │     ▼                                                                │
  │  6. ☁️ TFC runs APPLY on the merged commit                           │
  │     │  Sentinel policies check → cost estimate → apply               │
  │     │                                                                │
  │     ▼                                                                │
  │  7. ✅ Infrastructure updated! TFC stores run history.               │
  │                                                                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🧠 VCS-Driven = The Most Common TFC Run Mode

The exam expects you to know: VCS-driven is the default and most common TFC run mode. Speculative plans run on PR OPEN and PR UPDATE. Applies run on MERGE to the tracked branch. You NEVER need to run terraform plan locally — TFC handles it. This is what makes TFC "GitOps for infrastructure."

14.5 Sentinel — Policy as Code (Cost Control, Security, Compliance)

Sentinel is HashiCorp's Policy as Code framework. It lets you define rules that run before every terraform apply — blocking insecure, non-compliant, or expensive changes. Think of it as a firewall for your infrastructure.

SENTINEL POLICY FLOW — Guardrails for Every Apply
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  terraform plan → SENTINEL CHECK → Pass? → terraform apply          ║
  ║                                 │                                    ║
  ║                                 ▼ FAIL                               ║
  ║                           ┌─────────────┐                            ║
  ║                           │ 🚫 BLOCKED  │                            ║
  ║                           │ Policy      │                            ║
  ║                           │ violation!  │                            ║
  ║                           └─────────────┘                            ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  EXAMPLE SENTINEL POLICIES FOR ANIHPJ:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. COST:    Block instances > t3.large (max $100/month)             │
  │ 2. SECURITY: Require encryption on all S3 buckets                   │
  │ 3. SECURITY: Block public S3 buckets                                │
  │ 4. COMPLIANCE: All resources must have "Owner" and "Environment" tags│
  │ 5. NETWORK: Block 0.0.0.0/0 in security groups                      │
  │ 6. REGIONS: Restrict to us-east-1 and us-west-2 only                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🟡 Advisory

"This is a suggestion." Warning only — apply proceeds regardless. Use for: best practice reminders, cost awareness.

🟠 Soft-Mandatory

"Override possible with approval." Can be overridden by authorized users. Use for: most production policies.

🔴 Hard-Mandatory

"NO OVERRIDE — apply is BLOCKED." Cannot be bypassed. Use for: security-critical rules, compliance requirements.

14.6 Cost Estimation — Before You Apply, Know What It Costs

TFC can estimate your monthly cloud costs before you apply. It analyzes the planned resources and calculates the approximate monthly bill using public cloud pricing data. This prevents "$10,000 surprise bills."

COST ESTIMATION — See the Bill Before You Pay
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  $ terraform plan (with TFC cost estimation)                        ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  Plan: 5 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy                          ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   ║
  ║  │                   COST ESTIMATE                              │   ║
  ║  │                                                              │   ║
  ║  │  aws_instance.anihpj[0]  t3.medium   $30.29/month           │   ║
  ║  │  aws_instance.anihpj[1]  t3.medium   $30.29/month           │   ║
  ║  │  aws_instance.anihpj[2]  t3.medium   $30.29/month           │   ║
  ║  │  aws_db_instance.pg      db.t3.medium $78.84/month           │   ║
  ║  │  aws_elasticache.redis   cache.t3.micro $23.36/month         │   ║
  ║  │                                                              │   ║

14.7 Run Modes — VCS-Driven, API-Driven, CLI-Driven

ModeTriggerPlan Runs OnBest For
VCS-Driven Git push / PR open / merge TFC servers Standard GitOps workflow — most common mode
API-Driven TFC API call / CI pipeline TFC servers Custom CI/CD pipelines, external orchestration
CLI-Driven terraform plan/apply from local TFC servers (remote) Migration path from local CLI, ad-hoc runs
🧠 CLI-Driven ≠ Local Execution

In CLI-driven mode, even though you type terraform plan on your laptop, the actual execution STILL happens in TFC. Your laptop sends the config to TFC; TFC runs plan/apply and sends results back. This is different from pure local CLI where everything runs on your machine.

14.8 Notifications — Slack, Email, Webhooks on Run Completion

TFC can send notifications to Slack, email, or custom webhooks when runs complete — success or failure. This keeps the team informed without checking the TFC dashboard constantly.

💬 Slack

Post run results to a Slack channel. Include plan summary, apply status, and who triggered it.

📧 Email

Send run notifications to specific email addresses. Useful for team leads and compliance officers.

🔗 Webhooks

POST JSON payload to any HTTP endpoint. Integrate with PagerDuty, ServiceNow, or custom systems.

📝 Chapter 14 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions

Q1
What are the three run modes in Terraform Cloud?
Show Answer
Answer

VCS-Driven (plan on PR, apply on merge), API-Driven (triggered via TFC API), CLI-Driven (triggered from local terraform commands but executed remotely).

Explanation

VCS-driven is the most common — it's GitOps for Terraform. API-driven is for custom CI/CD pipelines. CLI-driven is a migration-friendly option where local commands trigger remote execution.

Q2
What is a speculative plan in TFC and when does it run?
Show Answer
Answer

A speculative plan runs on PR open/update in VCS-driven mode. It shows what WOULD change if merged, but makes NO changes — read-only preview.

Explanation

Speculative plans are the heart of TFC GitOps. Dev opens PR → TFC auto-runs plan → plan output as PR comment → team reviews → merge → TFC applies. No local Terraform needed.

Q3
What are the three Sentinel enforcement levels?
Show Answer
Answer

Advisory (warning only, apply proceeds), Soft-Mandatory (can override with approval), Hard-Mandatory (NO override — apply is blocked).

Explanation

Soft-mandatory = approver CAN override. Hard-mandatory = NO ONE can override (not even org owners). Use hard-mandatory for: block public S3 buckets, require encryption, restrict regions. These are non-negotiable security rules.

Q4
In TFC, where do Terraform plan and apply actually execute?
Show Answer
Answer

On TFC's remote infrastructure, NOT on your laptop. Your laptop sends the config; TFC runs plan/apply in its secure execution environment.

Explanation

Even CLI-driven mode executes remotely — you type terraform apply locally but TFC runs it on their servers. This means: credentials stay in TFC, consistent execution environment, full audit trail. The exam tests this: "Where does execution happen?" → TFC servers.

Q5
What is the benefit of TFC cost estimation?
Show Answer
Answer

It shows estimated monthly cloud costs BEFORE you apply, preventing surprise bills. Available in Team & Governance tiers.

Explanation

Cost estimation analyzes planned resources against public cloud pricing. Shows per-resource and total monthly cost. Catches expensive mistakes before they hit your bill. Not available in Free tier.

Q6
How many TFC workspaces should you create for anihpj (dev/staging/prod)?
Show Answer
Answer

Three — one per environment. Each has its own state, variables, and Sentinel policy levels (advisory→soft→hard as you go dev→prod).

Explanation

3 workspaces = 3 isolated states. Dev can auto-apply; prod requires manual approval. Sentinel: advisory in dev, soft-mandatory in staging, hard-mandatory in prod. This pattern prevents dev mistakes from reaching production.

Q7
Can you use TFC without VCS integration?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes — use API-driven or CLI-driven modes. VCS is recommended for the full GitOps experience but not required.

Explanation

API-driven: upload config + trigger via API. CLI-driven: terraform login then remote execution. VCS-driven: Git push triggers plan/apply. All three execute on TFC servers — credentials never leave the platform.

Q8
What happens when a Hard-Mandatory Sentinel policy fails during apply?
Show Answer
Answer

The apply is BLOCKED completely — no override possible. The violation must be fixed before the run can proceed.

Explanation

Hard-mandatory = NO override by anyone, not even org owners. Only way forward: fix the violating resource in your .tf code and re-plan. Use for: block public S3, require encryption, restrict regions.

Q9
How does TFC improve security compared to local CLI?
Show Answer
Answer

Credentials never leave TFC (encrypted at rest), Sentinel policies enforce security rules, and all runs have an audit trail.

Explanation

Local CLI: credentials on laptops (risk of theft). TFC: credentials encrypted, never downloaded, never in state (sensitive variables). Every action logged who/when/what. SOC2/ISO27001 compliant.

Q10
What notification channels does TFC support for run completion?
Show Answer
Answer

Slack, Email, and Webhooks (for PagerDuty, ServiceNow, custom integrations). Notifications fire on run success or failure.

Explanation

Webhooks POST JSON payloads to any HTTP endpoint — integrate with anything. Slack posts to channels with run summary. Email notifies specific addresses. Configure per workspace for granular alerting.
