Chapter 15: Collaboration, Governance & Operations 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 15: Collaboration, Governance & Operations — Running TFC at Scale

TFC is more than just remote execution — it's a full collaboration platform for infrastructure teams. This chapter covers team RBAC, run triggers for chaining workspaces, private module registries, API automation, audit logs, SSO integration, and agent pools for running Terraform on your own infrastructure. These are the operational features that make TFC production-ready for organizations managing anihpj/jobpost across multiple teams.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 7 Sections 🎯 Domain 6

15.1 Team Management — RBAC, Organization-Level Permissions

TFC supports Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) at the organization and workspace level. You can grant different permissions to different teams — ops gets full access, developers get read-only, auditors get log access only.

RolePermissions
OwnerFull control — manage workspaces, teams, settings, billing
AdminManage workspaces, variables, run triggers. Cannot manage billing.
WriteQueue plans, approve applies, read state. Cannot change settings.
ReadView workspace, read runs, read state. Cannot trigger apply.

15.2 Run Triggers — Chaining Workspace Executions

Run triggers let you chain workspace executions. When Workspace A (networking) completes successfully, Workspace B (compute) automatically starts. This ensures infrastructure layers are always updated in the correct order.

RUN TRIGGERS — Networking → Compute
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐     TRIGGERS      ┌─────────────────┐          │
  │  │ anihpj-networking│─────────────────▶│  anihpj-compute  │          │
  │  │ (Workspace A)   │                   │  (Workspace B)  │          │
  │  │                 │                   │                 │          │
  │  │ 1. VPC updated  │                   │ 2. EC2 gets new │          │
  │  │ 2. New subnets  │                   │    subnet IDs   │          │
  │  │ 3. Apply done ✅│                   │ 3. Apply with   │          │
  │  │                 │                   │    new VPC info  │          │
  │  └─────────────────┘                   └─────────────────┘          │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 Workspace B automatically plans/applies when A succeeds!         │
  │  💡 No manual coordination needed — TFC handles the dependency chain │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

15.3 Private Module Registry — Sharing Modules Within Your Organization

TFC includes a private module registry scoped to your organization. Teams can publish modules internally with the same workflow as the public registry — but only visible within your org. Perfect for sharing the anihpj networking, compute, and database modules across multiple projects and teams.

🔒 Private

Modules are visible only within your organization. No public exposure. Perfect for proprietary infrastructure patterns and internal best practices.

📦 Same Workflow

Publish and consume modules exactly like the public registry. source = "app.terraform.io/my-org/vpc/aws" with version = "~> 1.0".

15.4 API-Driven Workflows — Automating Terraform with the TFC API

The TFC API lets you trigger runs, fetch plan outputs, and manage workspaces programmatically. This enables deep CI/CD integration — your Jenkins/GitHub Actions pipeline can call the TFC API to create a run, wait for the plan, and approve the apply.

15.5 Audit Logs — Complete History of Who Changed What & When

Every action in TFC is logged with timestamp, user identity, and details. This provides full compliance traceability — essential for SOC2, HIPAA, and PCI-DSS environments.

EventWhat's LoggedWhy It Matters
Run triggeredWho triggered, source (VCS/API/CLI), commit SHATrace every infra change to a person and PR
Plan/Apply completedResources changed (+/-/~), duration, cost estimateAudit what actually changed in production
Variable changedWhich variable, old value (masked if sensitive), whoTrack configuration changes over time
Policy modifiedWhich Sentinel policy, what changed, whoSecurity compliance — who weakened the guardrails?
State accessedWho read state, when, from whereDetect unauthorized state access

15.6 Single Sign-On (SSO) — SAML, OIDC Integration

TFC supports enterprise SSO via SAML and OIDC. Integrate with Okta, Azure AD, or any identity provider. Teams and permissions can map directly from your IdP groups — when someone joins the "platform-engineering" group in Okta, they automatically get Write access to the anihpj workspaces.

15.7 Agent Pools — Running Terraform on Your Own Infrastructure

By default, TFC runs plans and applies on HashiCorp's infrastructure. Agent pools let you run Terraform on YOUR infrastructure — in your VPC, behind your firewall, with access to private resources that TFC can't reach directly.

AGENT POOLS — Your Infrastructure, TFC Control Plane
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  DEFAULT (TFC-hosted):          AGENT POOLS (your infrastructure):  ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  ☁️ Terraform Cloud             🏢 YOUR VPC / DATACENTER            ║
  ║  ┌──────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────────────┐    ║
  ║  │ Run terraform on     │      │ TFC Agent (Docker container) │    ║
  ║  │ HashiCorp's servers  │      │ → Runs terraform plan/apply  │    ║
  ║  │                      │      │ → Connects back to TFC       │    ║
  ║  │ ✅ Nothing to manage │      │ → Accesses private resources │    ║
  ║  │ ❌ Can't reach       │      │                              │    ║
  ║  │    private VPCs      │      │ ✅ Full VPC/private access   │    ║
  ║  └──────────────────────┘      │ ❌ Must manage the agent     │    ║
  ║                                └──────────────────────────────┘    ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

📝 Chapter 15 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions

Q1
What is a run trigger in TFC?
Show Answer
Answer

A mechanism to chain workspace executions — when Workspace A succeeds, Workspace B automatically runs. Used for infrastructure layer dependencies (networking → compute).

Explanation

Run triggers auto-chain workspaces: A completes → B starts. No manual coordination. Use for layer dependencies (networking→compute→app). TFC handles the dependency chain.

Q2
What are the four RBAC roles in TFC?
Show Answer
Answer

Owner (full control), Admin (manage, no billing), Write (queue/approve runs), Read (view only).

Explanation

Owner=billing+all perms. Admin=manage workspaces, no billing. Write=queue/approve runs. Read=view only. Map these to your team structure: platform team=Admin, developers=Write, auditors=Read.

Q3
What is a private module registry?
Show Answer
Answer

A TFC-hosted module registry scoped to your organization. Teams publish and consume modules internally with the same workflow as the public registry.

Explanation

Private registry = modules visible only within your org. Same source format: app.terraform.io/my-org/vpc/aws. Supports versioning, same as public registry. No public exposure.

Q4
How does TFC audit logging help with compliance?
Show Answer
Answer

Every action is logged: who triggered runs, who approved applies, who changed variables. Full traceability for SOC2, HIPAA, PCI-DSS.

Explanation

Audit logs capture: who triggered run, who approved apply, variable changes (old/new values), policy modifications. Immutable, exportable, API-accessible. Essential for compliance audits.

Q5
What SSO protocols does TFC support?
Show Answer
Answer

SAML and OIDC. Integrates with Okta, Azure AD, and any SAML/OIDC identity provider. Teams map from IdP groups.

Explanation

SAML = traditional enterprise SSO. OIDC = modern cloud-native auth. Both support group mapping: "platform-team" group in Okta → Admin team in TFC. No separate TFC user management needed.

Q6
Why would you use TFC agent pools instead of TFC-hosted execution?
Show Answer
Answer

To run Terraform on YOUR infrastructure — in your VPC, behind your firewall, with access to private resources (internal databases, private APIs) that TFC can't reach.

Explanation

Agent pools bridge the gap: TFC control plane + your execution environment. The agent is a Docker container that phones home to TFC. Use when you need VPC access, private endpoints, or on-prem connectivity.

Q7
How does the TFC API enable CI/CD integration?
Show Answer
Answer

CI/CD pipelines (Jenkins, GitHub Actions) call the TFC API to create runs, fetch plan outputs, and approve applies programmatically.

Explanation

TFC API is RESTful: create run → wait for plan → get plan output → approve → wait for apply → get result. Full CI/CD without local Terraform. Use hashicorp/tfc GitHub Action for native integration.

Q8
What happens when you link your IdP groups to TFC teams?
Show Answer
Answer

Permissions are automatically managed. When someone joins the "platform-engineering" group in Okta, they automatically get the corresponding TFC team permissions.

Explanation

SSO + IdP group mapping = zero manual permission management. When someone leaves the company (removed from Okta), they lose TFC access automatically. When they join a team, they get the right TFC role instantly.

Q9
Can you trigger a TFC run via API without using VCS integration?
Show Answer
Answer

Yes — the API-driven run mode lets you trigger runs programmatically without Git/VCS. Configuration is uploaded directly via the API.

Explanation

API-driven = no Git needed. Upload a .tar.gz of your config via the Configuration Versions API, then create a run. Great for CI/CD systems that already package configs. Full TFC features (Sentinel, cost est.) still apply.

Q10
What's the main operational difference between TFC-hosted agents and agent pools?
Show Answer
Answer

TFC-hosted: nothing to manage, but can't reach private resources. Agent pools: you manage the agent, but it runs in YOUR network with access to private VPCs and internal services.

Explanation

Agent pools = Docker container running in your VPC. It phones home to TFC for instructions. Trade-off: you must patch, monitor, and scale the agent yourself. TFC-hosted = zero maintenance but limited network access.

Chapter 16: CI/CD with Terraform — Automated Infrastructure Delivery 🔴 Advanced

Chapter 16: CI/CD with Terraform — From Manual CLI to Fully Automated Pipelines

Running terraform apply from your laptop works — until it doesn't. CI/CD pipelines automate the entire Terraform workflow: lint → validate → plan → approve → apply. Every change goes through a pull request with automated plan output. No more "I forgot to run terraform fmt" or "who applied that change directly to production?" This chapter covers the full CI/CD stack for anihpj/jobpost — from GitHub Actions to Atlantis to drift detection.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 8 Sections 🚀 CI/CD

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Set up GitHub Actions pipelines for automated Terraform plan on PR and apply on merge
  • ✅ Configure GitLab CI for Terraform with environment-scoped pipelines
  • ✅ Implement the Terraform testing pyramid — tflint, tfsec, checkov, terratest
  • ✅ Detect and remediate infrastructure drift automatically
  • ✅ Design multi-environment deployment strategies with approval gates

16.1 Terraform in GitHub Actions — Automated Plan on Pull Request

GitHub Actions is the most popular CI/CD platform for Terraform. The pattern: plan on PR → review → merge → apply. Every PR gets a speculative plan posted as a comment. No manual CLI needed.

GITHUB ACTIONS + TERRAFORM — Full Pipeline Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    GITHUB ACTIONS CI/CD FOR TERRAFORM                │
  │                                                                      │
  │  DEVELOPER                  GITHUB ACTIONS                           │
  │  ┌──────────┐              ┌────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ 1. Edit  │              │  On: pull_request                   │   │
  │  │   .tf    │─────────────▶│  ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │   │
  │  │   files  │   git push   │  │ Job: terraform-plan           │  │   │
  │  └──────────┘              │  │ ├─ Checkout code              │  │   │
  │                            │  │ ├─ Setup Terraform            │  │   │
  │  ┌──────────┐              │  │ ├─ terraform fmt -check       │  │   │
  │  │ 2. Open  │              │  │ ├─ terraform validate         │  │   │
  │  │   PR     │─────────────▶│  │ ├─ tflint                     │  │   │
  │  └──────────┘              │  │ ├─ tfsec                      │  │   │
  │                            │  │ ├─ terraform plan             │  │   │
  │  ┌──────────┐              │  │ └─ Post plan as PR comment    │  │   │
  │  │ 3. Review│◀─────────────│  └──────────────────────────────┘  │   │
  │  │   PR     │  plan output │                                      │   │
  │  └──────────┘              │  On: push to main                   │   │
  │       │                    │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │   │
  │       │ MERGE              │  │ Job: terraform-apply          │  │   │
  │       └───────────────────▶│  │ ├─ terraform init             │  │   │
  │                            │  │ ├─ terraform apply -auto-approve│  │   │
  │                            │  │ └─ Notify Slack ✅            │  │   │
  │                            │  └──────────────────────────────┘  │   │
  │                            └────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
.github/workflows/terraform.yml
name: Terraform CI/CD
on:
  pull_request: { branches: [main] }
  push:        { branches: [main] }

jobs:
  terraform-plan:
    if: github.event_name == 'pull_request'
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: hashicorp/setup-terraform@v3
        with: { terraform_version: "1.7" }
      - run: terraform fmt -check -recursive
      - run: terraform init
      - run: terraform validate
      - run: terraform plan -no-color -out=tfplan
      - uses: actions/github-script@v7
        with:
          script: |
            const plan = require('fs').readFileSync('tfplan','utf8')
            github.rest.issues.createComment({
              issue_number: context.issue.number,
              owner: context.repo.owner, repo: context.repo.repo,
              body: `### Terraform Plan\n\`\`\`\n${plan}\n\`\`\`` })

16.2 Terraform in GitLab CI — Apply on Merge to Main Branch

GitLab CI offers native Terraform integration with its Managed Terraform State and pipeline templates. The pattern is identical to GitHub Actions but uses GitLab's .gitlab-ci.yml syntax.

.gitlab-ci.yml — Terraform Pipeline
variables:
  TF_ROOT: ${CI_PROJECT_DIR}

stages: [validate, plan, apply]

terraform-validate:
  stage: validate
  script: [terraform fmt -check, terraform init, terraform validate]

terraform-plan:
  stage: plan
  script: [terraform plan -out=tfplan]
  artifacts: { paths: [tfplan] }
  only: [merge_requests]

terraform-apply:
  stage: apply
  script: [terraform apply tfplan]
  only: [main]
  when: manual  # ← Manual approval gate for production!

16.3 Testing Terraform — tflint, tfsec, checkov, terratest

Terraform configurations are code — and code needs testing. The Terraform testing pyramid ranges from fast static analysis to full integration tests that actually deploy and destroy infrastructure.

THE TERRAFORM TESTING PYRAMID
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                  │
  │                      ┌─────────┐                                 │
  │                      │ terratest│  ◀── Integration tests (Go)    │
  │                      │ (Go)    │       Deploy → verify → destroy │
  │                      └────┬────┘                                  │
  │                           │                                       │
  │                    ┌──────┴──────┐                                │
  │                    │   checkov   │  ◀── Policy scanning (Python) │
  │                    │   (Python)  │       Security + compliance   │
  │                    └──────┬──────┘                                │
  │                           │                                       │
  │              ┌────────────┼────────────┐                          │
  │              │            │            │                          │
  │        ┌─────┴─────┐ ┌───┴────┐ ┌─────┴─────┐                   │
  │        │   tfsec   │ │ tflint │ │terraform │                    │
  │        │ (Security)│ │(Linter)│ │ validate │                    │
  │        └───────────┘ └────────┘ └──────────┘                   │
  │         Static Analysis       Syntax Check                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💡 Run FAST tests first (fmt, validate, lint) in CI            │
  │  💡 Run terratest on schedule (nightly) or pre-release          │
  │  💡 checkov scans for CIS, PCI-DSS, HIPAA compliance            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
ToolTypeWhat It ChecksSpeed
terraform fmtFormatterCode style, indentation⚡ Instant
terraform validateValidatorHCL syntax, resource references⚡ Fast
tflintLinterBest practices, deprecated syntax, provider errors⚡ Fast
tfsecSecurity ScannerAWS/Azure/GCP security misconfigurations⚡ Fast
checkovPolicy ScannerCompliance (CIS, PCI, HIPAA), security, cost🟡 Medium
terratestIntegration TestEnd-to-end: deploy → verify → destroy🔴 Slow (minutes)

16.4 Drift Detection — When Reality Diverges from State

Drift happens when someone changes infrastructure outside Terraform — through the AWS console, another automation tool, or a disaster recovery event. terraform plan is your drift detection tool: it compares desired state (.tf) against actual state (real infra) and shows the difference.

DRIFT DETECTION — terraform plan IS your drift detector
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  WHAT IS DRIFT? Someone changes infrastructure OUTSIDE Terraform:   ║
  ║  • Manual console change (someone clicked in AWS UI)                ║
  ║  • Another automation tool modified a resource                      ║
  ║  • Disaster recovery restored from backup with different config     ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  HOW TO DETECT (terraform plan = drift detector):                   ║
  ║  $ terraform plan                                                   ║
  ║  Plan: 0 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy  ← Drift detected!      ║
  ║  ~ resource "aws_security_group" "anihpj" {                         ║
  ║      ~ ingress { from_port = 80 → 443  ← Someone changed this!     ║
  ║    }                                                                ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  HOW TO PREVENT:                                                     ║
  ║  1. NEVER manually edit Terraform-managed resources                 ║
  ║  2. Use IAM policies to BLOCK console access to prod                ║
  ║  3. Run terraform plan on SCHEDULE (daily CI job = drift check)     ║
  ║  4. TFC: enable "Automatic Drift Detection"                         ║
  ║  5. Alert: webhook → Slack when scheduled plan shows changes        ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  💡 terraform plan = drift detection (no changes made)              ║
  ║  💡 terraform apply = drift correction (reverts to desired state)   ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

16.5 Multi-Environment Strategies — Workspaces vs Branches vs Directories

StrategyHow It WorksProsCons
Workspaces${terraform.workspace} toggles behaviorSimple, same codeLess isolation, shared backend
Directory Layoutdev/, staging/, prod/ directoriesFull isolationMore files, code duplication risk
Git Branchdev branch → dev, main → prodGit-native workflowComplex merging, state confusion
TFC WorkspacesOne TFC workspace per environmentBest of all worldsRequires TFC (paid tier)

16.6 Approval Gates — Manual Approval Before Production Apply

Production changes should NEVER auto-apply. Approval gates insert a manual review step between plan and apply. The approver reviews the plan output and explicitly approves before infrastructure is modified.

APPROVAL GATE WORKFLOW
  DEVELOPER          TERRAFORM CLOUD              APPROVER
  ─────────          ───────────────              ────────
  git push ──────▶  Speculative Plan
                         │
                         ▼
                   Plan succeeded
                   (3 adds, 1 change)
                         │
                         ▼
                   ┌──────────────┐
                   │ WAITING FOR  │────────▶  "Plan ready. Approve?"
                   │ APPROVAL     │
                   └──────────────┘
                         │
                         ▼
                   ┌──────────────┐    ◀────  APPROVER clicks
                   │ APPROVED!    │           "Approve & Apply"
                   └──────┬───────┘
                          │
                          ▼
                   terraform apply ✅

  💡 Sentinel policies run BEFORE the approval step
  💡 soft-mandatory can be overridden by approver
  💡 hard-mandatory = NO override possible

16.7 Plan Storage — Saving & Comparing Plans Across CI Runs

Storing plan files enables comparison across runs: "What changed between this plan and yesterday's?" TFC stores plan outputs automatically. For CLI-based workflows, upload plan files to S3 or store as CI artifacts.

16.8 Atlantis — Pull Request Automation for Terraform

Atlantis is an open-source Terraform bot for pull requests. You comment atlantis plan on a PR and Atlantis runs terraform plan, posts the output, and locks the workspace. Comment atlantis apply to apply. No CI/CD pipeline setup needed.

ATLANTIS — "Terraform Bot for Pull Requests"
  Developer comments on PR: "atlantis plan"
  ┌──────────┐
  │ PR #42   │
  │ atlantis │────▶ 🤖 ATLANTIS BOT
  │ plan     │     ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          │     │ 1. Locks the workspace (no DynamoDB!)│
  │ atlantis │     │ 2. Checks out PR branch              │
  │ apply    │────▶│ 3. Runs terraform plan               │
  │          │     │ 4. Posts plan output as PR comment    │
  └──────────┘     │ 5. On "apply": runs terraform apply  │
                   │ 6. Unlocks workspace                 │
                   └──────────────────────────────────────┘

  💡 Atlantis = full Terraform automation without complex CI/CD
  💡 Built-in workspace locking (no DynamoDB needed!)
  💡 Comments on PR: atlantis plan | atlantis apply | atlantis unlock

📝 Chapter 16 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions

Q1
What is the standard CI/CD pattern for Terraform with GitHub Actions?
Show Answer
Answer

Plan on PR, apply on merge. fmt → validate → lint → plan (on PR), then apply (on merge to main).

Explanation

Plan-on-PR = safety net: every PR gets an automated plan output. Team reviews BEFORE merge. Apply-on-merge = automation: merged code auto-deploys. No manual CLI needed.

Q2
What is the Terraform testing pyramid from fastest to slowest?
Show Answer
Answer

fmt/validate (instant) → tflint/tfsec (fast static) → checkov (policy scan) → terratest (integration, slow).

Explanation

Pyramid: fast tests in CI (every commit), slow tests on schedule (nightly). fmt/validate = seconds. tflint/tfsec = seconds. checkov = 30s. terratest = minutes (actually deploys infra).

Q3
How do you detect infrastructure drift with Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

Run terraform plan. It compares desired state (.tf) against real infrastructure and shows differences. Schedule it daily as a CI job.

Explanation

terraform plan IS your drift detector — it compares desired (.tf) vs actual (real infra). Schedule daily: if plan shows changes but nobody committed, that's drift. Alert via webhook → Slack.

Q4
What are the three multi-environment strategies for Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

Workspaces (${terraform.workspace}), Directory Layout (separate directories), Git Branch (branches per env).

Explanation

Workspaces = simple, shared backend. Directory = strong isolation, separate state/accounts. Git branch = Git-native but complex merging. TFC workspaces = best of all worlds with managed state per env.

Q5
What is an approval gate in the context of Terraform CI/CD?
Show Answer
Answer

A manual review step between plan and apply. An approver reviews the plan output and explicitly approves before infrastructure is modified — essential for production.

Explanation

Approval gate = plan completes → enters "waiting for approval" state → approver reviews plan → clicks "Approve & Apply" → apply runs. Sentinel policies run BEFORE the approval step.

Q6
What is Atlantis and how does it differ from a full CI/CD pipeline?
Show Answer
Answer

Atlantis is a Terraform bot triggered by PR comments (atlantis plan/atlantis apply). Simpler than full CI/CD — no pipeline YAML needed.

Explanation

Atlantis = comment-driven Terraform. atlantis plan on PR → bot runs plan → posts output. atlantis apply → bot applies. Built-in workspace locking. Great for teams wanting Terraform automation without CI/CD complexity.

Q7
What tools would you use to scan Terraform for security misconfigurations?
Show Answer
Answer

tfsec (security scanning) and checkov (policy + compliance scanning for CIS, PCI-DSS, HIPAA).

Explanation

tfsec = focused security scanner (misconfigurations). checkov = broader compliance scanner (CIS benchmarks, PCI-DSS, HIPAA, SOC2). Both run as CLI tools or CI pipeline steps. Run BEFORE terraform plan in CI.

Q8
How do you prevent Terraform drift in production?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Never manually edit Terraform-managed resources. 2. Use IAM to block console access. 3. Run scheduled terraform plan. 4. Alert on unexpected changes.

Explanation

Prevention layers: (1) IAM policies block AWS console write access for humans, (2) Only CI/CD can apply changes, (3) Scheduled plan detects any drift that slips through, (4) Slack/PagerDuty alert on unexpected plan results.

Q9
What's the difference between terraform plan and terraform apply regarding drift?
Show Answer
Answer

plan = detects drift (shows differences, no changes). apply = corrects drift (reverts to desired state, makes API calls).

Explanation

Plan = read-only drift detector. Apply = writes changes to fix drift. Think: plan is like git diff, apply is like git commit. Plan shows what changed; apply makes it happen.

Q10
Why wouldn't you auto-apply Terraform changes directly to production?
Show Answer
Answer

Production changes should always have human review. Auto-apply risks deploying untested, unreviewed changes that could cause outages. Use approval gates.

Chapter 17: Terraform at Scale — Patterns for Large Organizations 🔴 Advanced

Chapter 17: Terraform at Scale — Production Patterns for Enterprise Teams

Running Terraform for a 3-person startup is easy. Running it for a 50-person platform engineering team across 50 AWS accounts is a different challenge entirely. This chapter covers the scaling patterns: monorepo vs polyrepo, state file optimization, multi-account strategies, secrets management at scale, and safe version upgrade paths. These patterns are what separate certified practitioners from production-ready Terraform engineers deploying anihpj/jobpost at enterprise scale.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📖 10 Sections 🏢 Enterprise

17.1 Monorepo vs Polyrepo — Structuring Terraform at Scale

MONOREPO vs POLYREPO — Code Organization Patterns
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  MONOREPO (one repo for all Terraform):                              ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  terraform-infra/                     ← ONE Git repo                ║
  ║  ├── modules/       (shared)                                        ║
  ║  ├── dev/           (env)                                           ║
  ║  ├── staging/       (env)                                           ║
  ║  └── prod/          (env)                                           ║
  ║  ✅ Easy code sharing  ✅ Single CI/CD  ❌ Large repo, slow clones  ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  POLYREPO (separate repo per component):                             ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  terraform-modules/    ← Shared modules (tagged releases)            ║
  ║  terraform-networking/ ← VPC + subnets (tagged)                      ║
  ║  terraform-compute/    ← EC2/EKS (tagged)                            ║
  ║  terraform-database/   ← RDS (tagged)                                ║
  ║  ✅ Independent versioning  ✅ Isolated blast radius                 ║
  ║  ❌ Module version coordination  ❌ Multiple CI/CD pipelines         ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

17.2 Remote State Backends — Production Comparison

BackendLockingEncryptionBest For
S3 + DynamoDB✅ Table✅ SSE-KMSAWS-native shops
Terraform Cloud✅ Built-in✅ At-restTeams, CI/CD
GCS❌ Extra✅ DefaultGCP-native shops
Azure Blob✅ Lease✅ DefaultAzure-native shops

17.3 Partial Backend Configuration — Init-Time Config

Hardcoding backend config in backend "s3" { ... } inside terraform {} is inflexible — it can't use variables. Partial configuration lets you pass backend settings at terraform init time, making your code reusable across environments.

Partial Backend — main.tf
terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    # Only the KEY is hardcoded — bucket, region, dynamodb_table
    # are passed via -backend-config at init time.
    key = "anihpj/terraform.tfstate"
  }
}
Init with Backend Config — CI/CD Pipeline
# Dev environment:
terraform init \
  -backend-config="bucket=anihpj-tfstate-dev" \
  -backend-config="region=us-east-1" \
  -backend-config="dynamodb_table=terraform-lock-dev"

# Prod environment (SAME code, different backend!):
terraform init \
  -backend-config="bucket=anihpj-tfstate-prod" \
  -backend-config="region=us-east-1" \
  -backend-config="dynamodb_table=terraform-lock-prod"
🧠 Why This Matters

Without partial configuration, you'd need separate main.tf for each environment — or risk committing prod backend config to the dev repo. Partial config keeps one codebase, many backends. The -backend-config flag can also point to a separate config file: terraform init -backend-config=dev.conf.

17.4 Large State Files — Performance Optimization & State Splitting

As infrastructure grows, state files can exceed 100MB with 1000+ resources — making every plan and apply slow. Strategies to manage this:

StrategyHowWhen
State SplittingSeparate state per service (networking, compute, database)100+ resources in one state
Parallelismterraform apply -parallelism=20Many independent resources
Refresh=falseterraform plan -refresh=falseQuick syntax/ref check
Targeted Applyterraform apply -target=module.xDebugging (not production!)

17.5 Provider Caching — terraform init with Provider Mirror

In CI/CD, downloading providers fresh every run wastes 30-60 seconds. A provider mirror caches binaries locally:

.terraformrc — Provider Mirror Config
provider_installation {
  filesystem_mirror {
    path    = "/opt/terraform-providers"   # ← Pre-downloaded cache
    include = ["hashicorp/*", "terraform-aws-modules/*"]
  }
  direct {
    exclude = ["hashicorp/*"]   # ← Fall through to registry for others
  }
}

# Populate the mirror:
# terraform providers mirror /opt/terraform-providers

17.6 Dependency Inversion — Data Sources for Cross-Stack References

DEPENDENCY INVERSION WITH DATA SOURCES
  NETWORKING TEAM                          APP TEAM
  ┌──────────────────────┐                ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ resource "aws_vpc"   │                │ data "aws_vpc" "shared" {│
  │   "main" {           │                │   filter {               │
  │     tags = {         │                │     name   = "tag:Name"  │
  │       Name =         │───────────────▶│     values = ["shared"]  │
  │       "shared-vpc"   │    Tag-based   │   }                      │
  │     }                │    lookup      │ }                        │
  │   }                  │                │ subnet_id = data.aws_vpc │
  └──────────────────────┘                │   .shared.id             │
                                          └──────────────────────────┘
  ✅ No shared state  ✅ No hardcoded IDs  ✅ Teams fully decoupled

17.7 Terraform in a Multi-Account AWS Strategy

Production teams use separate AWS accounts per environment — never put dev and prod in the same account. Terraform supports this via provider aliases with assume_role: each environment directory assumes a role in its corresponding AWS account. This gives you full blast-radius isolation.

AWS ORGANIZATIONS + TERRAFORM — anihpj at Enterprise Scale
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  AWS ORGANIZATION (Root)                                     │   │
  │  │                                                              │   │
  │  │  ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────┐    │   │
  │  │  │ ACCOUNT:       │  │ ACCOUNT:       │  │ ACCOUNT:   │    │   │
  │  │  │ anihpj-dev     │  │ anihpj-staging │  │ anihpj-prod│    │   │
  │  │  │                │  │                │  │            │    │   │
  │  │  │ Terraform:     │  │ Terraform:     │  │ Terraform: │    │   │
  │  │  │ provider "aws" {│  │ provider "aws" {│  │ provider   │    │   │
  │  │  │  assume_role { │  │  assume_role { │  │ "aws" {    │    │   │
  │  │  │    role_arn =  │  │    role_arn =  │  │  assume_   │    │   │
  │  │  │    "arn:..."   │  │    "arn:..."   │  │  role {    │    │   │
  │  │  │  }             │  │  }             │  │    ...     │    │   │
  │  │  │ }              │  │ }              │  │  }         │    │   │
  │  │  └────────────────┘  └────────────────┘  └────────────┘    │   │
  │  │         │                   │                    │          │   │
  │  └─────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────┘   │
  │            │                   │                    │              │
  │       $ terraform        $ terraform         $ terraform           │
  │       apply (dev/)       apply (staging/)    apply (prod/)         │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 Each account has its OWN state backend (separate S3 bucket)     │
  │  💡 SCPs + IAM guardrails prevent cross-account access              │
  │  💡 No blast radius — dev failure NEVER touches prod                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

17.8 Managing Secrets at Scale — Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault

SECRETS MANAGEMENT HIERARCHY
  🥇 HashiCorp Vault (dynamic secrets, rotation, audit)
  🥈 Cloud Secret Managers (AWS SM, Azure KV, GCP SM)
  🥉 TFC Variable Sets (encrypted, write-only sensitive)
   4. CI/CD Secret Variables (GitHub Secrets, GitLab CI Vars)
  ❌ NEVER: Hardcode in .tf files or commit .tfvars with secrets

17.9 Terraform Registry Mirror — Air-Gapped / Offline Environments

In air-gapped environments (no internet access), the default terraform init fails — it can't reach registry.terraform.io to download providers. The solution: pre-download all providers on an internet-connected machine, then copy them into the air-gapped environment.

AIR-GAPPED WORKFLOW — How Providers Get In
  INTERNET-CONNECTED MACHINE              AIR-GAPPED ENVIRONMENT
  ┌──────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. terraform init        │           │                                │
  │    (downloads providers) │           │                                │
  │         │                │           │                                │
  │         ▼                │           │                                │
  │ 2. terraform providers   │           │                                │
  │    mirror ./providers/   │────USB───▶│ /opt/terraform-providers/      │
  │    (exports all .zip)    │   drive   │                                │
  │                          │           │         │                      │
  │                          │           │         ▼                      │
  │                          │           │ terraform init                 │
  │                          │           │ (reads from mirror)            │
  └──────────────────────────┘           └────────────────────────────────┘

  💡 Also copy .terraform.lock.hcl — it pins exact versions + checksums
  💡 Without the lock file, providers in the mirror might still fail
.terraformrc — Air-Gapped Config (NO internet)
provider_installation {
  filesystem_mirror {
    path    = "/opt/terraform-providers"
    include = ["registry.terraform.io/*/*"]
  }
  # NO "direct" block = NEVER try to reach the internet
}

# On the internet-connected machine (prep step):
# $ terraform providers mirror /tmp/providers/
# $ rsync -av /tmp/providers/ airgap-server:/opt/terraform-providers/
🧠 Air-Gapped Checklist

1. Mirror providers on internet machine. 2. Copy mirror + .terraform.lock.hcl to air-gapped env. 3. Configure .terraformrc with filesystem mirror. 4. Run terraform init — it uses cached providers. 5. For module sources, use local paths (source = "./modules/vpc") or a private Git server accessible from the air-gapped network.

17.10 Upgrade Strategies — Safely Moving Between Terraform Versions

Terraform major versions can contain breaking changes — state file format changes, provider API changes, and deprecated syntax. A disciplined upgrade process prevents outages.

StepActionDetails
1. ReadCheck CHANGELOGReview breaking changes between your version and target version
2. PinLock current versionEnsure required_version and .terraform.lock.hcl are committed
3. TestPlan with NEW versionRun terraform plan in non-prod env — look for deprecation warnings
4. UpgradeInit with upgradeterraform init -upgrade — gets new providers matching new TF
5. PlanVerify no-opPlan should show "No changes" — if it shows changes, investigate!
6. ApplyApply to non-prodOnly if plan is clean. Deploy to dev first.
7. VerifyCheck resourcesManually verify in cloud console — did anything break?
8. PromoteRepeat for staging → prodSame process, one environment at a time
9. RollbackIf issues: revertPin old version → terraform init → re-apply. Always backup state first!
⚠️ State File Warning

Once the state file is processed by a NEWER version of Terraform, older versions CANNOT read it. Always backup your state file before upgrading — copy it to S3/GCS with a versioned bucket or use terraform state pull > backup.tfstate.

📝 Chapter 17 — Terraform Associate Practice Questions

Q1
What's the key difference between monorepo and polyrepo for Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

Monorepo = all Terraform in one repo (easy sharing, slower clones). Polyrepo = separate repos per component (independent versioning, more coordination).

Q2
Which AWS services provide state storage and locking?
Show Answer
Answer

S3 stores the state file. DynamoDB provides the lock. Both are required for production-grade remote state.

Q3
How do you handle large state files that cause slow plans?
Show Answer
Answer

Split state by service (networking, compute, database each own their state). Use terraform state mv/rm to migrate. Increase parallelism with -parallelism=N.

Q4
What is dependency inversion in Terraform and why use it?
Show Answer
Answer

Using data sources instead of terraform_remote_state for cross-stack references. Teams stay decoupled — app team doesn't need access to networking team's state file.

Q5
How do you manage Terraform across multiple AWS accounts?
Show Answer
Answer

Separate directories per account (dev/staging/prod) with provider assume_role to switch accounts. Each directory has its own state backend for full isolation.

Q6
What is the safest way to store secrets used by Terraform?
Show Answer
Answer

HashiCorp Vault (dynamic secrets) or cloud secret managers (AWS SM, Azure KV). NEVER hardcode in .tf files. NEVER commit .tfvars with secrets.

Q7
How do you run Terraform in air-gapped environments?
Show Answer
Answer

Set up a local provider mirror — pre-downloaded provider binaries. Configure provider_installation in .terraformrc to point to the mirror.

Q8
What is the safe strategy for upgrading Terraform versions?
Show Answer
Answer

1. Read upgrade guide. 2. terraform plan with new version (no apply). 3. Fix deprecation warnings. 4. Apply with new version. 5. Update required_version. Test in dev first.

Q9
What is partial backend configuration?
Show Answer
Answer

Passing backend config at terraform init time via -backend-config flags instead of hardcoding. Essential for CI/CD where backend varies per environment.

Q10
Why cache Terraform providers in CI/CD pipelines?
Show Answer
Answer

Downloading providers fresh every CI run is slow and wastes bandwidth. A provider mirror/cache speeds up terraform init from minutes to seconds.

📎 Appendix A: HCL Syntax Cheat Sheet Reference

Appendix A: Every HCL Syntax Form, Function, and Pattern

This appendix consolidates every HCL syntax pattern you need to know for the Terraform Associate exam and daily use. From basic blocks to advanced for expressions, dynamic blocks, and string templates — this is your one-stop syntax reference.

📖 Reference ⏱️ ~30 min 8 Topics

A.1 Blocks, Arguments & Expressions — Every Syntax Form

Syntax ElementPatternExample
Block type "label" "name" { ... } resource "aws_instance" "web" { ... }
Argument key = value ami = "ami-0c55b159cbfafe1f0"
Expression Anything that produces a value var.instance_count + 1
Attribute Access TYPE.NAME.ATTR aws_instance.web.public_ip
String Template "prefix-${var.name}" "anihpj-${var.env}-web"
Heredoc <<EOF ... EOF Multi-line strings for user_data

A.2 Common Functions Reference — 50+ Functions by Category

CategoryFunctionsExample
Stringformat(), join(), split(), replace(), trim(), upper(), lower()join("-", ["dev","web","01"])
Numericmin(), max(), ceil(), floor(), abs()max(1, var.min_size, 3)
Collectionlength(), element(), lookup(), contains(), flatten(), merge(), concat()lookup(var.tags, "env", "dev")
Type Conversiontostring(), tonumber(), tolist(), toset(), tomap()tostring(var.port)

A.3 Operators — Arithmetic, Comparison, Logical

🔢 Arithmetic

+, -, *, /, %
Example: var.count * 2

⚖️ Comparison

==, !=, <, >, <=, >=
Example: var.env == "prod"

🧠 Logical

&&, ||, !
Example: var.enabled && var.env != "dev"

Conditional Expression (Ternary)
# Syntax: condition ? true_value : false_value
instance_type = var.env == "prod" ? "t3.large" : "t3.micro"
count         = var.create_public_ip ? 1 : 0

# Null coalesce (use try() for defaults):
ami = var.custom_ami != null ? var.custom_ami : data.aws_ami.ubuntu.id

A.4 For Expression Patterns — [for] with if, Grouping, and Mapping

PatternSyntaxResult Type
List → List [for s in var.list : upper(s)] List
List → List (filtered) [for s in var.list : upper(s) if s != ""] List
Map → List [for k, v in var.map : "${k}=${v}"] List
List → Map {for s in var.list : s => upper(s)} Map
Grouping {for s in var.list : s.env => s...} Map of Lists
For Expression Examples — Common Patterns
# Transform list of instance names to uppercase
upper_names = [for name in var.instance_names : upper(name)]

# Filter + transform: only enabled instances
enabled_ids = [for k, v in var.instances : v.id if v.enabled == true]

# Build a map from a list of objects (key = name, value = id)
instance_map = {for inst in aws_instance.web : inst.tags.Name => inst.id}

# Group by tag value (group-by pattern)
by_env = {for name, inst in aws_instance.web : inst.tags.Env => name...}

A.5 Dynamic Block Patterns — Nested Block Generation

Dynamic blocks generate nested blocks from a collection — essential when you don't know how many blocks you need at write time.

Dynamic Block — Security Group Ingress Rules
resource "aws_security_group" "anihpj" {
  name = "anihpj-sg"

  dynamic "ingress" {
    for_each = var.ingress_rules    # ← Map of rules
    content {
      from_port   = ingress.value.from_port
      to_port     = ingress.value.to_port
      protocol    = ingress.value.protocol
      cidr_blocks = ingress.value.cidr_blocks
    }
  }
}

# var.ingress_rules:
# {
#   "http"  = { from_port = 80,  to_port = 80,  protocol = "tcp", cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"] }
#   "https" = { from_port = 443, to_port = 443, protocol = "tcp", cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"] }
#   "ssh"   = { from_port = 22,  to_port = 22,  protocol = "tcp", cidr_blocks = ["10.0.0.0/8"] }
# }
🧠 Dynamic Block Iterator

The iterator variable defaults to the block name (ingress → ingress.value). You can rename it with iterator = "rule" — then use rule.value.from_port. This is cleaner with nested dynamics.

A.6 String Templates — %{if}, %{for}, ${}, and Heredoc Syntax

STRING TEMPLATE DIRECTIVES
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  %{ if CONDITION } ... %{ else } ... %{ endif }                     ║
  ║  %{ for ITEM in COLLECTION } ... %{ endfor }                        ║
  ║  ${ EXPRESSION }   — Interpolation                                  ║
  ║  %%{ ... }         — Literal %{ (escaped)                           ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
Template Examples
# Heredoc with template directives (user_data example):
user_data = <<-EOF
  #!/bin/bash
  echo "Deploying ${var.app_name} to ${var.env}"
  %{ if var.env == "prod" }
  echo "Running production checks..."
  %{ else }
  echo "Running dev checks..."
  %{ endif }
  %{ for host in var.allowed_hosts }
  echo "  Allowed: ${host}"
  %{ endfor }
  EOF

# templatefile() function (separate .tpl file):
user_data = templatefile("${path.module}/init.tpl", {
  app_name  = var.app_name
  env       = var.env
  hosts     = var.allowed_hosts
})

A.7 Type Constraints — All Available Type Keywords

TypeSyntaxExample Value
stringQuoted text"hello"
numberInteger or float42, 3.14
booltrue / falsetrue
list(string)[...]["a","b","c"]
map(string){key = "value"}{env = "dev"}
set(string)Unique, unordered["a","b"] (unique)
tuple([string,number])Fixed-type list["a", 1]
object({...})Structured object{name="web", port=80}
anyAny type (use sparingly)Anything
optional()Optional attributeoptional(string, "default")

A.8 Meta-Arguments Quick Reference

🔢 count

Creates N copies of a resource. Use count.index (0-based). Index changes cause replacement.

count = 3
📦 for_each

Creates one resource per map/set element. Stable keys — adding/removing items doesn't affect others.

for_each = var.instances
🔗 depends_on

Explicit dependency — use when Terraform can't infer the order (e.g., null_resource, IAM policy).

depends_on = [aws_iam_role.ecs]
🛡️ lifecycle

3 sub-options:
• create_before_destroy = true — New resource FIRST, then delete old (zero-downtime).
• prevent_destroy = true — Block accidental deletion (terraform destroy fails).
• ignore_changes = ["tags"] — Ignore external changes to listed attributes.

lifecycle { prevent_destroy = true }
🔀 provider

Overrides the default provider for this resource. Used for multi-region/account setups.

provider = aws.us_west_2

📎 Appendix B: Terraform CLI Command Reference Reference

Appendix B: Every Terraform CLI Command — Categorized & Explained

A complete reference of every terraform subcommand organized by category. Each command includes its purpose, common flags, and a real example from the anihpj/jobpost workflow.

📖 Reference ⏱️ ~20 min 9 Categories

B.1 Core Commands — init, plan, apply, destroy

CommandPurposeKey Flags
terraform init Initialize directory — downloads providers, sets up backend -upgrade, -backend-config=FILE, -reconfigure, -migrate-state
terraform plan Preview changes before applying (READ-ONLY) -out=FILE, -var="k=v", -var-file=F, -target=RES, -refresh=false
terraform apply Apply changes to reach desired state -auto-approve, PLAN_FILE, -parallelism=N, -target=RES
terraform destroy Delete ALL resources managed by this config -auto-approve, -target=RES
Core Workflow — anihpj Example
# Standard daily workflow:
terraform init -upgrade                          # Update providers
terraform fmt -recursive                         # Format all .tf files
terraform validate                               # Check syntax
terraform plan -out=anihpj.tfplan               # Save plan
terraform apply anihpj.tfplan                    # Apply saved plan

# Destroy dev environment (CAREFUL!):
terraform destroy -target=module.anihpj_dev

B.2 Validation & Formatting — validate, fmt, console

CommandPurposeKey Flags
terraform validateCheck HCL syntax and resource references-json
terraform fmtFormat .tf files to canonical style-recursive, -diff, -check
terraform consoleInteractive REPL — test expressions-var="k=v"

B.3 State Commands — list, mv, rm, pull, push, show, refresh

CommandPurposeDanger Level
terraform state listList all resources in state🟢 Safe
terraform state show RESShow attributes of one resource🟢 Safe
terraform state pullDownload remote state as JSON🟢 Safe
terraform state mv SRC DSTMove resource to new address (refactor)🟡 Careful
terraform state rm RESRemove resource from state (stop managing)🔴 Dangerous
terraform state pushUpload local state to remote (override!)🔴 Very Dangerous
terraform refreshSync state with real infrastructure🟡 Careful

B.4 Import Commands — Bringing Existing Resources Under Management

CommandPurposeUsage
terraform import ADDR IDImport existing resource into stateterraform import aws_instance.web i-0c55b159

Import workflow: 1. Write resource block in .tf. 2. Run terraform import TYPE.NAME RESOURCE_ID. 3. Run terraform plan to verify. 4. Adjust .tf to match reality. Generated config with terraform plan -generate-config-out=gen.tf (TF 1.5+).

B.5 Taint Commands — Force Resource Recreation

CommandPurpose
terraform taint ADDRMark resource for destruction & recreation on next apply
terraform untaint ADDRRemove taint mark — resource will NOT be recreated

⚠️ taint is deprecated in Terraform 1.5+. Use terraform apply -replace=ADDR instead — it's safer because it doesn't modify state until apply succeeds.

B.6 Workspace Commands — Multi-Environment State Management

CommandPurpose
terraform workspace new NAMECreate and switch to new workspace
terraform workspace select NAMESwitch to existing workspace
terraform workspace listShow all workspaces (* = current)
terraform workspace showDisplay current workspace name
terraform workspace delete NAMEDelete workspace (must be empty)

B.7 Output & Graph Commands

B.9 Debug & Utility Commands

CommandPurpose
terraform versionShow Terraform + provider versions

Debugging Tips

📎 Appendix C: State File Anatomy — Deep Dive Reference

Appendix C: Understanding the Terraform State File Inside and Out

The state file is the single source of truth for your infrastructure. Understanding its structure, how locking works, and how to recover from corruption is essential for production operations.

📖 Reference ⏱️ ~25 min 7 Topics

C.1 terraform.tfstate — Full JSON Structure Annotated

STATE FILE STRUCTURE — Annotated JSON
  {
    "version": 4,              ← State file format version
    "terraform_version": "1.7.0", ← Which Terraform wrote this
    "serial": 42,              ← Monotonic counter (increments each apply)
    "lineage": "abcd-1234-...", ← Unique ID for this state (created once)
    "outputs": {               ← All output values (cached)
      "db_endpoint": {
        "value": "anihpj.c...amazonaws.com",
        "type": "string",
        "sensitive": false
      }
    },
    "resources": [             ← THE MAIN ARRAY — all managed resources
      {
        "module": "module.rds",  ← Module path (empty = root)
        "mode": "managed",       ← "managed" | "data" (data sources)
        "type": "aws_db_instance",
        "name": "anihpj_pg",
        "provider": "provider[\"registry.../aws\"]",
        "instances": [{          ← Each resource can have multiple instances
          "schema_version": 1,
          "attributes": {        ← THE ACTUAL RESOURCE STATE
            "id": "anihpj-db",
            "endpoint": "anihpj.c...amazonaws.com",
            "engine": "postgres"
          },
          "dependencies": [...]  ← What this depends on
        }]
      }
    ]
  }

C.2 terraform.tfstate.backup — Recovery After Corruption

Every terraform apply creates a backup of the PREVIOUS state as terraform.tfstate.backup. If the current state gets corrupted, you can restore:

State Recovery Procedure
# 1. Verify backup looks valid:
cat terraform.tfstate.backup | jq '.serial'

# 2. Replace corrupted state with backup:
cp terraform.tfstate.backup terraform.tfstate

# 3. Verify state is clean:
terraform plan  # Should show "No changes"

# For REMOTE state (S3):
terraform state pull > backup.json       # Save current
terraform state push backup.json         # Restore if corrupted

C.3 State Locking — .terraform.tfstate.lock.info & DynamoDB

STATE LOCKING — Preventing Concurrent Modifications
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  LOCAL LOCK: .terraform.tfstate.lock.info (PID + timestamp)         ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  S3 + DYNAMODB:                                                     ║
  ║  Table "terraform-lock"                                             ║
  ║  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ ║
  ║  │ LockID (PK)    │ Info           │ TTL                          │ ║
  ║  │ anihpj.tfstate │ {"ID":"..."}   │ 1200 sec (20 min timeout)    │ ║
  ║  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ ║
  ║                                                                      ║
  ║  💡 DynamoDB uses "strongly consistent reads" — no stale locks      ║
  ║  💡 If lock is stuck: terraform force-unlock LOCK_ID                ║
  ║  ⚠️  Force-unlock ONLY when SURE no apply is in progress!           ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

C.4 Remote State Backends Comparison

BackendLockingEncryptionBest For
S3 + DynamoDB✅ DynamoDB✅ SSE-KMSAWS-native shops
Terraform Cloud✅ Built-in✅ At-restTeams, CI/CD
GCS❌ External✅ DefaultGCP-native shops
Azure Blob✅ Lease✅ DefaultAzure-native shops
Consul✅ Sessions❌ None by defaultOn-prem / private DC
HTTP❌ App-provided❌ App-providedCustom/GitLab managed

C.5 Backend Configuration — S3 + DynamoDB Production Setup

Production Backend — main.tf
terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "anihpj-tfstate-prod"
    key            = "prod/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "us-east-1"
    dynamodb_table = "terraform-lock-prod"
    encrypt        = true
    kms_key_id     = "alias/tfstate-key"
  }
}

C.6 State Encryption — At-Rest & In-Transit

🔒 SSE-S3

AES-256 server-side encryption. Enabled with encrypt = true in backend. Basic protection.

🔐 SSE-KMS

Customer-managed keys. Audit trail via CloudTrail. Required for compliance (HIPAA, PCI-DSS).

⚠️ Plaintext Warning

State stores ALL attributes in JSON. Secrets like password, private_key are visible. Always encrypt.

C.7 State Migration — Local → Remote

MIGRATION WORKFLOW
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. Add backend block to terraform {} in main.tf                     │
  │  2. terraform init -migrate-state                                    │
  │     → Prompts: "Do you want to copy state to new backend?"           │
  │     → Type "yes"                                                     │
  │  3. terraform plan → Should show "No changes"                        │
  │  4. Delete local terraform.tfstate (now stored remotely)             │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 -migrate-state auto-copies local→remote (TF 1.1+)               │
  │  💡 -reconfigure ignores existing backend, re-initializes            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📎 Appendix D: Common Terraform Patterns & Recipes Patterns

Appendix D: Battle-Tested Patterns for Everyday Terraform

Production-ready patterns for count, for_each, dynamic blocks, conditionals, provisioners, and template generation. Each pattern includes a real example from the anihpj/jobpost stack.

📖 Patterns ⏱️ ~30 min 8 Patterns

D.1 count — Creating Multiple Similar Resources

count Pattern — N Identical EC2 Instances
variable "instance_count" { default = 3 }
variable "azs" { default = ["us-east-1a", "us-east-1b", "us-east-1c"] }

resource "aws_instance" "anihpj" {
  count                  = var.instance_count
  ami                    = data.aws_ami.ubuntu.id
  instance_type          = "t3.medium"
  availability_zone      = var.azs[count.index]  # ← Round-robin AZs
  subnet_id              = var.subnet_ids[count.index]

  tags = {
    Name = "anihpj-web-${count.index + 1}"      # ← 1-indexed names
    Env  = var.env
  }
}

# References: aws_instance.anihpj[0], aws_instance.anihpj[1], ...

D.2 for_each — Creating Resources from Maps & Sets

Key difference from count: for_each uses stable keys — removing "staging" doesn't affect "dev" and "prod". count indices shift, causing recreate.

for_each Pattern — Per-Environment Subnets
variable "env_configs" {
  type = map(object({
    cidr   = string
    public = bool
  }))
  default = {
    "dev"  = { cidr = "10.0.0.0/24",  public = true  }
    "stg"  = { cidr = "10.0.1.0/24",  public = false }
    "prd"  = { cidr = "10.0.2.0/24",  public = false }
  }
}

resource "aws_subnet" "anihpj" {
  for_each          = var.env_configs
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block        = each.value.cidr
  map_public_ip     = each.value.public
  availability_zone = "${var.region}${each.key == "dev" ? "a" : "b"}"

  tags = { Name = "anihpj-${each.key}-subnet" }
}

# References: aws_subnet.anihpj["dev"], aws_subnet.anihpj["prd"]

D.3 Conditional Resource Creation — Toggling with count

Conditional Pattern — Optional NAT Gateway
variable "create_nat" { default = true }

# Create only if var.create_nat == true:
resource "aws_nat_gateway" "anihpj" {
  count         = var.create_nat ? 1 : 0   # ← 1 or 0
  allocation_id = aws_eip.nat[0].id
  subnet_id     = aws_subnet.public.id
}

# Reference (must handle both cases):
nat_id = var.create_nat ? aws_nat_gateway.anihpj[0].id : null

# For for_each (conditional):
resource "aws_instance" "optional" {
  for_each = var.enable_monitoring ? var.monitoring_instances : {}
  ami      = each.value.ami
  ...
}

D.4 Dynamic Blocks — Every Pattern with Real Examples

Use CaseDynamic BlockData Source
Security Group Rulesdynamic "ingress" + dynamic "egress"for_each = var.rules
IAM Policy Statementsdynamic "statement"for_each = var.policy_statements
S3 Lifecycle Rulesdynamic "rule" inside lifecycle_rulefor_each = var.lifecycle_rules
ALB Listener Rulesdynamic "action" / dynamic "condition"for_each = var.routing_rules

D.5 null_resource with local-exec — Running Scripts

null_resource — Post-Deployment Setup
resource "null_resource" "db_migrate" {
  triggers = {
    # Re-run when DB endpoint or app version changes:
    db_endpoint = aws_db_instance.anihpj.endpoint
    app_version = var.app_version
  }

  provisioner "local-exec" {
    command = <<-EOF
      python manage.py migrate
      echo "Migration complete for ${self.triggers.db_endpoint}"
    EOF

    working_dir = "${path.module}/../anihpj"
    environment = {
      DATABASE_URL = "postgres://user:pass@${aws_db_instance.anihpj.endpoint}/anihpj"
    }
  }

  depends_on = [aws_db_instance.anihpj]
}

D.6 templatefile Function — Dynamic Configuration Files

templatefile — Nginx Config Generation
# main.tf:
resource "aws_instance" "web" {
  user_data = templatefile("${path.module}/templates/user_data.tpl", {
    app_name    = "jobpost"
    env         = var.env
    db_endpoint = aws_db_instance.anihpj.endpoint
    redis_host  = aws_elasticache_cluster.anihpj.cache_nodes[0].address
  })
}

# templates/user_data.tpl:
#!/bin/bash
echo "Deploying ${app_name} to ${env}"
cat > /etc/nginx/sites-available/${app_name} <<NGINX
server {
    listen 80;
    server_name ${app_name}.${env}.example.com;
    location / {
        proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:8000;
    }
}
NGINX

D.7 try() and can() — Graceful Error Handling

Error-Handling Patterns
# try() — returns first non-error value:
tags = try(var.env_config[var.env].tags, var.default_tags, {})

# can() — boolean: "can this expression be evaluated?"
count = can(var.optional_setting.enabled) ? 1 : 0

# Safe nested lookup:
region = try(var.regions[var.env], "us-east-1")

# Real-world: safe optional map access:
db_name = try(local.db_config[var.env].name, "anihpj_default")

D.8 count vs for_each — Decision Tree

WHEN TO USE count vs for_each
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Use COUNT when:                     Use FOR_EACH when:               │
  │  ✅ Simple "create N copies"         ✅ Create from a map/set         │
  │  ✅ Position matters (count.index)   ✅ Stable identity needed         │
  │  ✅ Resources are truly identical    ✅ Adding/removing items safely   │
  │                                        without affecting others       │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ❌ count FAILS when:                ❌ for_each FAILS when:           │
  │  Removing index[1] shifts index[2]   Keys aren't known until apply    │
  │  → index[2] gets DESTROYED!          (use -target or depends_on)      │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 EXAM TIP: for_each = preferred for most cases.                   │
  │     count can cause "unexpected destroy" when list changes.          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

D.9 flatten + for — Working with Nested Data Structures

When you have nested lists (e.g., multiple VPCs each with multiple subnets), flatten() collapses them into a single list you can iterate with for_each.

flatten Pattern — Multiple VPCs → All Subnets Flat
locals {
  vpcs = [
    { name = "prod",  cidr = "10.0.0.0/16", subnets = ["10.0.1.0/24", "10.0.2.0/24"] },
    { name = "staging", cidr = "10.1.0.0/16", subnets = ["10.1.1.0/24"] },
  ]

  # flatten — [(vpc, subnet_cidr), ...] → single-level list
  flat_subnets = flatten([
    for vpc in local.vpcs : [
      for cidr in vpc.subnets : {
        vpc_name = vpc.name
        vpc_cidr = vpc.cidr
        subnet_cidr = cidr
      }
    ]
  ])
}

resource "aws_subnet" "all" {
  for_each = { for s in local.flat_subnets : "${s.vpc_name}-${s.subnet_cidr}" => s }
  vpc_id   = aws_vpc.main[each.value.vpc_name].id
  cidr_block = each.value.subnet_cidr
}

# Result: prod-10.0.1.0/24, prod-10.0.2.0/24, staging-10.1.1.0/24

📎 Appendix E: Docker + Kubernetes + AWS — anihpj/jobpost Complete Stack Production

Appendix E: The Full anihpj/jobpost Stack — From Local Docker to EKS in Production

This appendix ties everything together: the complete anihpj (Django project) / jobpost (Django app) stack from local development with Docker Compose, through containerization with a multi-stage Dockerfile, to production deployment on AWS EKS with Terraform. Every file, every command, every step.

🐳 Docker ☸️ Kubernetes ☁️ AWS ⏱️ ~40 min

E.1 Dockerfile — Multi-Stage Build for jobpost Django Application

Dockerfile — anihpj/jobpost Multi-Stage Build
# ──── STAGE 1: BUILD ────────────────────────────────────────────
FROM python:3.11-slim AS builder
WORKDIR /app
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y \
    gcc libpq-dev --no-install-recommends && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --user --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# ──── STAGE 2: RUNTIME ───────────────────────────────────────────
FROM python:3.11-slim AS runtime
WORKDIR /app
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y \
    libpq5 curl --no-install-recommends && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
COPY --from=builder /root/.local /root/.local
ENV PATH=/root/.local/bin:$PATH
COPY . .
RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
EXPOSE 8000
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=5s CMD curl -f http://localhost:8000/health/ || exit 1
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "3"]
# ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

docker-compose.yml — Django + PostgreSQL + Redis + Nginx
version: "3.9"
services:

    image: postgres:15-alpine
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: anihpj
      POSTGRES_USER: anihpj_user
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: dev_password
    volumes: [pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data]
    ports: ["5432:5432"]

  redis:
    image: redis:7-alpine
    ports: ["6379:6379"]

  web:
    build: .
    command: python manage.py runserver 0.0.0.0:8000
    volumes: [.:/app]
    ports: ["8000:8000"]
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://anihpj_user:dev_password@db:5432/anihpj
      REDIS_URL: redis://redis:6379/0
    depends_on: [db, redis]

  nginx:
    image: nginx:alpine
    volumes: [./nginx.conf:/etc/nginx/nginx.conf:ro]
    ports: ["80:80"]
    depends_on: [web]

volumes:
  pgdata:

E.3 Terraform Docker Provider — Local Infrastructure as Code

docker.tf — Local Dev Infrastructure
terraform {
  required_providers {
    docker = { source = "kreuzwerker/docker", version = "~> 3.0" }
  }
}

# ──── NETWORK ────────────────────────────────────────
resource "docker_network" "anihpj" {
  name = "anihpj-network"
}

# ──── DATABASE ────────────────────────────────────────
resource "docker_container" "postgres" {
  name  = "anihpj-db"
  image = docker_image.postgres.image_id
  networks_advanced { name = docker_network.anihpj.name }
  env = [
    "POSTGRES_DB=anihpj",
    "POSTGRES_USER=anihpj_user",
    "POSTGRES_PASSWORD=${var.db_password}"
  ]
  ports { internal = 5432; external = 5432 }
}

resource "docker_image" "postgres" {
  name = "postgres:15-alpine"
}

E.4 Terraform Kubernetes Provider — jobpost Deployment + Service + Ingress

kubernetes.tf — jobpost on K8s
resource "kubernetes_deployment" "jobpost" {
  metadata {
    name      = "jobpost"
    namespace = kubernetes_namespace.anihpj.metadata[0].name
    labels    = { app = "jobpost" }
  }
  spec {
    replicas = var.env == "prod" ? 3 : 1
    selector { match_labels = { app = "jobpost" } }
    template {
      metadata { labels = { app = "jobpost" } }
      spec {
        container {
          name  = "jobpost"
          image = "${aws_ecr_repository.anihpj.repository_url}:${var.app_version}"
          port  { container_port = 8000 }
          env {
            name  = "DATABASE_URL"
            value = "postgres://admin:${var.db_password}@${aws_db_instance.anihpj.endpoint}/anihpj"
          }
          resources {
            requests = { cpu = "250m"; memory = "512Mi" }
            limits   = { cpu = "500m"; memory = "1Gi" }
          }
          liveness_probe {
            http_get { path = "/health/"; port = 8000 }
            initial_delay_seconds = 30; period_seconds = 10
          }
        }
      }
    }
  }
}

resource "kubernetes_service" "jobpost" {
  metadata { name = "jobpost-svc" }
  spec {
    selector = { app = "jobpost" }
    port { port = 80; target_port = 8000 }
    type = "ClusterIP"
  }
}

E.6 ECR Repository + Image Push + K8s Rolling Update

CI/CD PIPELINE — Build → Push → Deploy
  DEVELOPER                  CI/CD (GitHub Actions)              AWS
  ─────────                  ────────────────────────            ───
  git push ────────────────▶ Build Docker image
                                   │
                                   ▼
                            docker build -t jobpost:v1.2.3 .
                                   │
                                   ▼
                            docker tag → ECR repo
                                   │
                                   ▼
                            docker push to ECR ───────────────▶ ECR
                                   │
                                   ▼
                            terraform apply -var="app_version=v1.2.3"
                                   │
                                   ▼
                            kubectl set image deployment/jobpost
                            jobpost=ECR_URL:v1.2.3 ──────────▶ EKS
                                   │
                                   ▼
                            kubectl rollout status
                            deployment/jobpost

  💡 Terraform manages the INFRASTRUCTURE (EKS, RDS, S3)
  💡 CI/CD manages the APPLICATION (Docker build, push, K8s deploy)
  💡 Kubernetes rolling update = zero-downtime deploy (1 pod at a time)

E.7 Local Development → ECR → EKS — Complete Workflow

StepCommandWhere
1. Local Devdocker-compose upLaptop
2. Test Locallypython manage.py testDocker container
3. Build Imagedocker build -t jobpost:v1.2.3 .CI/CD runner
4. Push to ECRdocker push ACCOUNT.dkr.ecr.../anihpj:v1.2.3CI/CD → AWS
5. Provision Infraterraform applyTerraform Cloud
6. Deploy Appkubectl set image deployment/jobpost ...EKS
7. Verifycurl https://anihpj.example.com/health/Browser/CLI
🧠 Terraform vs Kubernetes — Who Owns What?

Terraform owns the infrastructure: EKS cluster, RDS instance, S3 bucket, Route 53 records, ECR repository, IAM roles. Kubernetes owns the application: Deployments, Services, Ingress, ConfigMaps, Secrets. The Terraform Kubernetes provider creates the K8s resources, but ongoing app deploys happen through your CI/CD pipeline — not Terraform.

📎 Appendix F: Terraform Associate — Full Practice Exam (60 Questions) Exam

Appendix F: Full 60-Question Practice Exam — Simulate the Real Thing

This is a full-length practice exam mirroring the real HashiCorp Certified: Terraform Associate (004) exam. 60 questions across all 6 domains, with detailed answer key, explanations, domain score card, and exam day strategy. Time yourself: 60 minutes. Passing: 70% (42/60).

📝 60 Questions ⏱️ 60 Minutes 🎯 70% to Pass 6 Domains

F.1 Exam Structure & Instructions

DomainWeightQuestionsChapters
1. IaC Concepts~20%12 QsCh 1-2
2. Terraform Basics & Workflow~25%15 QsCh 3-5
3. Providers & Resources~20%12 QsCh 6-8

5. Modules & Code Reuse~10%6 QsCh 12-13
